## A Reading Comprehension Interface for Students with Learning Disorders

## [Martina Galletti](https://orcid.org/0000-0002-3477-4392)

## ABSTRACT

A strong reading comprehension ability is not only one of the cornerstones of a successful education but also a crucial skill for various aspects of our daily lives, both in the workplace and personal life. Nonetheless, text comprehension is a complex cognitive task that can prove challenging and frustrating for many children with learning disabilities. In this paper, we introduce an Natural Language Processing (NLP)-powered reading comprehension interface to explore the potential of modern NLP techniques in hybrid therapeutic practices, aiming to make the rehabilitation of text comprehension difficulties more accessible, automatic, multi-modal, and

truly inclusive for students with special educational needs and disabilities in presence or at a distance. Our contributions are twofold. Firstly, drawing inspiration from psycho-linguistic theories, we have designed, together with speech and language therapists, an NLP-powered reading comprehension interface as a proof of concept for the treatment of reading comprehension. Secondly, we have deployed and evaluated our proof of concept in a general public setting and initiated the evaluation of our preliminary results in a clinical setting.

## 1 BACKGROUND

Despite the absence of clear diagnostic categories, reading comprehension is a fundamental skill in our age. It not only facilitates the acquisition of knowledge and cultural enrichment but also unlocks one's full potential in education and professional life. This is precisely why we believe there should be a concerted effort to develop systems specifically designed to rehabilitate in presence or at a distance reading comprehension skills. This paper presents a reading comprehension interface that utilizes advanced Natural Language Processing techniques. The goal is to investigate how these modern technologies can enhance hybrid therapeutic approaches for rehabilitating text comprehension challenges among students with special educational needs and disabilities. In the literature, we are aware of a few systems available in Italian for the integrated tele-rehabilitation of specific learning disorders and other special educational needs, including RIDInet [2]. RIDInet includes a series of activities, referred to as "applications," that address text comprehension skills, specifically working on semantic and syntactic inferential processes related to text comprehension. However, in the English language, two primary systems are available: 3D Readers [1] and CACSR [10]. 3D Readers allow users to read texts and choose between four options, adopting either a verbal strategy (creating a question) or a visual strategy (interactively moving images on the screen) to enhance text comprehension. The generated questions and images are immediately evaluated, providing learners with feedback to improve subsequent performance [1]. On the other hand, CACSR provides personalized instruction to enhance comprehension strategies [10]. It employs visual images, graphic organizers, mnemonics, self-questioning, and summarizing techniques [18], offering real-time feedback for continuous assessment.

Text comprehension is a complex process that requires the construction of coherent and well-formed semantic representations [7, 11, 12] that integrates syntactic, semantic and narrative aspects of language [13]. This includes challenges in grasping the sequence, relationships, implicit information (inferences), and deeper meaning of the text content' [3]. The whole process is then controlled by one's meta-cognitive skills [6]. Despite this complexity, the existence of a diagnostic category for text comprehension disorder is still a matter of debate. In the literature, children with text comprehension problems have been referred to in various ways: bad readers [14], readers with learning problems [19], and finally poor readers [6]. Text comprehension difficulty is associated with different clinical profiles related to language difficulties or disorders, dyslexia, and cognitive delays.

Although these projects contribute to improved reading comprehension, it is important to note that 3D Readers, CACSR were primarily developed for educational contexts rather than therapeutic ones. Moreover, they are mostly used in schools and lack a specific focus on distinct clinical categories. Moreover, even if RIDInet was developed for clinical contexts, it does not incorporate Natural Language Processing (NLP) techniques. To the best of our knowledge, there is only one system that employs NLP algorithms: Open Book [4]. However, Open Book primarily serves as a reading tool for children with Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) [15]. Its key features include text simplification through NLP techniques and rich customisation functions, enabling users to quickly adapt the document presentation (font, font size, line spacing, colours) to their preferences. It also provides assistive tools such as dictionaries and images. However, Open Books was designed for a specific clinical population, namely children with ASD and focusing more on improving their decoding skills than reading comprehension per se.

For all these reasons, we believe that exploring the feasibility and usability of an NLP-powered interface, designed to aid in the rehabilitation of reading comprehension for children with special educational needs and disabilities, could address a significant gap in existing literature.

Finally, it's important mentioning all the systems targeting telerehabilitation [5, 16, 20, 23] specifically for Dyslexia. Even if some of them include some exercises to rehabilitate the decoding of single words and sentences and engage with written content, we are not aware of systems specifically targeting reading comprehension skills.

## 2 SYSTEM DESCRIPTION

In line with Kitsch and Van Dijk's model [11], we specifically integrated functionalities to enhance the creation of a superficial representation of the language, i.e. the first level of Kitsch and Van Dijk's model [11]. This involved enabling users to develop a strong understanding of the lexical and morphosyntactic aspects of the words present in the text. In our interface, this is accomplished in what we will indicate as 'First Module' through the paper. The 'Second Module' would like to help users training, with what Kitsch and Van Dijk's call the sequence representations and hierarchical organisation of information [11]; this effort is underway despite the fact that the exercises in this direction are currently in a very preliminary form; further work will be conducted in the future. Finally, in a very preliminary form, the 'Third Module', aims to train the capacity of creating a broader mental model of language, i.e.integrating the information found in the text with the knowledge already possessed by the users [11]. This need is addressed by the Synset map, which will be further explained later.

When designing our proof of concept, we had three primary goals in mind. Firstly, our aim was to develop a system specifically tailored to children from the elementary school up to 11 years old students who have been diagnosed with reading comprehension difficulties. Secondly, we wanted to create an interface that could be used during therapeutic sessions, always accompanied by a professional when working with younger age groups. Lastly, we sought to involve potential end-users and stakeholders right from the initial design phase, this is why our proof of concept was designed together with speech and language therapists. To achieve this, we collaborated with highly skilled speech and language therapists who not only conducted the clinical evaluation but also actively participated in the development of our proof of concept. Their valuable feedback and comments on preliminary exercises and versions greatly contributed to its advancement.

In the next subsection, we will describe each module and the algorithms behind each functionality in more details 1 .

Figure 2: The different levels of [11] and the corresponding implementation



## 2.1 Superficial Representation: understanding words and sentences meaning

On the left part of the screen, for each sentence, the three main keywords are extracted and their meaning is represented in the form of PCS (Picture Communication Symbols), the most widely used form of pictographs for our use case 3 . As a first step this visualisation module, the ability to visualize what is written can act as a support for text comprehension. Moreover, it allows for the activation of bottom-up strategies, in which the perception of the external stimulus guides the process of information processing. For extracting relevant keywords, Keybert [9] was used.

The interface starts by presenting the original text through an intermediate level of granularity: the sentence. This first sub-module is visible in Figure 1. As we can see, in this sub-module, the text, appears on the right divided into sentences by the Spacy Sentencizer 2 . For each sentence, users have the possibility of listening to the text through speech synthesis, integrated by calling the Google text-to-speech API. We decided to introduce a speech synthesis module, since we wanted our proof of concept to be accessible to those who have a technical difficulty in reading, together with a reading comprehension fragility. In fact, speech synthesis is extremely useful in cases where slowness and difficulty in decoding are present, as it allows users to bypass the reading process, thus enabling them to focus on the content rather than on form of the passage.

For evaluating whether the right keywords were extracted and where not misleading, speech and language therapists manually checked whether for the sentences chosen for the users studies the correct keywords were extracted. Once the keywords were extracted from the sentences, after lemmatisation, we used the Arasaac API 4 to link them to pictograms. In a previous version of this proof of concept, we fine-tuned Clip 5 on real images, using the Unsplash dataset 6 . Even if the algorithm was performing well computationally, the images returned, even if correct, were sometimes unsuitable to be seen by a fragile public. This is why, we thought that using pictograms, instead of real images, could make the whole process safer. Unfortunately, as far as we know, big dataset of PCS are lacking so data creation or augmentation techniques would be needed for creating robust text-to-pcs algorithms.

1 A recorded demonstration of our proof of concept can be found here : https://drive. google.com/drive/folders/16QuSPxHtBvOHKEkKhcbXSLVjnWzcpA1d

3 Pictographs are images created specifically for people who are illiterate due to age or disability. They offer the advantage of allowing from a very basic level of communication - adaptable to people with low cognitive levels or in very early stages - to a very rich and advanced level, although never as complete and flexible as that which can be achieved with the use of written language.

[2 https://spacy.io/api/sentencizer](https://spacy.io/api/sentencizer)

[4 https://arasaac.org/](https://arasaac.org/)

5 li2022cwelip

Following [13], in a second submodule, the interface presents the text through a lower level of granularity: the word. This second sub-module is visible in the supplementary material, see Figure 3. In this second step, the 10 words that are ranked as the rarest in the vocabulary of the Italian language for each selected text are presented to the user. For extracting those we used WordFreq [17], which provides access to estimates of the frequency of a word used in 40 languages, among which Italian. We decided to automatically extract the rarest words present in each text because, after long discussions with speech therapists, we found that, in many cases, children with reading comprehension fragilities are afraid of admitting that they do not know a particular word. If these words are selected by the user, the definition together with a Pcs representing the meaning of these definitions and the sentence in which the word was originally placed it's displayed. The definition, taken from the Oxford Dictionary API 7 is presented in both written form and spoken form through the use of speech synthesis.

## 2.2 Propositional Representation: hierarchical organisation

By taking into account the theories of Zachou et al [22] and the hierachical organisation of Kitsch and Van Dijk [11], we opted to embark on the initial phase of implementing exercises in this particular direction. As a very preliminary step, we implemented a co-reference resolution algorithm. This algorithm enables users to practice the linking relationship between different entities and pronouns within the text. While other exercises, such as cause-andeffect relationship extraction could be considered, we chose to start by pronouns precisely because difficulties in the recognition of pronouns seems to be particularly problematic. In this module, the text is divided into sentences and certain grammatical elements are highlighted using different colors depending on their Part of Speech Tags: common names with the light blue color, proper names with orange, pronouns with yellow. This second module is visible in the supplementary material, see Figure 4. This visual distinction helps users differentiate between various elements within the sentence and suggests the function of the lexical morpheme, aiding in the comprehension of the pronoun by identifying the correct referent. For each proposition, users are presented with four alternatives and asked to select the correct one. Among the four options, one is correct (determined by the co-reference resolution algorithm), two are misleading, and the last one closely resembles the correct answer. When the user provides the correct answer, the system provides positive feedback. To implement this functionality, we used the speech tagger of Spacy 8 for the parser and Coreferee 9 .

6 https://unsplash.com/data

[8 https://spacy.io/usage/linguistic-features](https://spacy.io/usage/linguistic-features)

[7 https://developer.oxforddictionaries.com/](https://developer.oxforddictionaries.com/)

[9 https://github.com/richardpaulhudson/coreferee](https://github.com/richardpaulhudson/coreferee)

## 2.3 A fi rst step towards the Mental Model: integrating a word with further knowledge

According to Kintsch and Van Dijk [11], comprehending a text involves more than just understanding its surface-level representation. A crucial aspect is the ability to connect newly acquired information from the text with existing knowledge. To initiate the rehabilitation of this deeper level of comprehension, we have implemented what we refer to as a Synset Network. This third module is visible in the supplementary material, see Figure 5. In the Synset Network, we focused on the smallest unit of granularity in a text, which is the word, and developed a method to establish connections between the words encountered in the text and prior knowledge. Each selected word is presented as linked to its synsets. Synsets can facilitate vocabulary expansion and retrieval, activating the user's pre-existing knowledge. Understanding the various meanings associated with a term not only ensures a deeper comprehension of its intended meaning, but also serves as an initial step towards constructing a mental model of the word. To determine the synsets of a word, we utilized the Merriam-Webster thesaurus 10 .

## 3 EVALUATION

The first prototype of this proof of concept was unveiled at the Rome-European Edition Maker Faire 11 .During the three-day event, visitors had the opportunity to explore the different sections of the platform, ask questions, and provide valuable insights and feedback. Parents, students from all walks of life, clinical specialists, teachers, software engineers, and the merely curious took turns. At the end of the experience, visitors were invited to complete an anonymous usability questionnaire which evaluated different aspects of the platform, such as sentence visualization, single word level visualization, semantic network, and pronoun exercises.A total of 110 individuals completed the questionnaire. The barplot, see Figure 6, demonstrates the platform's remarkable success among users, as the average score across all sections exceeded 8. Specifically, the section focusing on pronouns received the highest score, followed by the sentence display section, word display, and the general interface presentation along with the semantic network, which received nearly equal ratings. The differences in scores among the sections were minimal, indicating that the interface was well-received as a whole. In addition to rating each section on a scale from 0 to 10, users were also encouraged to provide their comments on the platform. One commonly shared comment pertained to the potential use of the interface for second language (L2) learning 12 . Moreover, we are testing the platform in a clinical setting with a small set of children (around twenty-five) with Neuro-developmental Disorders, selected from those attending the CRC in Rome 13 , based on age, schooling and functional profiles. The aim of the clinical evaluation is to assess the effectiveness of the use of the platform in the rehabilitation of subjects with Neuro-developmental Disorders and to analyse the usability and satisfaction of both the children and the operators following them during the training.

## 4 DISCUSSION AND FUTURE CLINICAL EVALUATION

The primary aim of the clinical evaluation is to assess the effectiveness of the use of the platform in the rehabilitation in presence or at a distance of subjects with Neuro-developmental Disorders,specifically targeting impaired text comprehension and fallouts on lexical semantic components and morphosyntactic comprehension (i.e. pronouns). This evaluation also seeks to analyze the usability and satisfaction levels of both the children using the platform and the professionals guiding their training. The testing subjects consists of children in primary school with diagnoses such as Specific Learning Disorder, Mild Intellectual Disability, and other Neuro-developmental Disorders associated with text comprehension difficulties. The experimentation period, currently just started, will span three months with two therapy sessions per week. The experimentation methods are taking the form of pre-testing to collect the functional profile, followed by reinforcement during therapy sessions with speech therapists and the platform (totalling 24 sessions), a post-test and satisfaction questionnaires. All subjects entering the project already have a functioning profile measured through the WISC-IV [21].

This paper introduces a reading comprehension interface specifically designed for children with language disorders. The interface was developed in collaboration with speech and language therapists and was inspired by psycho-linguistic theories. Our research has yielded two key outcomes, with one additional outcome currently in progress. Firstly, we have successfully implemented an initial proof of concept utilizing state-of-the-art Natural Language Processing tools. Secondly, we have deployed our system and gathered feedback from the general public to evaluate the interface. Additionally, we are conducting testing in a clinical setting with an initial group of approximately thirty children diagnosed with Neuro-developmental Disorders, selected from attendees of the CRC in Rome 14 based on age, schooling, and functional profiles.

In the future, we plan to design more specific and precise exercises to rehabilitate the propositional representation studied in [11] and proceed further with the design of training exercises concerning the mental model [11]. Moreover, we will leverage the clinical results obtained from user studies to enhance the overall interface. Through this collaborative research effort, we also aim to raise awareness about language disorders, particularly highlighting the lack of formalism and computational implementations supporting reading comprehension. Moreover, we would like to use SUS, system usability scale [8] to compare the score of our interface with similary systems, given the extensive use in the literature. If the interface proves effective based on the user studies, its potential impact would be significant and wide-ranging. Firstly, success in improving text comprehension skills would take us a step closer to equitable and quality education, one of the main objectives that was added to the United Nations' 17 Sustainable Development Goals to be reached by 2030. The interface has the potential to empower individuals with special educational needs, who are often marginalized and undervalued in social and educational settings compared to their peers. As improved education correlates with better employment opportunities, the formation of informed and aware citizens, and enhanced relationships and social conditions, our interface could trigger a chain of positive effects on the society we all belong to.

[14 https://www.crc-balbuzie.it/](https://www.crc-balbuzie.it/)

## Paper Source: AI-Driven Innovation Using Multimodal and Personalized Adaptive Education for Students With Special Needs
============================================================



Received 28 March 2025, accepted 9 April 2025, date of publication 15 April 2025, date of current version 28 April 2025.

Digital Object Identifier 10.1 109/ACCESS.2025.3560863



## AI-Driven Innovation Using Multimodal and Personalized Adaptive Education for Students With Special Needs



NESREN FARHAH 1,2 , MUHAMMAD ADNAN 3 , AHMED ABDULLAH ALQARNI 1,4 , M. IRFAN UDDIN 1,3 , (Member, IEEE), AND THEYAZN H. H. ALDHYANI 5

2 Department of Health Informatics, College of Health Science, Saudi Electronic University, Riyadh 11673, Saudi Arabia

3 Institute of Computing, Kohat University of Science and Technology, Kohat 26000, Pakistan

4 Department of Computer Sciences and Information Technology, Al-Baha University, Al-Aqiq 65779, Saudi Arabia

5 Applied College (Abqaiq), King Faisal University, Al Hofuf 31982, Saudi Arabia

Corresponding authors: M. Irfan Uddin (irfanuddin@kust.edu.pk) and Nesren Farhah (n.farhah@seu.edu.sa)

The authors extend their appreciation to the King Salman Center for Disability Research for funding this work through Research Group no KSRG-2024-288.

ABSTRACT This study provides an in-depth exploration of the use of multimodal techniques in developing adaptive learning systems designed for students with special needs using various neural network models: a Baseline Neural Network, Convolutional Neural Network, Attention Model, LSTM, GRU, and Transformer models. Adaptive learning systems play a major role in the customization of the educational process in such a way as to ensure the specific cognitive, behavioural and emotional needs of students with special educational needs are catered for. The models were trained using a sizeable amount of multimodal data that recorded factors such as academic achievement, behavioral interactions, and environmental factors. From our observations, it can be noted that transformer models and attention mechanisms allowed for higher performance in predicting and fulfilling the needs of students, and were more effective than simpler neural architectures. These findings suggest that more advanced and intelligent neural networks can improve how students with different learning abilities consume content and, consequently, provide better educational answers and solutions to the market. To identify the important features affecting student performance, various Explainable AI (XAI) techniques were utilized, including SHAP (Shapley Additive Explanations) values, logistic regression-based feature importance analysis, LIME (Local Interpretable Model-Agnostic Explanations), partial dependency plots, and whose global surrogate model was a Decision Tree Regressor. These XAI instruments allowed the model's decision to be explained and visualized so that teachers could understand the main beneficiaries of the improved educational results. Such XAI methods enable transparency in how models make decisions, thus allowing educators to understand the reasons for certain recommended learning paths.

INDEX TERMS Adaptive learning paths, students with special needs, neural networks, convolutional neural networks, attention mechanisms, long short-term memory, gated recurrent unit, transformer models, explainable AI.

## I. INTRODUCTION

Artificial, Intelligence (AI) has changed in almost all fields and progressive solutions have been provided in terms of

The associate editor coordinating the review of this manuscript and approving it for publication was Wei Wei .

time, accuracy, and individuality [1]. Starting from diagnosing patients or identifying fraud in finances to market trends or predicting the spread of diseases, AI applications offer incredible computational resources to extract information that cannot be achieved manually [2]. The main driving forces of these developments are innovations in Machine Learning



(ML) and Deep Learning (DL), whereby Smart Systems learn from patterns within large datasets, and then engage in tasks such as image recognition, natural language processing, and predictive analytics with unmatched accuracy [3]. All of these technologies allow systems to learn responses to specific contexts and change knowledge as it progresses, which is critical for environments requiring flexibility similar to that of humans.

Among the AI techniques, neural networks are crucial, with deep neural networks (DNN) at the core of adaptive applications. Neural networks are formed as layers of interconnecting nodes that simulate the neurons in the human brain. This architecture enables DNNs to detect and learn from different patterns within the data, such as language, images, or behaviors [4]. In adaptive learning, neural networks can incorporate many data inputs, such as learner responses, participation rates, and learning achievements, to produce specific student content and modify the learning processes. Progress in Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) and Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) structures coupled with new architectures such as transformers has extended the possibility of adept AI in managing and interpreting multimodal data, which are crucial in educational engineering [5].

However, the idea of adaptive learning still has a long way to go; hence, it faces many challenges, especially for disabled students. Some of the constraints of traditional learning systems include the inability to address different learning capabilities, learning models, and needs, owing to a variety of learning deficiencies, thereby reducing the reachability and efficiency of learning instruments [6]. The most challenging types of learners who might still require better and more personalized adaptive systems are those with cognitive, sensory, or physical impairments, because most standard adaptive environments are typically designed to handle only one and sometimes two modalities for input and output, but ignore how such a learner would best engage in a learning environment [7]. This gap shows that there is a strong need to develop learning models that incorporate more than one response style to teach, train, and inform students about diverse needs for sensory and interaction modes.

The absence of individuality in present-day AI-based adaptive interfaces amplifies the learning gap due to poor feedback and supports students with learning disabilities [8]. For instance, most existing AI models predominantly work with the text of images that are inaccessible to students with visual or auditory disabilities. Second, existing adaptive learning algorithms do not consider customized details because they provide generalized responses when, for instance, a student with multisensory learning impairment uses the system [9]. Thus, there is a gap between AI-enabling teaching tools and specialized learning needs that need to be corrected through the proposed multimodal adaptive approach, which can help provide greater access to learning for students with learning disabilities.

These problems have spurred the development of new and practically applicable adaptive learning strategies that incorporate multiple ML and DL models. This approach combines architectures such as CNNs for images, LSTMs for temporal information, and transformers for contextual computations into one system to process and produce learning in different formats. Multimodal adaptive learning systems can handle more data from students'sensory modalities and provide individual learning pathways that can accommodate disabled students [10].



The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: Section II discusses various studies related to AI in education, ML models used in adaptive learning systems, Neural Networks used for personalized learning, CNN used for visual learning, RNN for spatial data processing, transformer models used in adaptive learning, and DL models for multimodal adaptive learning systems. In Section III, various DL models are trained and evaluated in addition to Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA). Section IV discusses the use of the transformer model to generate adaptive learning paths. Section VI discusses the conclusions, limitations, and scope of future work.

## II. BACKGROUND AND LITERATURE REVIEW

## A. AI IN EDUCATION: BROAD OVERVIEW

AI integration in education has been a significant boost in creating better learning systems, customarily for curriculum and personalized learning. Studies such as [11] provide a primary background on deep learning architectures and how CNNsandRNNsprocesslargedatasets from which the model must learn for adaptation. One of the advantages of this study is the analysis of architectures for basic pattern recognition, based on the adaptation of learning machines. However, these studies often do not report practical examples of SN students, implying that generalizing the benefits of such technologies to this group of children is difficult.

CNNs are invaluable for processing visual learning data for content engagement and automated evaluations [12]. In engagement tracking, students' facial expressions during recorded or live lectures are analyzed using CNNs to assess whether they are interested or bored. If a student is disengaged from the content, the system can lower the content difficulty level or add motivating features in an attempt to regain student attention. In addition, automatic grading of handwritten answers can be provided through feedback using CNNs' recognition of digits, letters, and diagrams. CNNs further perform content recommendation by recognizing images in educational graphics so that the system can recommend additional materials based on the interaction of the student with resources.

On the other side, RNNs assist in learning sequence analysis through pattern recognition of a student's progress to predict future learning trajectories [13]. When an individualized learning path is constructed, RNNs, including LSTM and Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) models, examine the order in which students perform activities, such as time on lessons and quizzes. This enables the system to anticipate the learner's needs in later stages and adapt the lesson plans proactively.



## B. MACHINE LEARNING FOR ADAPTIVE LEARNING SYSTEMS

Adaptive systems also involve identifying performance and preferences for content delivery and feedback, which can be successfully handled with the help of machine learning models. Discussions of these systems by [14] highlighted the features of flexibility and how neural networks modify learning paths. Their study demonstrates that these models are flexible in various educational settings. Nevertheless, a limitation of these models is that they are highly dependent on large labelled datasets, which may not be easily found in the context of specialized education for learners with special needs. Thus, future studies that apply such models in these settings would require additional datasets.

## C. NEURAL NETWORKS AND PERSONALIZED LEARNING

Neural networks are advantageous, particularly for applications in which each learner requires different input data from which they are capable of learning. Many studies have successfully applied neural networks in adaptive education because they can consider the varying rates and styles of student learning. For example, [15] discussed the learning of students using neural networks and their enhancing effects on the engagement and learning aspects of students with deficits. Something that remains unclear in this study is that despite its focus on the strengths of neural networks in terms of customization of learning, the approach could not satisfy the full needs of students with sensory disabilities since neural networks mostly work with either images or texts.

## D. CONVOLUTIONAL NEURAL NETWORKS (CNNS) FOR VISUAL LEARNING

CNNs are best exploited for handling visual data, making them suitable for handling images and visual content data. This ability should be used in educational applications that offer a visual means of teaching, which is useful for particular learning disabilities. For example, as demonstrated in [16], CNNs have advantages when dealing with visual input and image classification for learning, and may be used as adaptive learning tools in education. However, CNNs do not model sequence or temporal data, which presents a problem for any application that needs to understand the language or behavior over time.

Because of their capability to extract hierarchical features, CNNs perform best with visual data. For instance, ResNet-50 is popular in image-based engagement detection, in which students' facial recognition expressions are evaluated to determine the levels of attention in adaptive learning environments [17]. Similarly, the VGG-16 was used to evaluate handwritten responses to enable automated scoring and feedback [18]. In aiding students with dyslexia, CNNs based image classification models help automate the grading and feedback processes by improving font readability through the alteration of font, spacing, and contrast. These examples demonstrate the importance of CNN architectures in improving adaptive learning using CNN's.

## E. RECURRENT NEURAL NETWORKS (RNNS) AND SEQUENTIAL DATA PROCESSING

LSTM and GRU are highly efficient for analyzing language and time-dependent learning interactions, because RNNs are designed to work on sequential data. These networks perform well in settings where an adaptive learning system interacts with learners and the context must be interpreted over time. Goodfellow et al., state that these areas are perfectly suited for use by LSTMs and GRUs since they are sequencedependent activities, like language processing and students' interaction patterns [14]. Nevertheless, a drawback of RNNs is their poor computational efficiency and vanishing gradient, which can hamper their performance in real-life educational applications if not optimized.

## F. TRANSFORMERS IN ADAPTIVE LEARNING

These transformer architectures have undergone a shift to utilize contextual information in language-processing applications. The BERT and GPT models, among others, are transformers that can process the context of words and phrases and offer useful feedback and responses in flexible learning spaces. Vaswani et al. detailed the effectiveness of transformers in manipulating long-range dependencies of text data necessary for adaptation [19]. However, transformers are excellent in context-aware applications, and their inherent structure and training demands prove to be crucial barriers, despite their mightiness, especially in learning-deficient environments such as education.

Adaptive learning systems pose significant challenges for serving students with disabilities [20]. Conventional information technology in learning has developed with some presumptions of learning deficiencies. Hence, they cannot fully address the learning deficiencies of individual students [21]. Studies on adaptive systems have shown that most do not incorporate sensory or cognitive impairment, which eliminates students with such conditions who require an alternative way of interacting. Nevertheless, one shortcoming of the work of Edyburn et al. is that it provides a critically important analysis of these difficulties, but does not propose concrete technical solutions for coping with them; thus, further investigation of adaptive multimodal systems is required [22].

A type of bid data or multiple-modality adaptive learning model was proposed to solve the problems and shortcomings of single-mode learning systems. Owing to the combined use of text, images, and sounds in the learning process, these systems can provide students with a wider range of needs, especially those with disabilities, with materials that would best suit their needs. A related argument was proposed by Rose &amp; Meyer, who pointed out that multichannel learning environments increase people's interest because information can be comprehended in several ways [23]. However, there are some limitations associated with the proposed approach, such as the technical difficulty in facilitating different data types and ensuring consistency between them, which can be problems in the practical realization of the system and its application.

## G. DEEP LEARNING IN MULTIMODAL ADAPTIVE LEARNING SYSTEMS

CNN and transformers have been successful in predicting the function of genes together with multimodal adaptive learning using several types of inputs. Schwartz et al. operationalized these models in their study of multimodal learning environments to enhance accessibility by supporting variations in visual, aural, or textual interfaces [24]. Strengths such as the deployment of multimodal methodologies in actual educational contexts are qualities that enrich this study. Nevertheless, drawbacks are in question, the model learning and training process, and the necessity to use a substantial number of labelled samples for desired accuracy regarding a great variety of inputs.

When considering the future of adaptive learning, these types of systems based on newly developed advanced neural network architectures can help change special needs education. The further development of the application of neural models is of great interest. For example, the application of CNNstovisual tasks and transformers for contextual learning resulted in a multifaceted adaptive configuration [25]. This study also focuses on the possibility of using multiple AI architectures simultaneously to meet extant learning needs. However, there are still barriers to overcome, such as high computational costs, ethical concerns regarding the use of learners' data, and the enhancement of teachers' capacity in the use of these tools in learning environments.

Table 1 provides an overview of state-of-the-art research on the positive outcomes and some drawbacks of using multimodal strategies in adaptive learning for learners with special needs.

## III. MATERIAL AND METHODS

Figure 1 shows the complete process of providing adaptive learning paths for students with special needs. Each stage of the process is explained as follows.

## A. DATASET OVERVIEW AND FEATURE DETAILS

The dataset can be accessed from https://figshare.com /s/3ae5b09234338c01263a. The features used in the dataset was extracted from publicly available datasets provided by the National Center for Education Statistics (NCES) through their website https://nces.ed.gov/programs/coe/indicator/cgg /students-with-disabilities. This study considered a dataset designed to model heterogeneous learning profiles of students with different disabilities. The dataset includes attributes that enable one to create a comprehensive picture of a student's particulars including age, intellect, and overall performance. A student ID was assigned to each student, which contained all the children's data separately, thus enabling the study to be clear and effective. The dataset encompasses school-aged children and adolescents, particularly those aged 5-18 years, and depicts various advances in and levels of educational achievement of learners.



Regarding the male-female ratio, the dataset comprises 'Male,' 'Female' and 'Non-Binary' which are broad categories providing a holistic attempt to establish adaptive learning requirements of all genders. It is important to note this demographic diversity when assessing the impact of adaptive learning on various groups. The participants in the dataset were classified according to the nature of their learning disabilities. These include dyslexia; ADHD; autistic spectrum disorder; and visual, hearing, and intellectual disabilities. These types were selected because of their significance in educational performance and their effect on students' learning activities.

The dataset consists of a variety of features that are believed to capture the essential characteristics of students' cognitive and academic performance. An essential feature is reading comprehension score, which defines how well students interpret and understand written words. This score is particularly relevant in examining the learning patterns of students with learning disabilities such as dyslexia. Math score is another important feature that examines the degree of quantitative reasoning possessed by learners, which is necessary for the attainment of set educational goals in most subjects.

The concept of memory performance is captured by a memory score that describes the extent to which a student can wait for some time and remember or retrieve learned material. This feature sheds light on some of the cognitive processes associated with successful learning, particularly among students who are likely to experience memory problems. The attention score in the dataset indicates the level of concentration exhibited by the students, which is a key component for students with attention-related disorders such as ADHD. The addition of a visual-spatial score demonstrates the ability of students to see information and turn it around, a skill that is crucial to learners with visual problems.

In addition to cognitive measurements, the dataset also captures student metrics regarding interactions with adaptive learning platforms, which provides a better understanding of student engagement and learning actions and behaviors. Time was measured in minutes and presented as an indicator of students' degree of interaction with the learning tool offered on the platform. This metric allows us to establish the correlation between use and performance outcomes, and makes it possible to determine the dynamics of learning engagement.

The completed assignment metric represents the number of learning tasks performed by students within the range of 0-10, which indicates the level of student effort and engagement in the learning process. This function serves as an empirical indicator of student persistence and work patterns in an adaptive learning environment. A teacher feedback score was also added as a measure of performance quality, as this measure enhances the assessment of student improvement and underscores the role of external evaluation. This score is measured in terms of teachers' assessments, and provides a counterbalance when supported by quantitative measures.



TABLE 1. Literature review on multimodal approaches to adaptive learning for students with special needs.

As this dataset covers demographic, cognitive, and engagement-based characteristics, it is useful for analyzing various types of approaches applied to adaptive learning for students with special needs. Thus, the integration of a vast number of features makes it possible to provide strong evidence of how adaptive learning systems can address Age IEEE AccesS'



FIGURE 1. End-to-End process for adaptive learning for students with special needs.



various learning issues, and therefore advocate differentiated educational approaches that may fit learners' profiles.

## B. DATASET PREPROCESSING

Ensuring the quality and reliability of the collected dataset prior to model training was very important, and the data preprocessing phase aided this. First, unnecessary columns, which included Unnamed: 0 and student\_id, were removed to eliminate unwanted features. Missing values and duplicate records were checked and a thorough examination confirmed the completeness of the dataset, ensuring data integrity.

Categorical features were encoded in a systematic manner to allow compatibility with the machine learning models. For example, binary categorical features, such as gender, were label encoded, whereas multi-class categorical features, such as disability\_type, were converted using one-hot encoding. To reduce scale discrepancies and standardize the numerical features, Minmax scaling was employed, ensuring that all features were within a comparable range.

Finally, the dependent variable's distribution was retained, and an efficient model evaluation was performed after performing an 80-20 train-test split. The data quality, consistency, and fair distribution obtained through these processing steps form a stiff foundation for subsequent actions.

## D. EXPERIMENTS: BASELINE NEURAL NETWORK MODEL FOR ADAPTIVE LEARNING

A deep feedforward network was constructed and used as a baseline model to predict the student performance. Up to a certain point, the architecture includes an input layer, three hidden layers, and an output layer, each of which performs a certain role in terms of structuring and modelling the nonlinear relationships between the variables and the dependent variable, resulting in the construction of these layers. This network's input layer consists of points that cover student demographics, cognitive abilities, and level of engagement, thereby ensuring a wide span of student attributes.

A feedforward neural network with three hidden layers (128, 256, and 128) used the ReLU activation function. The model was trained for 200 epochs with early stopping based on validation loss. Overfitting was mitigated with dropout (0.3) and the Adam optimizer was used with a learning rate of 0.001. A grid search was implemented on the batch size, unit stride, and learning rate with values of (16,32), (0.01, 0.001), and (64, 128, 256), respectively. The best configuration was found for a batch size of 32, learning rate of 0.001, and 256 hidden units in the middle layer.

In addition, to help increase the level of abstraction over the features, hidden layers were made to decrease in the order of dimensions. The first hidden layer contained 64 neurons and used a Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU), which is a nonlinear activation to output layers used to model complex functions. After this layer, a dropout layer was applied at a rate of 0.3 while training 30% of the neurons to be dropped randomly. This step was performed to avoid overfitting the network, as it ensured that the network was not overly reliant on certain routes during its operation. The second hidden layer comprised 32 needs that were also activated by the ReLU, where another dropout layer was placed at 0.3 to further advance the cause of the reduction in overfitting. Finally, in the third hidden layer, 16 neurons were placed, where more abstraction of the learned information occurred, and the feature space representation was further simplified.

Two neurons in the output layer have a softmax activation function that provides an output that can be interpreted as a probability for binary classification. This design ensures that the model predicts the probability of classifying two classes-passing or failing-making decisions more straightforward. The fundamental principle of this neural network is to take the input vectors and transform them through fully connected layers constructed in a manner that takes a linear combination and, most of the time, applies a nonlinear function. The hidden layers use the ReLU activation function because they enable the model to learn about the nonlinear relationships between the features of the reading methodology, mathematics scores, attention, and other engagement metrics. Internal neural network dropout layers are also highly important for generalization because a few neurons are randomly inhibited as they are switched off, and many neurons do not focus too specifically on the training data in which the model is being trained. The last layer uses softmax activation; it provides class probability distributions, which sum to one, making it easier to interpret the output of the model for classification. The presented probability-based output provides accurate predictions and helps in the calculation of performance indicators such as accuracy.

The Adam optimizer was preferred because it has an adaptive learning rate that changes based on the first

Model training utilizes the categorical\_ cross-entropy loss function that gauges the predicted class probabilities of the expected distribution. This loss function is appropriate for multiclass or binary classifications with one-hot-encoded target variables. Accuracy is the primary metric for measuring how well the training has been performed, which helps track the correct predictions of the model through training.

trained for 200 epochs. In this case, a batch of 32 is considered appropriate because it represents an acceptable compromise between computational speed and consistency in the gradient updates. Such selection aims to ensure that the model receives as many examples in a batch as possible to learn effectively, while accommodating the limits of the system's memory.

Throughout the training period, the dataset was partitioned into training and validation sets such that the generalization ability of the model could be measured periodically during training. The batch of data is processed by the model, predictions for the input are produced, and the produced predictions are compared with true labels to generate losses using categorical\_crossentropy. The weights were further modified using the backpropagation algorithm to reduce the loss depending on the gradient and hyperparameters using the Adam optimizer. Upon completion of the training phase, the trained model was tested on a test set that was not used in the training phase, to avoid introducing any bias in the evaluation of the model. This sequence of events is called the testing phase, and it assesses the model's ability to generalize new data. This is useful because it provides an insight into how an AI model can be utilized in a real-world scenario. To evaluate the predictions, the success metric used was quantitative accuracy, with other measures such as recall and precision utilized as the situation demands. The usefulness of this baseline neural network model provides an understanding of how multimodal educational data can be processed by using basic feedforward architectures. This model is also important because the results of this research set a benchmark in the search for more efficient models, such as recurrent neural networks and transformer architectures, which could be more efficient in capturing the sequential dependencies or interactions of multiple features. Table 2 lists the relevant evaluation metrics for the baseline model to predict students' performance outcomes. These metrics were defined as precision, recall, F1, and support for both classes (Classes 0 and 1).

Class 0 (students predicted not to pass): The model yields 0.21 precision, but the recall was only 0.03, leading to an F1 score of 0.05. This means that even though some correct decisions are made with respect to Class 0, the model does not perform well with respect to sensitivity for this class, and performs many false omissions or errors.

Class 1 (students predicted to pass): The metrics for this class differed significantly in terms of performance. The precision was 0.69, with a recall of 0.95, resulting in an F1 score of 0.80. The performance shows that this class has a strong passing ratio and the model can pass a substantial number of correctly passing students. 0.67 stands as the overall accuracy of the model, showing the general effectiveness for a dataset containing 2000 samples. For the macro average, the results returned (0.45 precision, 0.49 recall and 0.42 for F1 score) are reflective of the model performance for both classes without the bias of differential weights. The weighted average scores often changed owing to skew within the dataset, with a precision of 0.54, recall of 0.67, and F1 score of 0.57, indicating better performance in the large Class 1 group. Figure 9 shows the training and validation accuracies and loss of the baseline model.

## E. TRANSFORMER MODEL

Next, a Transformer-based architecture was used to predict learners' performance. A custom Transformer Encoder Block implemented in TensorFlow is employed. This encoder block IEEE AccesS'



FIGURE 10. Training-validation accuracy and training-validation loss of the transformer model.



TABLE 3. Transformer model evaluation metrics.

emphasizes certain relationships among input features using a multihead attention mechanism. This mechanism allows for the simultaneous consideration of several representation subspaces. The encoder is composed of MultiHeadAttention, which enables the parallel computation of attention, and two fully connected layers are arranged as a feed-forward neural network. A Layer Normalization (LayerNormalization) mechanism was also included because it guarantees the numerical stability of the model and improves the training efficiency by normalizing the inputs of each layer. Additionally, dropout layers were added to minimize overfitting when the model was trained.

A 6-layer transformer encoder with multihead selfattention (eight heads each) and feedforward layers of 512 dimensions was used. Positional encoding was applied to maintain sequence order. Pre-training on a broad multimodal dataset, prior to fine-tuning, was performed on the adaptive learning dataset. The optimizer was set to AdamW with a learning rate scheduler (10% of the steps were warmed and subsequently decayed). A batch size of 16 along with 200 epochs of training was implemented. Hyperparameter Tuning: A random search was performed over learning rates (5e-05, 3e-05, 1e-04), batch sizes (8, 16, 32), and number of attention heads (4,8,12). Optimal results were achieved using eight attention heads, a learning rate of 3e-05, and a batch size of 16.

The attention process comprises two serial operations: Transformer Encoder Block attention, and feature transformation. First, the input passes through the multiheadattention layer and reaches an attention-weighted output, which focuses on the most significant portions in the context of the input sequence. This output is then summed up with the input and undergoes the layer normalization process, which is a standard procedure known as Addition and Normalization. In the second stage, the output after normalization is fed into a feedforward network (dense layers) to enhance the representation of the features. The final step involves transforming the output features by applying the ''Add &amp; Norm'' step to the attention output. Dropouts were applied at each stage to generalize the model.

This Transformer Encoder Block acts as a simple building block when stacking transformers for time-series data, which includes the time spent on activities, the number of answers provided, and explanations of previous questions. These steps allow the model to use encoded pieces of information to determine whether a learner is able to answer the next questions correctly and assist in pattern recognition during the learning process. Focusing on multihead attention and feedforward networks, the architecture defines both time dependencies and feature relationships, which are important for evaluating learner performance.

The essential component of the model is a custom TransformerEncoderBlock that uses multihead attention (MultiHeadAttention) to attend to relevant portions of the input sequence, and a feed-forward network to enhance feature representation. The block incorporates dropout layers to counterbalance overfitting and Layer Normalization to enhance training stability. This encoder block accepts the input sequentially and forms a part of an entire transformer that uses time-series data (input layer). The output of the encoder block was squeezed (flattened) and configured to work with a dense layer (dense) using a softmax function to predict the probabilities of a given outcome, such as the appropriateness of the learners' answers. A categorical crossentropy loss function with the Adam optimizer was used to train the model, and its performance was evaluated in terms of accuracy metrics over several epochs, thereby demonstrating the capability of the model to analyze sequential educational data.



TABLE 4. Deep neural network model performance summary.

Table 3 provides an insightful analysis of the transformer model performance in the test dataset. There are subclass metrics for precision-recall and F1-score for both parts: Class 0, the participants who provided incorrect answers; and Class 1, the participants who provided correct answers. Class 0 metrics indicated that the performance in the identification of other incorrect metrics for this classification achieved a zero score. However, Class 1, which clustered the correct answers, had a very good recall (1.00) and reasonable F1 score (0.82), which confirms that the model will be able to consistently find correct answers.

In this case, the model exhibited a moderate performance accuracy of 69%. The macro average scores (precision: 0.35, recall: 0.50, F1-score: 0.41) measure are presented, which show the average across all classes and, hence, the gap in inequality between class performances. Weighted averages (precision: 0.48, recall: 0.69, F1-score: 0.57) measures show the average across class values, and therefore show a helpful measure of accuracy when determining the overall average accuracy of the model. This evaluation indicates that the model has high efficiency in the prediction of correct answers, whereas soft spots exist when defining false answers. Figure 10 shows the training and validation accuracies and losses of the transformer model.

Table 4 summarizes the evaluation of various deep neural network models, including the Baseline Neural Network (NN), Convolutional Neural Network (CNN), attention-based model, long short-term memory (LSTM), Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), and transformer models, for four parameters: accuracy, precision, recall, and F1 score. Of these models, the transformer model scored the highest accuracy at 69.45% while achieving an ideal recall value (1.00), suggesting that this model is good at retrieving all relevant instances correctly. The Transformer model also possessed the highest F1 score of 0.819711, making it ideal for moderation between precision and recall.

The Baseline NN model performed accurately, with accuracy values of 67.05% and very high recall values of 0.953204, which are crucial for its F1 Score, with values of 0.800726. Similarly, the CNN model exhibited moderate performance with an accuracy of 62.10% and an average F1 score of 0.743572. In general, the Attention, LSTM, and GRU models exhibited lower accuracies, with attention performing marginally better than the LSTM and GRU models. These findings highlight the advantages of the transformer model in terms of learner performance prediction and support its application in this area compared with other NN architectures.

## F. CONVOLUTIONAL NEURAL NETWORK TRAINING AND TESTING

A Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) model suitable for pattern learning was developed, trained, and tested to predict the student performance. The model consists of three layers, where the initial layers have one convolutional onedimensional Conv1D with 32 filters and a kernel size of three pieces, with ReLU as the activation function. This layer extracts the local patterns and relevant feature interactions from the input data. Third, a dropout layer with a fraction of 0.3 is introduced as an additional regularization technique for the model to overcome potential overfitting by switching off a proportion of some of its units at random during the training of the network. Second, a Conv1D layer, which has 16 filters but the same activation function, is used to deepen feature extraction. More abstractions of the data characteristics were captured. Third, the output of the convolutional layers is fed into a flattened layer that outputs the feature graph into a one-dimensional array. This is such that the dense layer can receive them immediately after the flattened layer, which IEEE AccesS'



FIGURE 11. Training-validation accuracy and training-validation loss of the CNN.



further expands the output of the flattened filter to 16 neurons. The last layer is the softmax layer for probabilities obtained from the two neurons of the dense layer, which lasts in the entire network and is built to provide the linear rate and/or binary outcomes of student performance prediction in this case.

The CNN model possesses four convolutional layers: batch normalization and max-pooling. Fully connected layers were added after the classification. The model considers the cross-entropy loss and is trained with an Adam optimizer on an lr of 0.0001. Trained for 200 epochs with early stopping if the validation loss did not improve in five epochs. Grid search on the number of filters (3,4,5), filter sizes of 32,64,128 and learning rates of 0.01,0.001,0.0001. The optimized model had four layers and 64 filters at a learning rate of 0.0001.

The training procedure of the CNN model is such that it is compiled using the Adam optimizer and categorical cross-entropy loss, because both are good for classification. The model was iteratively trained for over 200 epochs with a batch size of 32, and a backpropagation technique was used to update the weights and minimize the categorical cross-entropy loss function. During training, validation data are available to assess the performance of the model, and the trend towards overfitting or underfitting can be observed early. The increased input shape works well, such that the data are reshaped to fit the Conv1D layer without losing the sequential order of the data. Finally, the model was tested on a test dataset to evaluate its performance in terms of generalization to unseen data. Measures, such as network accuracy, were used to ensure that the network accurately captured the temporal structure of the dataset.

Table 5 summarizes the evaluation metrics of the CNN model developed for forecasting the student outcomes. Class 0 (students who were not expected to pass the course) had a precision value of 0.33, recall value of 0.23, and F1-score value of 0.27, which shows that the vast majority of students who were expected not to pass the course could fail with no significant likelihood that they would fail. Class 1 (students predicted to pass) appears to be better off, with precision-recall and F1-scores higher than those of the previous class, at respective values of 0.70, 0.79, and 0.74, indicating that several students have a strong chance of passing the course level.

TABLE 6. Attention model evaluation metrics.

In general, the accuracy rate of the model is 0.62, which is a mediocre performance on a dataset with 2000 samples. In addition, the macro average precision, recall, and F1-score yielded values of 0.52, 0.51, and 0.51, respectively, indicating average performance for both classes. The weighted average considers the proportion of instances of the classes and gives the values of 0.59 for precision, 0.62 for recall and 0.60 for the F1 score, which implies that the model is relatively good at predicting the majority class, while the minority class (Class 0) can be better managed. ## G. ATTENTION MODEL

In this paper, we propose an attention-based model that improves the network approach by focusing on important input features. The architecture begins with an input layer that is used to resize the input data for compatibility with the attention mechanism. Two fully connected layers with 64 neurons activated by Rectified Linear Units (ReLUs) were then implemented to obtain a multiple-feature space of the input. These layers facilitate feature extraction before IEEE AccesS'





100

Epoch

FIGURE 12. Training-validation accuracy and training-validation loss of the attention model.

the attention layer filters and emphasizes the relevant features. The attention mechanism assigns different relevance to various segments of the input sequences, enabling the model to concentrate on where it matters most. This function significantly enhances the efficiency of the model in understanding the dependencies and intricate relationships within the data.

The attention output is the flattened layer, which transforms the attended features into a 1-D vector, before passing through a full connection layer with 16 neurons for further feature abstraction. The last fully connected layer is a softmax activated layer that provides the probabilities of the classes assigned to a given problem as a binary classification issue. The model was then trained using the Adam optimizer and categorical cross-entropy loss for 200 epochs with a batch size of 32. This configuration assists in learning and guarantees generalization through validation monitoring. The strength of the attention mechanism as a way to concentrate on salient features makes this model a promising approach to modelling patterns in educational data.

Table 6 shows the assessment indicators for the attention-based model applied to the classification of student performance results. Class 0 (for students who were likely to fail) obtained a precision, recall, and F1-score of 0.33, thus implying high operation with nearly similar sensitivity and precision. When it comes to the classification of students among Class 1 (students predicted to pass), it has a relatively higher accuracy with a precision of 0.70, recall of 0.71 and F1-Score of 0.71 is good enough to conclude that the majority of the class is identified.

The accuracy of the model was 0.59, indicating moderate results in making predictions for a dataset with 2000 samples. The 0.51 of Macro Avg Precision, Avg Recall, and Avg F1-Score indicate that the accuracy of the model is evenly split between both classes; the weight average score of 0.59 also suggests a balanced model output. However, the classification of Class 1 appears reasonable based on the model, whereas there is scope for improving the classification of Class 0. ## H. LONG SHORT-TERM MEMORY PERFORMANCE AND PREDICTIONS

The network architecture had one LSTM layer containing 128 hidden units and one fully connected dense layer that employed a Softmax function for classification. Because the dataset had sequences of different lengths, padding was used such that all inputs to the model were of a standard size.

The optimization process used Adam with a learning rate of 0.001, because it offered a reasonable compromise between the speed of convergence and generalization. The model was set up with a multiclass categorical cross-entropy loss function for the same educational recommendations for different classes of students. To avoid overfitting the model, dropout regularization (0.2) was used after the LSTM layer. The training took 200 epochs, and an early stopping strategy supervised the validation accuracy. Training was stopped if, for five consecutive epochs, the model's validation accuracy did not improve, to avoid overfitting and computation waste. The memory and training stability were optimized with a batch size of 64.

The long short-term memory (LSTM) model was developed to consider dependencies in the sequence of data concerning student performance. The proposed model started with an LSTM network of 64 units, and its main function was to read the sequence and remember the crucial steps. This layer allows for deeper temporal feature extraction because it

## I. DENSE AND OUTPUT LAYER TRANSFORMATION

After passing through the stacked LSTM layers, a dense layer with 16 neurons was applied to a nonlinear ReLU transformation as follows :



Finally, the Softmax activation function outputs the predicted probabilities.



The suggested layout was composed of profuse long short-term memory (LSTM) layers. Immediately thereafter, there was a single dense layer with 16 neurons and ReLU activation, which was responsible for performing nonlinear transformations and additional abstraction of the learned features. The output layer utilizes activation softmax with two neurons, which is used to predict the student's performance on Pass or Fail. The model was trained using the Adam optimizer in an adaptive manner and categorical cross-entropy loss over 200 epochs with a batch size of 32. The input data are transformed to match the format relevant to the LSTM processing input data type. Finally, the model was tested on a different dataset to make general comparisons for appropriate evaluation of the model patterns across increased time intervals in the dataset.

Table 7 lists the evaluation criteria of the LSTM model for recognizing student outcomes. For Class 0 (students predicted not to pass), we found a low precision of 0.30 and a low recall of 0.28, resulting in a very low F1-score of 0.29, which appears to be insensitive to this class, and a high percentage is misclassified as negative. However, in Class 1, the students who were predicted to pass showed a higher performance, with a precision of 0.69, recall of 0.72, and IEEE AccesS'

TABLE 8. GRU model evaluation metrics.

F1-score of 0.70, which indicates a better performance of the model in predicting the majority class.

The overall accuracy was 0.58; therefore, it exhibited a moderate performance when working with an overall dataset of 2000 samples. The macro average of each measure was 0.50, meaning that the model could classify instances of each class with fairly good accuracy, whereas the weighted average displayed the model's preference for the larger class Class 1. From these results, it is understood that although there is a fair understanding of temporal dependencies using LSTM models, there is a need for further upgrades in classification in general, and Class 0 in particular. ## J. GATED RECURRENT UNIT (GRU)

The model architecture had one GRU layer with 128 hidden units, followed by a dense layer with Softmax for classification. Because the GRU of the network had a fixed-length requirement for sequences, zero padding was used to make all input sequences uniform for the GRU network. The model was trained with Adam, using a learning rate of 0.001 for fast convergence and stability. A categorical cross-entropy loss function was used to address the multiclass classification problems. To avoid overfitting, dropout (0.2) was used after the GRU layer. The model was trained for 200 epochs with early stopping based on validation accuracy. Training was stopped if the validation accuracy did not increase over five epochs, to reduce unnecessary calculations. A batch size of

32 was chosen to optimally use memory while maintaining stable training.

The GRU model applied in this study follows a sequential structure for input sequence classification and employs two Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) layers to capture temporal relationships. The model starts with a GRU layer of 64 units, whose input shape is determined by the input\_shape input sequences. The return sequences are set to True so that this layer can produce the entire output sequence, and the next GRU layer continues to capture dependencies over the inputs. The second-layer GRU consisted of 32 units, from which we passed another fully connected layer of 16 units to introduce nonlinearity through the ReLU activation function. The last layer in the model is a dense layer with softmax activation and thus produces probabilities in two classes. This architecture is intended for use in binary classification problems in which sequences of data values are present. The mathematical operation of the GRU is as follows:

The GRU network processes sequential input data using the following operations.

## 1) UPDATE GATE

The update gate controls how much of the past information needs to be passed along to the future :



## 2) RESET GATE

The reset gate determines how much of the previous hidden state contributes to the current candidate state :



## 3) CANDIDATE HIDDEN STATE

The candidate hidden state computes a potential update based on the reset gate and current input :



## 4) HIDDEN STATE UPDATE

The final hidden state is a linear interpolation between the previous hidden state and the candidate state, weighted by the update gate :



## K. DENSE AND OUTPUT LAYERS

After the GRU layers, a dense layer and output layer process the data for classification.

## 1) DENSE LAYER TRANSFORMATION

A dense layer with 16 neurons performs a nonlinear ReLU transformation :



## 2) OUTPUT LAYER PREDICTION

A softmax activation function outputs the probabilities.



During training, the model was compiled using the Adam optimizer, because it automatically adjusted the learning rate during training to achieve more rapid convergence. This is a multiclass classification problem; thus, categorical cross-entropy was used as the loss function and accuracy was selected as the primary performance measure. During training, the changes were processed for more than 200 epochs using a batch size of 32 while using validation data for the general performance of the model. Recording the history\_gru variable makes it easier to check the training as well as the validation accuracy and loss, which is important when determining the extent to which the model may overfit. This study focuses on learning architectures that include recurrent and convolutional models, as well as training configurations to achieve high levels of accuracy when classifying sequential data.

Table 8 presents different evaluation parameters for the binary classification task related to the GRU model. The performance of the model for each class (i.e., classes 0 and 1) was evaluated in terms of the precision, recall, and F1 score. Owing to this imbalance, better results were observed for Class 1, with a precision of 0.69, recall of 0.71, and F1 score of 0.70, whereas Class 0 consisted of only 611 instances. The overall accuracy of the model was 57 per cent. The final result, which is the macro-average of the Precision, Recall, and F1-Score, was 0.46, which is the average of both classes without considering their size. However, the weighted model (0.56 precision, 57 recall, and 0.57 F1-score) corrects for the class distribution, indicating that the model is more accurate when predicting a larger class category. Figure 14 shows the training and validation accuracies and the loss of the GRU model.
