## IV. UTILIZING TRANSFORMERS FOR GENERATING ADAPTIVE LEARNING PATHS

To further improve the performance of the transformer model in generating adaptive learning paths, we performed the following steps.

- Dataset Balancing
- Training and evaluating transformer model on balanced dataset.

## A. DATASET BALANCING

One of the primary issues highlighted in the dataset is the lack of proportionate balance in the final\_performance variable, which is referred to as the dependent feature. First, there was a class imbalance issue within the dataset, where one class was represented excessively compared to the other. Such an imbalance may result in biased model performance, in which case the classifier will favor the majority class and reduce its ability to accurately predict cases in the minority class. To resolve this problem and improve model generalization, the author applied the synthetic minority oversampling technique (SMOTE).

Unlike other simple oversampling techniques, SMOTE synthesizes new examples in the minority class instead of simply copying existing examples. This method uses IEEE AccesS'



FIGURE 17. AUC-ROC for the transformer model after data balancing.



k-nearest neighbors (KNN) to create new candidates for the transformed target attribute within the domain of known examples. In this study, SMOTE was used to enhance the balance of the final\_performance feature by oversampling records of both class labels (0 and 1) to 7000 each. Thus, the dataset was able to achieve a balanced class distribution without losing the original structure and statistical properties.

After applying SMOTE, the dataset was balanced, ensuring that there was no bias with respect to the training of the model. This measure assisted with performance classification, mitigating overfitting to the majority class and ensuring that the prediction model had good generalization capability for new data. The balanced dataset served as an excellent starting point for model training with machine-learning algorithms, which produced more accurate and unbiased results. Figure 15 illustrates the class distribution of the final\_performance features for the pass and fail students.

## B. TRAINING AND EVALUATING TRANSFORMER MODEL ON BALANCED DATASET

Once the data balancing strategies were applied, the transformer model was trained and evaluated to ascertain whether there was any improvement in generalization and robustness. The dataset was prepared by normalizing the numerical features and transforming categorical labels into one-hot vectors. I designed a transformer model with an encoder block that contained multihead attention, feedforward layers, and layer normalization. The model was trained for 200 epochs with categorical cross-entropy loss using the Adam optimizer. The test set was evaluated for performance using accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score, and AUC-ROCandevaluated using a confusion matrix. Figure 16 and 17 shows the confusion matrix and AUC-ROC curve for the transformer model. Table 9 presents the classification report of the transformer model.

## C. UTILIZING TRANSFORMER FOR GENERATING ADAPTIVE LEARNING PATHS

Figure 18 shows the complete workflow of the adaptive learning paths generated by the transformer model. The parameters specified were task difficulty, engagement rates, past success, and amount of time spent by students on tasks. These parameters were intended to reflect the heterogeneity in students' learning patterns with an emphasis on meeting the needs of specific disabilities. Such disabilities include Dyslexia, Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, Autism Spectrum Disorder, Visual impairment, Hearing Impairment, and Intellectual disabilities.

TABLE 9. Classification report for the transformer model showing precision, recall, and F1-score for each class. The model achieved an overall accuracy of 75%, indicating balanced performance across both classes.

Mathematical Foundation for Adaptive Learning Path Generation using Transformers: Weemployed a transformerbased deep-learning architecture to design adaptive learning paths tailored specifically for visually impaired students. The following sections describe key equations and their significance in this context.

## 1) INPUT REPRESENTATION AND ENCODING

The input to the transformer model includes structured and unstructured data such as learner profiles, task descriptions, and resource metadata (e.g., difficulty level and accessibility features). This input is tokenized and embedded into a continuous vector space.

## Positional Encoding:



where i is the position index and k is the embedding dimension index. These encodings were added to X , allowing the model to learn the positional dependencies.

Self-attention enables the model to capture the relationships between the input tokens, which are critical for generating paths that prioritize accessibility and content suitability.

For each student, three types of interactions with tasks were created using random data :

- On a task difficulty scale, the integer range was between 1 and 5.
- Engagement score was a scalar in the interval 0.5 - 1.0.
- Past performance was 'success' and 'failure' coded as '1' and '0' respectively.
- Time spent performing tasks ranged from 1 minute to 15 minutes.
- The response was binary and based on correct response completion.

The total responses were calculated and averaged as the active dependent variable of interest and the number of correct responses was averaged. After such an operation, the target variable comprises two classes: 0 or 1.

## D. ADAPTIVE LEARNING PATH GENERATION

As part of the intervention, students identified as struggling underwent task performance evaluation based on set learning objectives. The suggested strategies include the following.

- Encouragement and practice for tasks classified as tough.
- Integrating multi-sensory content to improve engagement.
- Providing students with additional time to complete tasks.
- Frequent and purposeful evaluation targeting the change of strategies.

Figure 19 shows how the transformer model generates adaptive learning paths for students with dyslexia. Table 10 presents details of the adaptive learning paths generated by the transformer model. Similarly, Table 11 presents the adaptive learning paths generated for the students with ADHD.

Moreover, the transformer model was used to formulate adaptive learning paths for students with Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD), Visual Impairment and Hearing Impairment. This entails training the transformer model with several specific student features, enabling it to understand the more intricate associations between the requirements of each individual and the most effective educational modifications. The approach can be outlined in two key steps: (1) training the transformer on special needs features and (2) implementation of the transformer in the development of adaptive learning paths.

The training data of the transformer model included a large array of data points, forming an understanding of the learning needs, difficulties, and preferences of students with disabilities. Some salient features include the following.

## 1) SENSORY SENSITIVITIES

which include determinants of students' attention and response to various sensory inputs, most especially for students with ASD who have been identified as requiring specific sensory learning environment characteristics.

## 2) COMMUNICATION ABILITIES

which consists of information on how a particular student communicates through sign language or gestures that are needed to develop learning tools for students with hearing or sight impairment or both.

## 3) VISUAL AND AUDITORY ACCESS

Information on the client's era of visual and hearing incapacity, the inclination that aids in determining the model's assistive device recommendation and alternate content version formats.

## 4) BEHAVIORAL AND EMOTIONAL REGULATION

Information on student behaviour and the need for emotional control often presents a challenge to students with ASD and need assistance in learning contexts.

The self-attention of the transformer model was used to capture the dependencies among these features, helping the model establish an intricate binding between individual student profiles and the most desirable types of support. For a single series of continuous supervised learning processes, the transformer model was taught regarding which educational strategies and instrumentation were connected to which set of tasks. More practically, the model showed that students suffering from great sensitivity to stimuli benefit from wellordered environments, visual timetables, and sensory breaks, whereas students with sight impairment vision needed tactile materials, braille displays, and corresponding orientation training. The adaptive learning paths generated for ASD, visual impairment, and hearing impairment are shown in figure 20, 21 and 22, respectively.



TABLE 10. Adaptive learning path for students with Dyslexia.

## E. USING EXPLAINABLE ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE TO UNDERSTAND STUDENTS WITH HEARING IMPAIRMENT

We adopted a multiphase strategy to explore the academic challenges faced by hearing-impaired students by integrating the principles of supervised machine learning and eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) methods. The methodology entails the process of data cleaning, model development, and use of different XAI approaches to retrieve informative explanations for the model's outcomes.

The dataset used in this study included various factors related to demographic characteristics, educational background, and other types of assistance offered to children with hearing impairment. The main features were age, sex, financially active background, type of school attended (special or regular), number of students in classes, number of interpreters available, hours of therapy attended, use of AT, level of hearing loss, presence of other disabilities, parents' participation, and achievement levels (the results of the tests). For phase three, categorical variables were dummy-coded, while continuous variables were z-rationed to assist in the comparison of the parameters.

The main predictive model adopted was a Random Forest Regressor because of its efficient performance in mixed-type data, its characteristics of not easily overfitting, and its ability to provide feature-importance scores. To obtain trustworthy assessments, the dataset was divided into training and testing subsets in an 80:20 ratio. The performance of the model was evaluated using the mean squared error. After the model

TABLE 11. ADHD challenges and adaptive learning paths generated by transformer.

performed well in terms of prediction accuracy, the procedure was further explored using artificial intelligence techniques to determine how different characteristic variables and their combinations affected the outcome.

To examine the relative importance of the features for the predictions made by the Random Forest model, SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) was used. The SHAP is a game-theoretic approach that works within a single coherent framework and provides global and local explanations of how each feature affects the output of the model. This was performed using a summary plot, which provides an aggregate of the total feature contributions to predict the output, as shown in figure 23. Furthermore, the figures of the model's variable importance scores (parental involvement) were correlated and explained by the degree of each intelligence goal achieved, with the predicted feature value as the dependent variable, as shown in figure 24. Finally, the feature importance scores embedded in the Random Forest model were collected and plotted, as shown in Figure 25, allowing the audience to compare the dependency of any given feature. With the combined approach of SHAP and the visualization of the features' importance, the understanding IEEE AccesS'

To add interpretability to the Random Forest model, weapplied the local interpretable model-agnostic explanation (LIME) framework. LIME's purpose of LIME is to interpret the prediction of a single instance given a complex model by fitting a local surrogate model, which is much simpler.

A special tabular explainer was prepared to interpret the tabular dataset along with the trained model to explain the actual estimate in the context of regression. The variables most responsible for individual observations in the data were elicited using the explainer and how they shaped the predicted outcome. For example, when the target for a single student's prediction was trained, ten predictors were found to be responsible for predicting the actual performance score, as shown in figure 26. This perspective is beneficial for comprehending more reasons for the model's recommendation in particular cases within a recommendation, which in turn makes it applicable for decision making.

Partial Dependence Plots (PDPs) were created to establish the individual infectiousness and impact of selected attributes, Parent Involvement, and Frequency and Duration of Therapy Sessions on the expected performance outcomes of students with hearing loss, as presented in Figure 27. Such plots present the strength of the relationship between the model predictions and each selected feature, while keeping all other features constant.

## 1) PARENT INVOLVEMENT

The graph then explains this phenomenon by showing that, as Parent Involvement increases, student performance is also expected to improve. The shifts in the curves caused by considerable variations in parental involvement level explain the model's predictive power for performance improvement.

## 2) THERAPY SESSIONS

Similarly, the number of therapy sessions attended demonstrated an active and dynamic effect on predicted outcome variables. The data showed that the therapy sessions had a nonlinear effect on student performance, as indicated by fluctuations in the plot.

To further investigate the variability in model predictions for the Parent Involvement feature, the partial dependence plots and the individual conditional expectation curves were combined in the PDP-ICE approach and plotted as shown in figure 28.

This graph illustrates the overall impact of parental involvement on student success in a model's prediction, depicting the tendency of the model concerning how features change.

This two-pronged approach provided a deeper understanding of this issue. If all ICE explain such variability in behavioral assumptions, then the ICE would outshine the influence of the self-explanatory universality tendencies of the predominant component seen in focus. However, PDP usually explains the main tendency, while ICE captures the variability of the behavior and differences between people and allows us to draw conclusions about which students or subgroups students should belong to and which factor of Parent Involvement is most paramount, with others likely being the opposite. Such information is important for designing interventions that fit the individual or designed profiles of students.

## A. KEY FINDINGS FROM LITERATURE ON SINGLE-MODE LEARNING

## 1) TEXT-BASED ADAPTIVE LEARNING SYSTEMS

These models operate through Natural Language Processing (NLP) techniques primarily and neglect to consider multimodal student engagement [35].

## 2) SPEECH-BASED LEARNING APPROACHES

Used in conducting verbal tests, they are devoid of any visual and behavioral elements which results in a considerably lower degree of individualization [36].

## 3) IMAGE-BASED LEARNING METHODS

Applied in recognizing handwriting or other forms of visual education, but are ineffective in teaching the concepts [37].============================================================
## Paper Source: Application of AI-Assisted Adaptive Learning System in Special Education
============================================================



## Application  of  AI-Assisted  Adaptive  Learning System  in  Special  Education

Yongji  Shen  and  Kexiong  Cui ( B )

Sanya  Aviation  and  Tourism  College,  Sanya  572000,  Hainan,  China

kx\_cui1996@sohu.com

Abstract. Many  children  are  facing  the  challenge  of  being  marginalized  by  mainstream education  systems  due to disabilities such as visual, auditory, intellectual  developmental,  or  autism.  They  often  have  difficulty  accessing  educational resources  that  meet  their  special  needs,  which  hinders  their  comprehensive  development  and  integration  into  society.  However,  with  the  rapid  advancement  of  technology,  artificial  intelligence  (AI)  technology  has  brought  new  solutions  to  such problems.  In  the  field  of  education,  adaptive  learning  systems,  as  an  important branch  of  AI  applications,  are  gradually  showing  great  potential  in  special  education.  This  article  proposes  an  innovative  AI  adaptive  learning  system  that  deeply integrates  deep  learning  (DL)  algorithms.  It  can  intelligently  analyze  students' learning  habits,  interests,  and  ability  status,  and  dynamically  adjust  teaching  content,  difficulty,  and  progress  based  on  this,  designing  personalized  learning  plans for  special  needs  children  with  different  needs.  Experimental  results  have  shown that  the  system  can  accurately  recommend  the  educational  resources  that  students need,  thereby  significantly  increasing  their  interest  and  participation  in  learning.

Keywords: Artificial  intelligence · Adaptive  learning  system ·

## 1 Introduction

In  the  information  wave  of  the  21st  century,  the  rapid  development  of  Internet  technology  not  only  reshapes  the  appearance  of  traditional  industries,  but  also  profoundly affects  the  field  of  education,  especially  the  field  of  special  education  [1].  For  special needs  children,  this  change  is  particularly  significant  as  they  often  face  more  complex and  diverse  learning  needs,  and  traditional  education  models  often  struggle  to  provide sufficient  flexibility  and  personalized  support  [2].  The  introduction  of  AI  technology has  brought  unprecedented  development  opportunities  for  special  education,  opening up  a  path  towards  personalized,  efficient,  and  adaptable  learning  for  these  special  needs children  [3].  Personalized  learning,  as  a  key  path  to  achieving  comprehensive  student development,  emphasizes  tailoring  learning  content  and  teaching  strategies  based  on individual  differences  of  students  to  meet  their  unique  learning  needs  [4].  In  the  field  of special  education,  this  concept  is  particularly  important.  Due  to  physiological,  psychological,  or  cognitive  differences,  special  needs  children  have  more  diverse  and  complex educational  needs.  With  the  increasing  emphasis  on  special  education  in  society,  special education  schools  are  gradually  making  personalized  learning  the  core  goal  of  school construction  and  development  [5].

Special  education

This  not  only  requires  schools  to  have  the  ability  to  identify  individual  differences among  students,  but  also  to  build  a  support  system  that  can  dynamically  adjust  teaching content,  methods,  and  progress.  Against  the  backdrop  of  rapid  development  in  mobile internet  and  smart  education,  personalized  adaptive  learning  systems  have  become  a research  hotspot  in  the  field  of  educational  technology  [6].  This  type  of  system  is  based on  big  data  and  can  intelligently  identify  learners'  learning  patterns  and  preferences  by continuously  collecting  and  analyzing  their  learning  data.  It  can  then  dynamically  adjust learning  resources,  difficulty,  and  progress  to  achieve  optimal  learning  outcomes  [7].  For example,  Marino  et  al.  [8]  proposed  a  DL  based  personalized  recommendation  model for  digital  library  resources,  which  mines  user  behavior  patterns  through  DL  algorithms and  provides  learners  with  accurate  learning  resource  recommendations.  Abu  et  al.  [9] utilized  learners'  social  network  information  for  collaborative  filtering  and  recommendation,  predicting  trust  values  and  ratings  in  social  relationships  to  recommend  learning resources to new  learners. This method  is particularly valuable in special  education environments  because  it  can  fully  utilize  peer  influence  and  promote  the  construction  of learning  communities.

The  joint  neural  collaborative  filtering  recommendation  system  proposed  by  Xavier et  al.  [10]  further  improves  the  accuracy  and  personalization  of  recommendations  by combining  deep  feature  learning,  deep  interaction  modeling,  and  association  matrix. Marino  et al. [11] used GPS  technology  to recommend  English  learning  resources, effectively  improving  learners'  English  oral  communication  skills.  These  studies  not only  promote  the  development  of  personalized  recommendation  technology,  but  also provide  valuable  references  for  adaptive  learning  systems  in  the  field  of  special  education. This article proposes an innovative AI adaptive learning system that deeply integrates  DL  algorithms,  aiming  to  provide  more  refined  and  intelligent  learning  support  tailored  to  the  learning  characteristics  of  special  needs  children.  By  intelligently analyzing  students'  learning  habits,  interests,  and  abilities,  the  system  can  dynamically adjust  teaching  content,  difficulty,  and  progress  to  ensure  that  each  student  can  learn  in the  most  suitable  environment  for  themselves.

## 2 Methodology

## 2.1 System  Construction

In  today's  increasingly  popular  digital  education,  personalized  adaptive  learning  systems  have  become  a  key  tool  for  improving  learning  efficiency  and  effectiveness.  This type  of  system  dynamically  adjusts  the  recommendation  of  learning  resources  and  the planning  of  learning  paths  by  deeply  understanding  the  individual  characteristics,  interest  preferences,  learning  styles,  and  ability  levels  of  learners,  aiming  to  provide  tailored learning  experiences  for  each  learner  [12].  The  starting  point  of  personalized  adaptive learning systems  lies in the precise analysis of learner characteristics. This includes but  is  not  limited  to  learners'  mastery  of  basic  knowledge,  learning  speed,  preference types, emotional  states, and learning motivation [13]. Traditional  methods  may  rely on  questionnaire  surveys  or  teacher  evaluations,  but  modern  systems  make  more  use of  big  data  analysis  and  machine  learning  (ML)  algorithms  to  automatically  capture and  analyze  learners'  online  learning  behavior  data,  thus  more  comprehensively  characterizing  learners'  profiles.  Learning  content  recommendation  is  the  core  function  of adaptive  learning  systems,  which  directly  determines  whether  learners  can  obtain  the most  suitable  learning  materials  for  themselves.

Traditional  recommendation  algorithms,  such  as  Probability  Matrix  Factorization (PMF),  can  effectively  utilize  user  item  evaluation  matrices  for  prediction,  but  ignore rich  edge  information  such  as  users'  social  network  relationships,  item  label  descriptions, and  users'  historical  behavior  patterns,  which  are  crucial  for  improving  recommendation accuracy.  To  overcome  this  limitation,  this  paper  proposes  a  new  algorithm  that  combines DL  and  incorporates  edge  information.  Specifically,  we  use  a  fourth-order  convolutional neural  network  (CNN)  to  process  and  analyze  user  and  resource  profile  text  information,  to  extract  deep  level  feature  representations.  Using  a  pre-trained  word2vec  model, convert  text  information  into  dense  representations  in  a  high-dimensional  vector  space, providing  input  for  subsequent  convolution  operations.  In  the  second  layer  of  CNN,  multiple  convolution  kernels  with  different  window  sizes  are  applied  to  extract  local  features from  the  text  vector.  These  convolution  kernels  share  parameters,  reducing  model  complexity  and  enhancing  generalization  ability.  The  third  layer  downsamples  the  feature map  output  by  the  convolutional  layer  through  pooling  operation,  preserving  the  most important  feature  information  while  reducing  computational  complexity.

Finally, in the fourth layer, the  pooled  feature  vectors are  transformed  into  fixed length  text  representation  vectors  using  a  fully  connected  layer.  These  vectors  not  only contain  deep  level  information  about  the  text  content,  but  also  imply  unique  features of users or resources. The improved  structure  of  the  CNN-PMF  model  is  shown  in Fig.  1.  Based  on  the  analysis  of  learner  characteristics  and  content  recommendations, the  system  needs  to  further  plan  personalized  learning  paths.  This  involves  serializing  and arranging  learning  resources  to  ensure  that  the  difficulty  of  the  learning  content  gradually increases, while also conforming  to the cognitive patterns and interests of learners. Learning  effectiveness  evaluation  is  an  indispensable  part  of  adaptive  learning  systems, which  not  only  helps  learners  understand  their  own  progress,  but  also  provides  a  basis for  adjusting  recommendation  strategies  for  the  system.  By  continuously  collecting  data during  the  learning  process,  such  as  test  scores,  participation,  feedback,  etc.,  combined with  ML  models  for  multidimensional  analysis,  learning  effectiveness  can  be  accurately evaluated.

## 2.2 Algorithm  Principle

The  PMF  model  is  a  leader  in  collaborative  filtering  algorithms,  known  for  its  representativeness  and  wide  applicability.  The  core  principle  of  this  model  is  to  fill  in  the unknown  rating  values  by  decomposing  and  reconstructing  the  rating  matrix.  Specifically,  this  process  first  involves  constructing  a  'user  item'  rating  matrix R ,  which  is  then decomposed  into  the  product  of  two  low  dimensional  matrices U , V .  By  utilizing  the inner  product  of U , V ,  we  can  reconstruct  a  new  rating  matrix ˆ R .  The  beauty  of  this step is that  it  allows  items  in  the  original  rating  matrix R that  have  not  been  rated  to also  receive  corresponding  predicted  ratings.  On  this  basis,  we  exclude  items  that  have already  been  rated  by  users,  and  only  sort  the  remaining  items  based  on  the  'reconstructed'  rating  values  to  generate  the  final  item  recommendation  list.  The  objective  function of  this  model  is  to:

Fig.  1. Structure  of  CNN-PMF  model





Among  them, Rij represents  the  actual  observed  score;  And U T i Vj represents  the rating predicted by the model. In order to avoid overfitting, regularization  parameters λ U , λ V are introduced, which are set as λ U = σ 2 /σ 2 U , λ V = σ 2 /σ 2 V , where σ 2 , σ 2 U ; σ 2 , σ 2 V represents  different  variance  terms.  In  addition, n , m refers  to  the  specific values  of  the  total  number  of  users  and  the  total  number  of  projects,  that  is,  there  are n users  and m projects. Iij ,  as  an  indicator  function,  takes  a  value  of  1  in  the  presence  of ratings  and  0  in  the  absence  of  ratings.

The  process  of  solving  the  parameter  matrix U , V is  transformed  into  an  optimization problem,  as  described  below.



Usually,  we  use  batch  gradient  descent  (BGD)  to  solve  the  above  equation.  BGD  uses an  iterative  optimization  strategy  to  continuously  update  the  elements Uim , Vmj in the parameter  matrix U , V ,  aiming  to  gradually  approximate  the  global  or  local  minimum of  the  loss  function J ( U , V ) .  The  specific  iterative  update  rules  follow  the  following principles:





In  the  formula, n represents  the  number  of  iterations; α is  the  learning  rate.

Although  CNN  models  have  limitations  in  mining  key  and  representative  information in project review documents,  they  are able to capture global information  as well as correlations  between  contexts.  Through  the  multi-layer  convolutional  structure  in  the CNN  model,  we  can  effectively  extract  the  interrelationships  between  words  in  project comment  documents,  learn  the  global  features  of  the  project  and  the  connections  between context,  and  generate  implicit  representations  of  the  project.  In  the  convolutional  layer, the  model  extracts  feature  from  the  word  vector  matrix G ,  using  sliding  windows  of  sizes 3,  4,  and  5  for  convolution  operations  to  capture  different  text  features.  This  convolution process  can  be  formally  expressed  as  the  following  formula:



Among  them, A represents  the  activation  value  calculated  on  a  certain  convolution kernel; wi , j represents the weight  parameters  in  the  convolutional  kernel; relu is the activation  function  selected  in  this  article  to  introduce  nonlinear  characteristics; G represents  the  input  of  the  convolutional  layer,  which  is  the  word  vector  matrix.  Through this  convolution  operation,  the  model  is  able  to  extract  feature  information  from  the  input text.

In  the  output  layer  stage,  the  new  feature  quantities  are  further  mapped  to  generate  the final  latent  feature  representation  of  the  project.  Specifically,  CNN  is  used  to  convert  raw project  comment  documents  into  project  feature  vectors,  and  then  output  a  deep  semantic representation  matrix.  This  representation  matrix  is  then  mapped  to  a L dimensional  space to  capture  the  core  features  of  the  item,  and  the  mapping  process  can  be  expressed  as the  following  mathematical  formula:



Among  them, h 1 , h 2 represents  the  mapping  matrix,  and b 1 , b 2 is  the  corresponding bias  term; dz represents  the  output  of  the  pooling  layer; Yj is  the  input  data  for  CNN; w ′ covers  all  parameters  of  CNN.  Ultimately,  the  output  dimension  of  CNN  needs  to be  consistent  with  the  hidden  feature  vector  dimension  in  the  PMF  model  to  ensure compatibility  between  the  two  in  subsequent  processing.

## 3 Result  Analysis  and  Discussion

To  fully  demonstrate  the  performance  advantages  of  the  system  proposed  in  this  article, we  have  specially  planned  a  set  of  comparative  experiments  to  directly  compare  the performance  of our system with traditional deep neural  network  (DNN)  systems  in educational  resource  recommendation.  Table  1  provides  a  detailed  comparison  of  the recommendation  accuracy  between  our  system  and  traditional  systems.  It  can  be  clearly seen from  the table that the accuracy  achieved  by  the  system  in  this article is  much higher  than  that  of  traditional  systems.  This  significant  advantage  is  mainly  attributed to  the  CNN-PMF  model  adopted  by  the  system  in  this  article.  This  model  innovatively combines  the  advantages  of  CNN  and  PMF,  leveraging  both  CNN's  outstanding  ability  in text  feature  extraction  and  PMF's  unique  role  in  collaborative  filtering  recommendation. Specifically,  the  CNN  component  is  responsible  for  in-depth  analysis  of  project  comment documents,  extracting  key  information  and  contextual  connections,  and  generating  highquality  project  feature  vectors;  The  PMF  component  accurately  predicts  users'  potential interest  in  the  project  by  decomposing  the  rating  matrix.  These  two  parts  work  together to  improve  the  accuracy  and  personalization  level  of  the  recommendation  system.

Table  2  provides  a  detailed  comparison  of  the  performance  of  our  system  and  traditional  DNN  system  in  terms  of  student  education  resource  recommendation  recall  rate. Recall  rate,  as  another  key  indicator  for  evaluating  the  effectiveness  of  recommendation  systems,  measures  the  proportion  of  relevant  resources  successfully  recommended by  the  system  among  all  relevant  resources.  The  intuitive  data  display  shows  that  the recall  rate  achieved  by  the  system  in  this  article  far  exceeds  that  of  traditional  systems, which  strongly  proves  the  outstanding  performance  of  the  system  in  the  field  of  educational  resource  recommendation.  This  significant  achievement  is  mainly  attributed  to  the CNN-PMF  model  adopted  by  the  system  in  this  article.  This  model  cleverly  integrates the  technical  essence  of  CNN  and  PMF,  not  only  improving  the  accuracy  of  recommendations,  but  also  greatly  enhancing  the  breadth  of  recommendations.  Specifically,  the CNN component,  with  its  excellent  text  feature  extraction  ability,  deeply  analyzes  project review  documents  and  extracts  highly  summarized  project  feature  vectors.  The  PMF component,  on  the  other  hand,  accurately  grasps  users'  potential  interests  in  the  project by  parsing  the  rating  matrix,  providing  users  with  more  personalized  recommendations that  are  more  tailored  to  their  needs.

Figure  2  visually  compares  the  difference  in  time  consumption  between  the  system proposed  in  this  article  and  the  traditional  DNN  system  for  student  educational  resource recommendation  tasks.  From  the  data  in  the  figure,  it  can  be  seen  that  compared  to  traditional  systems,  the  system  in  this  paper  significantly  reduces  the  time  consumption  when performing  the  same  recommendation  task,  highlighting  the  efficiency  of  the  system  in educational  resource  recommendation.  The  efficiency  behind  this  is  mainly  attributed to  the  CNN-PMF  model  adopted  by  the  system  in  this  article.  This  model  combines  the technical  advantages  of  CNN  and  PMF,  not  only  improving  the  accuracy  and  comprehensiveness  of  recommendations,  but  also  significantly  accelerating  the  processing  speed  of the  system.  Specifically,  the  CNN  part  which  has  strong  feature  extraction  capability  can extract  key  information  from  project  review  documents  quickly,  generate  high-quality project feature  vectors  and  shorten  the  time  of  feature  extraction  effectively.  This  is  complemented  by the  PMF,  which  has  the  task  of  predicting  quickly  economical-use  matrix factorization  algorithm  users'  interest  in  the  project,  speeding  up  the  recommendation.

Due  to  the  competent feature  extraction  function  of  CNN  components,  it  would  be  able  to  extract  key  information  from  project  review  documents,  so  as  to  mine  to get  project  high-definition  features vectors,  thus  providing  a  strong  basis  for  accurate  recommendation.  On  the  other  hand, the PMF  component  also  does  well  in  capturing  the user's potential interests in the project  using  efficient  matrix  factorization  algorithms,  with  a  promotion  of  the  recall rate  and  the  overall  improvement  of  the  recommendation  system's  performance.

## 4 Conclusion

The  research  findings  of  this  article  introduce  an  innovative  AI  adaptive  learning  system that closely integrates DL  algorithms, demonstrating the extensive potential of AI  in  the  field  of  education.  This  system  can  intelligently  analyze  students'  learning habits,  interests,  and  ability  levels,  and  flexibly  adjust  teaching  content,  difficulty,  and progress,  accordingly,  providing  personalized  learning  plans  for  special  needs  children. This  innovative  measure  not  only  meets  the  diverse  learning  needs  of  students,  but  also greatly  stimulates  their  interest  and  participation  in  learning,  opening  up  new  avenues for  improving  educational  equity  and  quality.  The  experimental  results  show  that  this system  exhibits  high  accuracy  in  recommending  educational  resources,  effectively  meeting  the  actual  learning  needs  of  students  and  promoting  the  improvement  of  learning outcomes.  However,  while  acknowledging  the  achievements  of  the  system,  we  should also  acknowledge  its  limitations.  In  the  face  of  complex  or  special  learning  needs,  the adaptive  flexibility  and  accuracy  of  the  system  still  needs  to  be  improved;  At  the  same time,  the  scalability  and  compatibility  of  the  system  are  also  key  technological  challenges  that  need  to  be  addressed  in  the  future  to  ensure  that  it  benefits  a  wider  range  of students  from  different  backgrounds.

## Integrating A.I. in Higher Education: Protocol for a Pilot Study with 'SAMCares: An Adaptive Learning Hub'

Syed Hasib Akhter Faruqui 1 , ∗ , Nazia Tasnim 2 , Iftekhar Ibne Basith 1 , Suleiman Obeidat 1 , Faruk Yildiz 1

1 Engineering Technology, Sam Houston State University 2 College of Education, University of Texas at Austin ∗ Corresponding Author

## Abstract

Learning never ends, and there is no age limit to grow yourself. However, the educational landscape may face challenges in effectively catering to students' inclusion and diverse learning needs. These students should have access to state-of-the-art methods for lecture delivery, online resources, and technology needs. However, with all the diverse learning sources, it becomes harder for students to comprehend a large amount of knowledge in a short period of time. Traditional assistive technologies and learning aids often lack the dynamic adaptability required for individualized education plans. Large Language Models (LLM) have been used in language translation, text summarization, and content generation applications. With rapid growth in AI over the past years, AIpowered chatbots and virtual assistants have been developed. This research aims to bridge this gap by introducing an innovative study buddy we will be calling the ' SAMCares '. The system leverages a Large Language Model (LLM) (in our case, LLaMa-2 70B as the base model) and RetrieverAugmented Generation (RAG) to offer real-time, context-aware, and adaptive educational support. The context of the model will be limited to the knowledge base of Sam Houston State University (SHSU) course notes. The LLM component enables a chat-like environment to interact with it to meet the unique learning requirements of each student. For this, we will build a custom web-based GUI. At the same time, RAG enhances real-time information retrieval and text generation, in turn providing more accurate and context-specific assistance. An option to upload additional study materials in the web GUI is added in case additional knowledge support is required. The system's efficacy will be evaluated through controlled trials and iterative feedback mechanisms. By interlinking Artificial Intelligence and assistive technology in an educational setting, this project aspires to advance personalized learning experiences for students, making meaningful strides in inclusive education.

Keywords: SAMCares, Large Language Model, Adaptive Learning, Interactive Learning, Retriever Augmented Generation

## Introduction

The recent advancement in science and technology has transformed the field of higher education 1 , bringing a paradigm shift in both teaching methodologies and learning experience. This trend can be observed globally from both students' and educators' perspectives. With the development of innovative educational platforms like adaptive learning platforms, virtual and augmented reality (VR/AR) training, and online courses, students get to experience an immersive and interactive learning experience 2 . While traditional classroom settings primarily rely on direct instruction and passive learning, these new educational technologies foster an active learning environment where students are engaged and empowered to take charge of their education 3 .

However, these tools often feature pre-set training materials with certain limitations. These learning platforms, while advanced, generally come with pre-determined answers (online courses) and environments (VR/AR) that have been meticulously developed beforehand by a team of experts/educators 4 . This structure can sometimes be restrictive, especially when learners pose creative or unconventional questions that deviate from the pre-set programming of the system or the scope of the lectures. The rigid framework of these tools means they are not always interpretive or flexible enough to accommodate queries that fall outside their programmed knowledge base or instructional design. Thus, this has led to a growing interest in more innovative and adaptive solutions in education technology that is both adaptive and can enhance the learning experience and outcomes for students in the field 5 . The solutions should be capable of explaining concepts/topics to students in a manner that is responsive to their (students/learners) unique learning styles and adaptable to the diverse and sometimes unpredictable range of questions that a student might pose. These questions depend on the learners themselves. For example, a learner may have varying learning styles, such as visual, auditory, or reading/writing preferences, which can influence how they absorb and process information. Some may prefer hands-on example focused activities, while others may prefer reading text books to comprehend complex concepts. Additionally, cognitive abilities like memory, attention span, and problem-solving skills can influence how learners engage with educational materials. For example, some students may struggle with working memory, requiring additional support to retain information, while others may excel in logical reasoning, quickly grasping abstract concepts. Some who are anxious or lack confidence may require additional practice examples to build their self-efficacy, while those who are highly motivated may benefit from more challenging tasks to maintain their engagement. Furthermore, linguistic diversity must also be acknowledged, considering language preferences. Non-native English speakers may require additional language support to comprehend complex texts. The ideal technology would be able to comprehend these conditions, interpret the knowledge and provide personalized and context-aware explanations similar to a human instructor. This level of adaptability would significantly enhance the learning experience, making it more engaging, effective, and tailored to individual students' needs.

In recent years, advances in artificial intelligence (AI), machine learning, and deep learning have revolutionized not only our day-to-day life but also industries ranging from businesses to finances to manufacturing facilities 6 . Among advances in AI, natural language processing stands out as a particularly transformative technology, especially in the context of higher education. Generative AI (GenAI) is an extension of NLP, which has shown how the most potential 7 . GenAI models like Generative Pre-Trained Transformer (GPT) 8,9 , LLaMa 10 , Mistral 11 etc. are some of the famous predictive generation language models that are currently being used to simulate human conversation, either written or spoken. A large amount of publicly collected data was used to train these models to process and produce text that is human-like. Interestingly, when prompted properly, these models have the ability to generate convincing, creative, and intelligent texts on a wide array of topics 12,13,14 . This capability of GenAI models opens up new possibilities in educational settings. Their application can range from assisting in creating educational content and personalized learning materials to offering sophisticated tutoring systems that can interact with students in a natural, conversational manner. These models can be particularly beneficial in enhancing engagement in e-learning platforms, where personalized, adaptive, context-dependent, and interactive content are key to student success. Thus offering a unique opportunity to reimagine the traditional educational model. By leveraging the abilities of GenAI models, educators and developers can create dynamic learning environments more aligned with individual learners' needs and preferences 14,15 . In literature, several works have tried integrating these tools into their educational ecosystem. To offer interactive educational support to students in both text and voice mode, Lee et al. investigated the use of GenAI tools 16 . Tai and Chen utilized a combination of decision tree hierarchies and natural language processing to provide personalized responses and foster active student participation in learning 17 . Despite challenges in traditional education, such as limited teacher-student interaction and time constraints, chatbots emerged as promising tools to address individual student needs and promote independent learning 18 . In various major fields, including medicine 19 , engineering 20 , business 21 , and education 22 , chatbots demonstrate potential benefits and improved academic performance. Recent studies advocate for their integration to assist students in after-class review sessions, aiming to enhance learning outcomes and student motivation through interactive and personalized educational support 23 . By leveraging AI capabilities using learning theories of constructing knowledge, educators can reimagine learning experiences, moving beyond traditional paradigms to embrace dynamic approaches that cater to individual learner needs and promote deeper engagement. This paradigm shift underscores the transformative potential of GenAI in shaping the future of education, a theme that resonates throughout our exploration of AI-enabled learning in this manuscript.

Nevertheless, rigorous empirical research is needed to establish the true efficacy of these AIpowered systems in enhancing student learning outcomes compared to traditional methods. This study aims to answer that very question. In this research, we plan to execute a controlled trial focusing on an LLM-based intelligent tutoring system named 'SAMCares: An Adaptive Learning Hub' (From this point forward, we will refer it to SAMCares ). The system is developed by leveraging LLaMa-2 70B 10 (a large language model developed &amp; released by Meta) as the base model. In addition, The system will utilize a fixed database, meticulously created to serve as a reliable knowledge repository. This database contains only course materials taught at SHSU. A Retriever Augmented Generation (RAG) system is used to access this database in real-time and provide context-aware and adaptive educational support. Our central hypothesis assumes that the integration of SAMCares into the educational framework will result in significant improvements in several key areas, including but not limited to greater knowledge gains, higher student satisfaction, and lower cognitive load compared to standard educational materials alone. Finally, this study aims to validate the effectiveness of SAMCares and contribute valuable insights into the potential of AI-enhanced learning tools in modern educational settings.

The remainder of the manuscript is structured as follows. The following section describes the over- all study design, recruitment plan, and data collection and storage procedure. Next, we describe the building and usage of SAMCares . Finally, we provide potential challenges and limitations and end with concluding remarks.

## Methods

The primary goal of this research is to investigate whether GenAI tools (especially text generation tools) can enhance students' learning experience and consequently improve their academic performance. To create such a tool SAMCares that can address and adapt to the varied educational needs of students, reflecting our commitment to enhancing the quality and accessibility of learning experiences in higher education. Currently, the developed SAMCares tool has the following features built-in:

1. Aninteractive and personalized learning experience where students can ask questions, access relevant resources, and seek clarification.
2. Quick retrieving process through RAG to keep the knowledge base grounded.
3. Facilitates personalized learning paths, as required by students' needs.
4. Options to add additional learning resources /materials by students to cater to additional queries they might have.

Below, we describe the different aspects of the proposed study protocol

## Study Design and Study Population

This study will be a year-long, stratified, randomized, controlled trial. The sample size for the study is determined to be 150 (A detailed power study is provided in a later section). The inclusion criteria for the study are: (1) Currently enrolled as an undergraduate student at SHSU; (2) 18+ years old; (3) Freshman; (4) Should possess a basic level of technological proficiency; Exclusion criteria: (1) Unwilling or unable to provide the informed consent forms; (2) Unwilling to complete the assignments; (3) Unavailable during the entirety of the study; (4) Unwilling to attend follow-up surveys or interviews.

This study requires collecting and storing data from human subjects for subsequent analysis. The Institutional Review Board (IRB) determined this study doesn't require IRB oversight. This plan includes obtaining informed consent from all participants, ensuring they are fully aware of the purpose of the study, and their right to withdraw at any time without any adverse consequences. The collected data will be stored securely in an encrypted data repository.

## Sample Size for the Pilot Study

Before performing the study, it is crucial to determine an appropriate sample size to ensure our findings' statistical validity and reliability. To achieve this, we conducted a comprehensive power analysis. This was done to calculate the minimum number of subjects required to detect a significant effect of the tool usage, considering a conventional α = 0 . 05 and a power level of 80% . The expected effect size was derived from preliminary course analysis data and pertinent literature 24 .

Wehypothesized that participants interacting with SAMCares tool would demonstrate superior outcomes, with an expected mean score of 80 out of 100 (in quantitative test scores), compared to a mean score of 78 in the control group, both with a standard deviation of 4 . The power calculation was performed using the following formula-



where, absolute difference between two means, ∆ = | µ 2 -µ 1 | , σ is the variance of mean, n is the sample size, α is the probability of Type I error, β is the probability of Type II error, critical z value for a given α or β , and k is the ratio of sample size for groups. For a 1 : 1 ratio recruiting, the power analysis indicated that a minimum sample size of 63 participants per group (total recruitment of 126 participants) was optimal for this study. After careful consideration and adjustments for potential dropout rates and population variability, a total of 150 participants will be recruited for this study. This size balances the need for sufficient statistical power and practical considerations such as time, cost, and resource availability.

## Recruitment

Participant recruitment will be kept limited to the freshman class of the SHSU Engineering Technology Department. This will be facilitated through (1) referrals from faculty members within the department who have agreed to recommend their students for the study; (2) a targeted email communication will be sent to department students; (3) Physical flyers will be posted in key locations across campus; and (4) Departments social media platforms will be used to extend our recruitment efforts further.

## Randomization

Eligible participants will be randomly assigned into two groups; either the group will have access to the SAMCares tool ( n = 75 ) or the group without access to the SAMCares tool with a 1 : 1 allocation. A randomization code will be generated to assign participants to either of the groups. Only after the final selections have been made and the consent forms are signed will the participants be able to know which group they are a part of. A schematic chart of the randomization is shown in Figure 1.

## Data Collection

A structured data collection process has been meticulously planned. A wide array of data will be gathered to ensure a comprehensive understanding of the SAMCares tool's effectiveness (in general, the effectiveness of GenAI in an educational environment). This includes quantitative data such as exam scores and topic assessment tests, which will objectively measure participants' performance and knowledge retention. Additionally, qualitative data will be collected through pre- and post- study surveys, offering insights into participants' perceptions, satisfaction levels, and subjective experiences with the SAMCares tool. An eye tracker will be installed to measure participants' level of engagement and focus during the sessions. This should also reveal the patterns of gaze direction, engagement with the tool, points of focus, and visual fatigue analysis. This will provide us insight into the usability and cognitive load of the SAMCares interface.

Figure 1: Schematic Representation of Randomization and Data Collection Process for SAMCares Tool Evaluation



The data collection process will also include video recordings. These recordings will add context by documenting participants' behaviors and interactions throughout the study sessions. This will provide an insight into the learning environment, offering a detailed and nuanced perspective on how participants engage with the material and the SAMCares tool. A sub-sample of participants will be interviewed for follow-up on the study ( n = 20 from each group). These interviews will also be recorded for further qualitative analysis. The goal of this follow-up interview is to get additional information and experience from the participants. Some potential questions that will be asked during this interview will include but are not limited to:

1. How do the participants describe their experience using GenAI/ SAMCare compared to traditional learning methods?
2. In what ways did GenAI/ SAMCare help them to better understand and engage with the topics, and how did it fall short?
3. What specific features or functionalities of GenAI/ SAMCare were most useful or frustrating for them and why?
4. How did participants emotional and motivational states (e.g., confidence, anxiety, interest etc.) shifted during the use of GenAI/ SAMCare , and what factors contributed to these changes?

## Trial Sessions

Each participant will have to attend a total of two sessions. Each session will be approximately 1 hour long. They will be provided with study materials (see the Materials and Assessment section for details). One of the groups will have access to the SAMCares tool, while the other group will be limited to the study materials. At the beginning of the study, the participants will complete the pre-survey and an instructional video on how to use the tool will be shown to them before starting the session (only for the participants in the SAMCares group). The pre-survey forms will collect demographic information (age, gender, race, and other relevant variables, etc.) and presession information related to the study. The participants will also be shown a set of instructional videos on how to use SAMCares the tool effectively. This will include a demonstration of how to effectively ask the same question in different ways to get the best possible explanation that suits their needs (details in Importance of Question section). The participants will start the session by watching a 15-minute video on a specific topic (randomly assigned for each session) selected beforehand. Afterward, they will be provided with study materials and the SAMCares tool. The participants will be given the next 30 - 45 minutes to study the materials provided with or without the help of the SAMCares tool. At the end of the study session, the participants will be asked to complete a short assessment test based on the study topic to test their retention and understanding of the topic. A second session will be conducted on a separate day following the same procedure as the first session. Once participants have completed two sessions, they will be asked to complete a post-trial survey.

## Materials and Assessment

For this study, several topics are meticulously selected and designed to fit into a concise, 15-minute teaching segment. The aim is not to cover these topics exhaustively but to give participants an introductory idea of the topic and see how they perform on them. For each selected topic, an array of educational materials will be developed, comprising a short video lecture limited to 15 minutes focused on the main concepts and key points. Alongside the video, participants will have access to lecture notes and the presentation file for review and study purposes. Additional reading materials will be provided to deepen their understanding of the topic. A varied question bank designed for topic assessment will further reinforce the learning process, including multiple-choice, true/false, fill-in-the-gaps, and multiple-answer questions, along with descriptive questions where necessary.

A structured short assessment test will be implemented for quantitative assessment. These tests will be prepared based on the study topics, and the participants will be introduced earlier. The question formats will follow the same format as the question banks provided to the participants during the study sessions. These assessments will help evaluate the effectiveness of SAMCares in enhancing academic achievement. This will be used as the primary outcome of this study.

## Primary and Second Outcome

The study's primary outcome is to assess the effectiveness of GenAI tools like SAMCares , which can help improve educational performance. This assessment will be conducted by analyzing the variance in assessment scores, survey analysis, and the magnitude of learning gains between participants granted access to GenAI tools (i.e., SAMCares ) and those without. Through this multifaceted approach, the study aims to provide a clear and evidence-based understanding of how SAMCares and similar GenAI tools can contribute to advancing the quality of education in modern academic environments.

The secondary outcomes of the study will be the qualitative analysis of the participants' experiences with SAMCares , aiming to gain deeper insights into user satisfaction and areas for improvement. This analysis will primarily utilize data from post-study surveys, focusing on gathering feedback about the tool's design, usability, and overall effectiveness. Additionally, eye-tracking data will help evaluate participants' engagement and fatigue during study sessions. By analyzing patterns in gaze and focus, we can assess participants' cognitive load and attention span, which will inform modifications to make course materials more engaging and less taxing, thereby optimizing the learning experience for future cohorts.

## Data Repository

A secure data repository will be set up to store all the collected data during this trial. Only authorized personnel will have access to the repository to ensure the confidentiality and integrity of the data. This repository will be designed to comply with data protection regulations at SHSU and the State of Texas. A de-identified version of the data will also be created and stored in a second repository to share with the scientific community. A request form needs to be submitted to access the data.

## Documentation and Dissemination Policy

To ensure the transparency and reproducibility of this study, each phase of the study will be documented rigorously. Detailed logs of the study sessions will be kept, including dates, times, and any deviations from the standard protocol, along with comprehensive notes on the use of the SAMCares tool and the engagement of participants with the study materials. The trial results will be submitted to peer-reviewed scientific journals, conferences, symposia, and seminars for publication. During these dissemination activities, only aggregated data will be presented/published to ensure confidentiality, with a strict policy against disclosing any identifiable participant data.

## Building SAMCares

The proposed SAMCares utilizes Llama 2 (70B model) 10 , developed by Meta, as its backend model. Llama 2 can be accessed through the official Meta website 1 , and access to its weights and tokenizers is granted after completing the proper access request forms. This is done to ensure the responsible use of the model. Loading LLaMa 2 on a GPU requires requires 140 GB of memory (70 billion * 2 bytes). Thus, to load it in two smaller GPUs (2 NVIDIA RTX A5000; 8192 Cores; 24 GB Memory) in SAMCares , it was quantized to 4-bit precision. Thus, we will need 35 GB of memory (70 billion * 0.5 bytes). Anyone interested in using and creating other applications using LLaMa 2 is suggested to read the provided llama recipes by Meta. This work utilizes some of the suggestions provided by Meta to build SAMCares . At the end of the study, a version of SAMCares will be open-sourced in GitHub for the research community.

1 https://ai.meta.com/llama/

Figure 2: Retriever-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for SAMCares : Process flowchart for generating context-aware responses using vector embeddings and LLAMA 2 70B model.



## Implementing Retrieval-Augmented Generation in SAMCares

SAMCares incorporates a base implementation of the RAG system to augment the LLM's capabilities and minimize hallucinations. The RAG approach involves integrating contextually relevant external data sources with the LLM. This data can range from course-specific materials to a wider range of educational resources. RAG requires converting query documents into embedding before passing them into the LLaMa 2 model for querying. Figure 2 shows the overall process of how SAMCares RAG is developed and participants' queries are executed. For more details on RAG, the readers are suggested to read the work by Gao et al. 25 .

## Web-Based Interface for Demonstration

To make SAMCares accessible and user-friendly, a web-based interface has been developed. This interface allows participants to interact with SAMCares using their SHSU credentials, ensuring secure and personalized access. The interface is intuitive, with features such as a query input field, a response display area, and options to upload additional study materials. Figure 3 shows the interface developed for the usage of this study.

Figure 3: Interface of SAMCares: An Adaptive Learning Hub that the participants will be using.



## Importance of Question

Figure 4: Different levels of explanation from SAMCares for the same knowledge, the explanations can change depending on the question asked to the tool.



For instance, a query such as, 'How does the kidney filter blood? Please explain in detail!' would prompt SAMCares to provide a detailed yet understandable explanation tailored to the participant's level of understanding. If a participant requires a deeper understanding, they might ask, 'What is the role of reabsorption in maintaining electrolyte balance? Explain in detail.' In response, SAMCares would offer a more complex explanation involving the relevant physiological processes and implications. The strength of such a system lies in its capacity to dynamically adjust or adapt to the complexity of its content based on the form of the prompt/question asked. Thus supporting a wide range of educational needs and learning stages. This feature enhances user engagement and promotes comprehension and retention of information, which is essential for an effective learning experience. A guide on how to use prompting and ask questions to get the best possible answers will be provided to this tools users for effective use of the tool. We made SAMCares accessible to public for future research and usage. It can be accessed using the following link: SamCares : An Adaptive Learning Hub.

## Potential Limitations and Challenges

While designing this study we realized there are several potential limitations and challenges that needs to be addressed. One limitation is the potential infrastructure requirements for setting up and running tools like SAMCares , which may necessitate access to powerful compute servers or expensive API access through cloud computing platforms such as Amazon AWS or Google Cloud. This could create barriers to entry level adoption in resource-constrained academic setup. Another potential challenge will be the risk of misuse by users, who may utilize this tool to complete their homeworks/assignments. This could undermine the educational value of the tool and necessitate the development of safeguards to prevent such misuse. We are currently working on adding fail safes to the current deployed model to detect if it's been asked to complete a homework/assignment and restrict the output.

The biggest potential limitation of this study is that it will be a single trial. To validate the results we plan to assess in the future and the widespread adoption of the tool, it is essential to conduct repeated trials in different study sites to establish the tool's effectiveness and generalizability across various contexts. It is also crucial to investigate whether the results are affected by geographical locations, student types (minority students, underserved populations, etc.), or other factors that may impact the tool's performance. This is more so as the current study was conducted on engineering technology students, and it is essential to expand this study to other major departments to ensure the tool's broader applicability and relevance. Finally, implementing the tool for students with disabilities poses a significant challenge (from UI/UX design to tool element access). The tool's design and functionality must be adapted to accommodate their special needs, ensuring all students benefit from its capabilities. Addressing these limitations and challenges will be essential to realizing the full potential of this educational tool.

## Discussion

In this manuscript, we documented the protocol to assess the effectiveness and efficacy of GenAI tools in improving student's performance and learning outcomes. The proposed randomized controlled trial aims to provide important empirical evidence on whether emerging GenAI technologies can enhance the learning process when implemented thoughtfully. To the best of our knowledge, this is one of the early efforts to rigorously evaluate GenAI tools like SAMCares in an educational context. Our central hypothesis is that integrating tools like SAMCares with traditional study materials will result in improved learning outcomes and student satisfaction compared to traditional methods only 2 . If confirmed, it would signify the potential of these tools to transform the traditional teaching practices that are designed around student needs. However, careful guidance of AI implementation in education remains critical. The constraints of current GenAI models need careful assessment before wide deployment. There are outstanding questions surrounding optimizing these tools to avoid harm, embed ethical principles, and promote equitable access that require further research.

2 This protocol paper hasn't included any result analysis. We plan to publish the raw data and analyzed results in future publications.

Beyond the primary outcomes, our trial will also attempt to uncover insights into how students interact with GenAI tools compared to standard materials. Analyzing usage patterns, queries, focus from eye tracking information, and qualitative feedback will reveal opportunities to optimize these systems for even greater effectiveness. Our findings can inform the iterative development of GenAI tools innovations that drive more equitable, empowering, and learner-centric educational experiences.

We believe this technology can positively impact students in underrepresented groups in STEM fields, such as women, minorities, students from low-income backgrounds, and students with disabilities. By providing personalized support, the tool may enable these students to overcome traditional barriers to entry and success in engineering and technology disciplines and beyond. Moreover, the tool's interactive and engaging nature should foster increased motivation and interest in STEM subjects among students who may have previously felt disconnected from these fields due to the complexity of explanation and access to study materials. Making tools like this accessible to all types of students, including students with disabilities, may better connect the students in education.

## Conclusion

This pilot study aims to evaluate the effectiveness of GenAI tools like SAMCares as an assistive tool in education. Through its deployment, we will gain empirical data and experiential knowledge of the effect of such tools in attaining students' learning objectives. This study will also help identify the broader implications of GenAI in education. By assessing the system's performance across diverse learning scenarios and student populations, researchers and educators can better understand the strengths and limitations of AI as a teaching tool in a way that will provide a framework for future developments in the field, emphasizing the importance of accuracy, user interaction, and adaptability in educational technologies. As we progress, the continual evolution of advanced tools promises the potential to transform traditional learning paradigms and pave the way for a more informed, interactive, and intuitive educational future.

## INFORMATION &amp; COMMUNICATIONS TECHNOLOGY IN EDUCATION|RESEARCH ARTICLE





## Enabling secure and inclusive education for students with disabilities and ensuring data through machine learning

Bader Muteb Alsulami a , Abdullah Baihan b and Ahed Abugabah c



a College of Education, Majmaah University, Al Majma ' ah, Saudi Arabia; b Computer Science Department, Community College, King Saud University, Riyadh, Saudi Arabia; c College of Technological Innovation, Zayed University, Abu Dhabi Campus, UAE

## ABSTRACT

The COVID-19 pandemic precipitated an abrupt transition to online learning, impacting students with disabilities uniquely. This study examines the experiences of 62 such students in the new educational paradigm, employing a mixed-methods approach. Quantitative data were collected through surveys and questionnaires to assess privacy and security concerns arising from online learning tools. Qualitative insights were gathered via interviews and focus groups, revealing that while students appreciate the flexibility of online learning, they express a critical need for enhanced guidance and support. Neurodiverse students, in particular, emphasized the necessity of a secure online environment. Addressing these challenges, our research integrates blockchain and machine learning technologies to enhance biometric authentication. Specifically, the Highly Secure Blockchain-Based Compressive Sensing (HSBCS) system is proposed, ensuring data integrity and improving accessibility for Personal Records. Preliminary testing of the HSBCS system showed promising results, with an average accuracy rate of 95% in biometric authentication among visually impaired students. Moreover, participants reported a 30% increase in perceived security and ease of access to their Personal Records compared to traditional authentication methods. These findings underscore the potential of integrating advanced technologies to meet the unique educational needs of students with disabilities while enhancing data security and accessibility in online learning environments.

## 1. Introduction

The COVID-19 pandemic initiated universal transformation into online learning for educational institutions, thereby offering both opportunities and challenges to those schools around the globe. This transition is especially impactful for students with disabilities, who need specialized support and accommodations (Alamri &amp; Tyler-Wood, 2017) to navigate the higher education landscape. Identifying their distinct needs in these digital learning spaces and designing tools to support them will be essential if education is to truly become inclusive.This paper analyses the experiences of 62 students with disabilities in this unique time period, exploring their use of digital learning tools and what this means for privacy, security and educational support (Basham et al., 2015). Our study will use a mixed-methods design, including quantitative surveys and qualitative interviews/focus groups in order to understand the nuanced experiences that students with disabilities have when it comes to online learning.Considering the challenges presented above, our research advocates for an incorporation of cutting-edge technology in addressing them using machine learning and blockchain to design Highly Secure Blockchain-Based Compressive Sensing (HSBCS) system (Zhang et al., 2020). Personal Records are maintained by students with disabilities, ensuring the strength of biometric protection over access and authentication circuits as

## 2. Related work

The work of Bakia et al. (2012) was an early examination into the potential advantages and disadvantages for different student populations, including students with disabilities precipitated by online learning. In the increase of online learning during the pandemic, Denisova et al. (2020) highlighted the different types of issues faced by students and learners with disabilities, including not only accessibility concerns but also support mechanisms inadequacy as well -all indicative of a call for tailored distance learning interventions (Kent, 2016); in an extensive study on access and barriers to online education for people with disabilities identified systemic as well as technical challenges that make equitable assistive technology utilization not perfect. Built for a time when educational institutions are going online and adopting hybrid learning models, this is interesting study. Kotera et al. (2021) examined the emotional cost of online learning among students with disabilities, and they magnified feelings about loneliness and isolation which were already a problem during pandemic.

Purwati et al. (2022) conducted a systematic literature review on the challenges of online learning for students with disabilities during COVID-19; they concluded that technology dawned new opportunities yet emerged colossal barriers within engagement and accessibility as well though primarily due to accessibilities issues prevalent before COVID-19 hit. Tomaino et al. (2022) examined the feasibility and effectiveness of distance learning in students with severe developmental disabilities, whereby some found to be advantageous because it provide flexibility for these students however there were significant challenges due to absence of individualized support and face-to-face interaction. A study by Mohammed Ali (2021) eLearning among Students with Disabilities during COVID-19: Faculty Attitudes and Perceptions found that many faculty recognized the importance of accessibility but some at times do not have the resources or know-how to accommodate these students properly. Finally, Rutherford (2021) also underscored the value of faculty collaboratively in order to design online interventions and develop professional skills that will permit them to support their students with disabilities.

Cain and Fanshawe (2021) recently conducted an audit of the state of provision, or lack thereof, with accessible materials for students who have print disability in online learning environment that revealed substantial deficits overall and further illustrates how poor organisations are at complying to accessibility standards. In a similar vein, Rice and Carter (2015) have alerted how online educators can support students with disabilities but also suggested that the process must be more than merely ensuring to provide necessary compliance and access for these students.

Recent work such as Patel (2020) and Grimes et al. (2021), which examined the psychiatric implications of online learning for students with disabilities, and concluded that targeted support systems are certainly needed to deal with these difficulties. Jayapriya and Vinay (2023) addressed developments of equitable inclusive online learning using assistive technology or adaptive intervention as its key integrant in servicing students with disability. Gin et al. (2021) similarly investigated the impact of online transition on undergraduates with disabilities in large-enrollment STEM courses, and described additional challenges precipitated by moving many accommodations to a remote format. Specific learning difficulties and mental wellbeing challenges were revealed in this context during COVID-19 by Walters et al. (2022) among secondary school students perceptions of online teaching. Repetto et al. The examination of K-12 online learning research conducted by Repetto et al. (2018) and the unique experiences at-risk learners face provides glimpse into what challenges students with disabilities may also be facing in these environments Their work underscores the importance of focused support to prevent any further learning loss for these students.

Consequently, students with disabilities and their families who were already isolated found themselves even more so during the pandemic as Dickinson et al. (2023) noted in emphasizing social supports for education. The research points the need for full support systems to secure online learning success of this type of students

Niazov et al. (2022) identified for example the dilemma between online education, academic procrastination and an increase in stress as well a decrease in self-efficacy among students with learning disabilities.
