## Paper Source: Quality Evaluation of Integrated Distance Course for Special Education Based on Group Decision-Makin
============================================================

## RESEARCH

## Quality Evaluation of Integrated Distance Course for Special Education Based on Group Decision-Making Algorithm

Wei Wang 1,2 · Zhichao Wang 1 · Jianfei Wang 3

Accepted: 9 September 2024

© The Author(s), under exclusive licence to Springer Science+Business Media, LLC, part of Springer Nature 2025

## Abstract

Due to the significant differences among normal and disabled students, the classical evaluation method leads to a lack of consensus and balance in the course quality evaluation, resulting to lower quality and accuracy. To solve this problem, this paper proposes a group decision-making algorithm for the quality evaluation of integrated distance courses. Firstly, the proposed algorithm uses the K-means algorithm to cluster and collect the learning behavior data of students into predefined K clusters based on feature similarity. Secondly, the proposed algorithm constructs evaluation indicators of course quality from multiple perspectives under the support of multiple evaluation principles. Finally, all personal preferences of each decision-maker are brought together into one collective preference, which is used to rank the decision-making algorithms. In this paper, students are regarded as decision-makers, the integrated courses are regarded as decision-making options, the learning behaviors and preferences of students are regarded as attribute decision variables. Thus, the quality evaluation of integrated distance courses can be regarded as a group decision-making process. Experimental results show that the proposed method performs well in both consistency ratio and comprehensive score. The consistency ratio of our method always remains above 0.9, and the TOPSIS comprehensive score reaches a maximum of 0.937.

Keywords Group decision-making · Integrated distance course · Quality evaluation · Indicator system · Cluster · Collective preference

## 1  Introduction

Integrated distance education is a part of higher education model that combines traditional face-to-face teaching with modern distance education technology. This model makes use of the Internet, multimedia, virtual reality and other technologies to provide students with a flexible approach of learning, enabling them to be educated without the limitations of time and space. Today, with the social demand for diversified education and the pursuit of educational equity, the integration of special and general education has

* Zhichao Wang wangzc340@nenu.edu.cn

1 The Faculty of Education, Northeast Normal University, Changchun 130000, China

2 College of Arts, Changchun University, Changchun 130000, China

3 School Office, Changchun University, Changchun 130000, China

become an important direction of educational reform [1, 2]. However, the quality evaluation methods for special education in integrated education, especially the related distance education, have not yet been sufficiently focused on and researched.

The course quality evaluation can be applied to different groups of students, including special and general education. Such an evaluation method considers the requirements and characteristics of different kinds of students and ensures the fairness and effectiveness of the evaluation. The research on the evaluation methods of the quality of integrated education has important theoretical and practical significance [3-5].

Firstly, through the study of different course evaluation methods, it enables educational institutions to assess the teaching effect and student learning situation of the integrated courses accurately and provides a scientific basis for teaching; Second, the establishment of scientific and effective evaluation methods promotes the integration and interaction between special and general education, which promotes the overall development of education [6]. In addition, studying the course evaluation methods of the integration of special and general education promotes the rational allocation of educational resources, improves the quality of education and teaching, and contributes to the cultivation of inclusive and innovative talents [7]. Therefore, it has important practical significance and development prospects.





The current research on course evaluation methods is popular. Reference [8] proposes a course quality evaluation method based on the random forest algorithm. This method analyses the level of assisted teaching during college and completes the evaluation of the quality of multimedia-assisted teaching in these courses through the specification of the random forest algorithm and the optimization of the indicators of the evaluation of teaching quality. The random forest algorithm tends to overfitting when handling complex datasets, i.e., the model is so complex that it not only learns the patterns in the data but also learns the noise, leading to a decrease in the prediction performance. Reference [9] proposes a quality evaluation method for online courses based on FCE-AHP. Based on real cases of online courses, this method divides the factors influencing the quality of online courses into 4 first-level indicators, which are subdivided into 14 s-level indicators. The weight vectors of the evaluation indicators were determined based on the opinions of the experts of the 'Academic Affairs Committee', and the fuzzy evaluation members were calculated based on the results of the questionnaire survey of 2021 students. However, the weight vectors in the method were determined by the experts of the Academic Affairs Committee, and this operation may introduce subjectivity.  Experts' opinions may be influenced by personal experience, bias, or knowledge limitations, resulting in less than objective weight assignments. So, Reference [10] proposes an online course quality evaluation method based on the Consciousness Normal Cloud. This method analyses the cloud digital features of the Consciousness Normal Cloud and derives the distribution of the cloud digital features based on the weights of the course teaching evaluation, achieving a comprehensive evaluation of the online course quality. Cloud digital features are the core concepts in the cloud model, but they are not as intuitive as traditional statistical indicators, which easily leads to difficulties for educational decision-makers and course designers to accurately understand the meaning of the evaluation results. Reference [11] proposes a MOOC quality evaluation method based on the Grounded theory, which constructs an evaluation framework for the quality of the catechism and determines the overall goal of course teaching. Based on the selected MOOC quality evaluation indicators, the AHP method is used to determine the weight of each evaluation indicator and the fuzzy algorithm is used to evaluate the comprehensive evaluation indicators at each level. The quality evaluation results of English MOOC are derived based on Grounded theory. Grounded theory is a qualitative research method that relies on the researcher's subjective interpretation and analysis. When constructing the MOOC quality evaluation framework, the researcher's subjective judgment may affect the construction of the evaluation framework and the final evaluation results.

In order to solve the overfitting or strong subjectivity in existing evaluation methods, a group decision-making algorithm based evaluation system is introduced to construct a more objective, accurate, and easy to understand curriculum quality evaluation framework, which supports education decision-makers and curriculum designers to make more effective teaching improvement and resource allocation decisions. Through group decision-making, the influence of the subjective bias of a single decisionmaker is reduced. Mutual discussion and weighing among group members balance personal bias and improve the objectivity and fairness of evaluation. Moreover, the group decision-making algorithm can be adjusted and optimized according to different evaluation needs and situations. This flexibility makes the evaluation process adaptable to the changing educational environment and course characteristics, ensuring the effectiveness of the evaluation method.

Detailed technical route of the quality evaluation in this paper is as follows:

- (1) The K-means algorithm is used to cluster and analyze the learning behavior data of students in integrated courses. The algorithm divides the data into K predetermined clusters based on their feature similarity. Through the clustering process, the learning patterns and behavioral characteristics  of  different  student groups are identified, providing a data foundation for subsequent course quality evaluation.
- (2) Guided by the principle of multiple evaluations, a comprehensive and detailed curriculum quality evaluation index system is constructed from multiple perspec-

tives such as teaching content, student interaction, and resource availability. Ensure that the evaluation indicators cover all aspects of the course, in order to conduct a comprehensive and in-depth evaluation of the course quality.

- (3) Considering students as decision-makers, integrating courses as decision-making plans, and students' learning behaviors and preferences as attribute decision variables, transforming the evaluation of teaching quality into a group decision-making process. By collecting individual preferences of each decision maker and integrating them into collective preferences, sorting and selecting the best among different decisionmaking methods can be achieved. Iterative optimization method is used to continuously adjust and optimize the decision-making processing until obtaining the optimal quality evaluation.

## 2    Course quality evaluation of integrated higher education

Integrated education is an educational philosophy that aims to enable students with different levels of ability and learning needs to learn, interact, and grow together. Integrated education focuses not only on general education students but also includes students with a variety of special needs and disabilities. This educational model ensures that each student can fully participate in the learning process and succeed by providing personalized support and resources. The objectives of integrated education are to promote mutual understanding, respect, and cooperation among students, to develop the social skills and humanistic qualities of all students, to break down barriers and discrimination in traditional education, and to achieve full inclusion in education. Students learning in higher integrated courses have a certain time sequence, so according to the sequence of courses, the learning behavior data extraction process is diagrammed in the form of a flowchart. The flowchart is shown in Fig. 1.

Fig. 1 Learning behavior data extraction process

As can be observed in Fig. 1, the arrows are connected sequentially from left to right, revealing the sequence of course advancement. This indicates that the course advancement follows a certain sequence. Only when one course is completed will students move on to the next course and the associated data extraction process follow. The flowchart makes it possible to visually track the student's entire learning process from the beginning to the end of the course, with each step of the process visible briefly.

## 2.1    Filtering and classification of learning behavior data in integrated courses

When conducting big data attribute analysis of learning behaviors in the field of special education, the way of data collection, data visualization and interpretation, and data collection cycle and frequency are important factors affecting analysis accuracy. Adopting scientific and objective data collection, such as visual sensor technology and learning behavior monitoring systems, contributes to improving the accuracy of data collection. At the same time, reasonable data visualization and interpretation contribute to the understanding of students' learning behaviors and the development of personalized education programs. In addition, shorter collection cycles and higher collection frequency avoid missing and outdated data and improve the validity and accuracy of the data. The big data attributes of learning behaviors in the field of special education mainly involve the following aspects:





- (1) Students' personal information: including gender, age, family background, physical condition, type of disability, etc.
- (2) Academic performance data: including student grades, academic progress, course selection, etc.
- (3) Learning behavior data: including study time, learning styles, learning strategies, homework completion, etc.
- (4) Mental health data: including self-esteem, self-confidence, adaptability, etc.
- (5) Extracurricular activities data: including participation in social activities, social skills, hobbies, etc.
- (6) Educational resources utilization data: including library borrowing, laboratory usage, etc.

The data used is from higher university which has the integration for special and general education. The analyzed students' learning behaviors include online activities, homework submissions, discussion participation, interaction frequency, learning duration, and exam scores, etc., which are collected from online learning platforms, course management systems by user' tracking software. The data collection cycle covers 16 weeks, an entire semester, to ensure the continuity and integrity of the data. Data are collected once a week or two to observe the changes in students' learning behavior at continuous time points. To protect participants' privacy, all collected data are anonymized before analysis by removing identifiable personal information [12].

The collected learning behavior data contains a large amount of noise, such as accidental clicks, false operations, or system error records, which interferes with the analysis of students' real learning behavior. Also, the duplicate/highly similar data records during the long-term data collection processing increase the complexity of data processing and reduce analysis efficiency. Therefore, it is necessary to clean the learning behavior data.

This paper designs an improved filtering algorithm to narrow down the number of cluster candidates by determining whether a data cluster is a dynamic or static cluster and deciding whether it should be removed from the cluster candidate set. This improved filter can effectively remove noise and redundancy from the data by identifying and deleting dynamic or static clusters, which improves the overall quality of the data. Reduction of the cluster candidates' number can reduce the complexity of subsequent data analysis, accelerate data processing speed, and improve analysis efficiency; the deletion of inconsistent data clusters helps to improve the accuracy of data analysis, making the analysis results more similar to students' real learning behavior.

The algorithmic flow of the improved filtering algorithm is as following Algorithm 1.

## Algorithm 1



At each stage of the iteration, Algorithm 1 determines the closest cluster center Ci to n ' s center and checks whether each member in n . SC ∕ { Ci } needs to be clipped out. Step 6 mainly determines the nearest neighbor cluster center Ci of n and checks whether each member in n . SCactive needs to be clipped out. It means that, at each stage of iteration, Step 6 reduces the calculation complexity by about ∑ | n . SC | ∕ ∑ | | n . SCactive | | times. Here, n . SC and n . SCactive are  obtained by clipping some members from the cluster center set and the active cluster center, respectively. When | n . SC | and | | n . SCactive | | are proportional to | n . SC | and | | n . SCactive | | respectively, that is, ( ∑ | n . SC | ∕ ∑ | | n . SCactive | | ) ≈ ( | CC | ∕ | | CCactive | | ) , we complete the data cleaning.

Because the students of integrated courses have big differences, the K-means algorithm is used to classify the learning behavior data, which is an unsupervised learning algorithm. So, K-means algorithm is suitable for analyzing and classifying the learning behavior data of the students because it  doesn't need pre-labeled training [13]. Moreover, the K-means algorithm is simple and easy to implement, computationally efficient, and quickly processes large amounts of data and produces better clustering results. Therefore, when exploring and evaluating the course quality of special education in higher integrated education, the K-means algorithm is conducive to effectively analyzing the learning behavior data of students with large differences.

The K-means algorithm requires a dataset with many data samples and specific values for the number of clusters [14]. Next, use randomly selected samples as the initial cluster centers, and calculate the distance between the rest of the samples and the initial center to measure which sample is more similar to the initial center. Then place the sample in the cluster class where the centers are located. We continue the calculation based on the centers of the changing cluster classes and stop until the error is reduced to a minimum. Clustering calculation of learning behavior data of higher integrated education course using K-means algorithm is started given a sample set of K :

Table 1 Indicator system for quality evaluation of integrated course

| Teaching Methods and Strategies             | To evaluate the diversity and effectiveness of teaching methods and strategies, including teachers' teaching skills, use of teaching aids, and interactive teaching.                                    |
| Student Learning Performance                | To evaluate the effectiveness of students' knowledge acquisition, skills enhancement, cognitive development, and potential development in the course.                                                   |
| Course Evaluation and Adjustment            | To evaluate the scientific and accuracy of course assessment methods, and the timely feedback and adjustment of the assessment results.                                                                 |
| Teaching Resources Support                  | To assess the adequacy and effectiveness of teaching resources, including the availability of teaching resources such as libraries, laboratories, and special equipment.                                |
| Faculty Development                         | To evaluate the level of educational background, professional knowledge, and teaching ability of teachers, as well as the support and mechanism for their continuing professional development.          |
| Student Participation and Satisfaction [19] | To evaluate the motivation and satisfaction of students in participating in the course, including class- room participation and extra-curricular activities participation.                              |
| Social Recognition and Effectiveness        | To evaluate the extent to which the course facilitates students' future entry into the workplace, social participation, and social adaptability.                                                        |



where n denotes the sample capacity, C denotes the set attributes of the learning behavior data sample, and x and j denote that the x -th data in the set K has j attributes.

After selecting the learning behavior data sample, the cluster class center is determined:

Based on the K-means algorithm, it quickly and efficiently classifies the learning behavior data of students. Based on the similarity merit to determine which cluster class a learning behavior belongs to, and minimize the classification error, provide an effective data base for the subsequent course quality evaluation.

## 2.2    Indicator system for courses quality evaluation

After completing the data collection of learning behavior in courses, the quality evaluation indicator system is constructed. Firstly, set the principles of higher integrated education course quality evaluation. The specifics are as follows:

## (1) Individualization

Special education involves students with different types and degrees of disabilities, who have differences in learning ability, communication style, cognitive level, and so on. Therefore, the evaluation system should develop personalized evaluation criteria and methods based on the personal differences of the students. This may involve a comprehensive evaluation based on the type and degree of disability, understanding the student's needs and potential, and providing him/her with appropriate support and services.

- (2) Comprehensiveness

Evaluation of special education courses needs to take into consideration all aspects of the course. This includes evaluating the reasonableness and clarity of the course objectives, e.g. whether they are in line with the needs in the field of special education; evaluating the design of the course content, e.g. whether it adequately meets the learning needs of the students; and evaluating the diversity and effectiveness of the teaching methodology, e.g. whether teaching strategies suitable for different students have been adopted.

- (3) Operability

Evaluation indicators should be operable, i.e. they can be collected and measured objectively and are easy to evaluate implementation and improvement measures. This means that the evaluation indicators should be clear, measurable, and able to quantitatively or qualitatively reflect the quality and effectiveness of the special education courses.

- (4) Sustainability

Evaluation of courses in special education should be an ongoing process that continues throughout the course. The evaluation process should include data collection, analysis of results and feedback, and provide support for continuous improvement of the courses. This facilitates early identification of problem-solving and sustained improvement in the quality of teaching and learning.

## (5) Participation

Evaluation process should encourage the participation of teachers, students, parents, and other relevant stakeholders. Their comments and suggestions will provide valuable information to help evaluators fully understand and assess the quality and effectiveness of the special education courses [15]. Through cooperation and communication with these stakeholders, the objectivity and comprehensiveness of the evaluation are enhanced to ensure their consensus on the course.

- (6) Transparency

Evaluation criteria and methods should be open and transparent, and all relevant parties should learn about the evaluation criteria and process. This ensures the fairness and credibility of the evaluation and enhances the participation of the evaluation. Transparency also improves teachers' and students' understanding and acceptance of evaluation and promotes their active participation and feedback [16].

The indicator system of course quality evaluation is an important tool for measuring the quality and effectiveness of courses in special education. This study constructs the indicator system of course quality evaluation referring to the existing literatures. Li et al. [17] constructed the indicator system from aspects of students' performance, sense of interest, sensibility, etc. Xu et al. [18] constructed the indicator system from the aspects of teaching course design, teaching method, teaching status, and learning effect.

Based on existing research and in-depth consideration of the course features, the indicator system is constructed by combining multiple dimensions such as learning objectives and standards, outcomes, process and methods, course evaluation and adjustment, learning resource support, faculty strength and development, student participation and satisfaction, social acceptance and effect. All these dimensions are used to evaluate whether the course design is scientific and reasonable, the teaching methods are diverse and effective, the student learning outcomes are significant, the course evaluation is timely and accurate, the teaching resources are sufficient and effective, the teaching staff is strong and continuously developing, the student's participation is active and satisfactory, the course has a positive impact on the students' future adaptability. Such a comprehensive evaluation provides a scientific basis for quality enhancement and continual improvement of courses. Detailed indicators are shown in Table 1.

In  the  evaluation  indicator  system,  a  comprehensive framework has been constructed, aiming to evaluate the effectiveness and quality of courses from multiple dimensions. It provides a structured evaluation framework and uniform standards to the group decision-making algorithm, ensuring the scientific consistency of the evaluation process. Additionally, it facilitates the integration of data from different evaluators and enhances the objectivity and fairness of the following decision-making. Through this indicator system, the group decision-making algorithm conducts multidimensional in-depth analysis, optimizes the decision-making process, and supports the continuous improvement of course quality.

## 2.3    Course quality evaluation based on group decision-making algorithms

With the evaluation indicator system, the group decisionmaking algorithm is constructed to evaluate the quality of these courses, effectively enhancing the scientific and effective evaluation quality. In the evaluation, different subjects and consensus mechanisms have a profound impact on the effectiveness; diverse perspectives and expertise enhance the evaluation participation and representativeness, and ensure the alignment of courses with industry requirement; consensus mechanism improves the quality of decision-making, enhances the acceptance of evaluation results, encourages continuous improvement, and reduces personal bias by promoting communication and negotiation among subjects. These indicators together ensure the comprehensiveness, fairness, and practicality of the evaluation results, thereby promoting the continuous improvement of the courses quality for integrated distance education.

Group decision-making algorithm brings together the opinions and experiences of multiple evaluators, avoiding the subjectivity and bias of a single evaluator, and facilitating the comprehensive consideration of different people's opinions on course quality. The group decision-making algorithm promotes the democratic participation of teachers, students, parents, and other relevant people, increasing the fairness and credibility of the evaluation. Moreover, this algorithm promotes knowledge sharing and exchange, incorporating the expertise and experience of all parties to provide more accurate and comprehensive results for the evaluation. Most importantly, the group decision-making algorithm produces more accurate evaluation results by integrating the opinions and decisions of all parties, providing scientific support for the improvement and development of higher integrated courses.

In group decision-making theory, group decision-making is the process of bringing together the individual preferences of each decision-maker into collective preferences, so that the decision-making group ranks each decision-making algorithm as optimal. If students are regarded as decision-makers, higher integrated courses as decision-making solutions, and students' learning behaviors and preferences as attributed decision-making variables, then according to the description of the problem, the teaching quality evaluation of higher integrated courses is regarded as a group decision-making process.

In the process of higher integrated education, different learning behavior data of students are acquired with different scales, and to avoid the course quality evaluation being affected by different scales, learning behavior data need  to  be  normalized  [20,  21].  Let X 1 i , j , X 2 i , j , … , X h i , j denotes the observation data of students' learning behavior. Then the standardized processing formula for student learning behavior data is expressed as:



Where:





The more students are involved in the teaching and learning process of higher integrated courses, the better the learning performance achieved. Similarly, different learning behaviors of students have different effects on the course quality evaluation. Therefore, we set the weights of students so that students with better performance of learning behaviors, are weighted higher importance of evaluation results in group decision-making.

Each student's quality evaluation of higher integrated courses needs to be considered comprehensively to see whether his evaluation results reach a coherent consensus with other students. Only when students reach a coherent consensus on the decision-making can the quality of higher integrated courses be evaluated objectively, fairly, and accurately [22]. The consensus index is calculated from three levels: students' learning behavior, educational courses, and students' decision-making.

## 3    Experimental validation

To validate the effectiveness of the proposed quality courses evaluation method based on a group decision-making algorithm for higher integrated education, comparative experiments are carried out.

## 3.1    Sample data

The sample data used in this experiment came from an institution that includes special education in higher integrated education, and the learning behavior data came from a semester of a 16-week higher integrated education course with 100 students. The detailed types of samples are as follows:

Type  1:  Student  engagement  data:  student  learning behavior is evaluated by observing student engagement in the classroom. The number of times students ask questions, the accuracy and depth of their answers, and the frequency of their participation in group discussions or teamwork are recorded.

Type 2: Learning performance data: Learning performance is evaluated through exams, assignments, projects, and so on. These data reflect the level of performance of students in terms of the knowledge, skills, and abilities they have learned in the course.

Type 3: Feedback data: teachers regularly collect feedback from students on the content and methods of teaching. It is obtained through verbal feedback, questionnaires, or grading.

Type 4: Learning process data: learning time data is obtained by students completing classroom activity sheets or recording the amount of time students spend learning in the classroom. This data includes the time students spend studying independently within the classroom as well as the time they spend discussing and collaborating with their classmates. In addition, observing how often students use the learning resources provided in the classroom (e.g., textbooks, courseware, lab equipment, etc.) is data. Recording the amount of time students spend completing and delivering assignments, enables this research to get the situation of how well students are completing their assignments as well.

Type 5: Learning Interaction Data: it focuses on the interaction  and  communication  between  students, including the number of posts and replies in online discussion forums, the frequency of private message exchanges, and real-time interactions in virtual learning environments. These reflect the collaboration, social skills, and social learning behaviors among students.

Fig. 3 Clustering effect of learning behavior data by different methods



Fig. 4 Consistency ratio results



Type 6: Technology usage data: the technology usage habits have a significant impact on students' learning experience and effectiveness, including the frequency of logging into the learning platform, the types of devices used (such as computers, tablets, and mobile phones), the stability of network connections, and familiarity with the platform functions. By analyzing these data, we can understand students' adaptability and utilization efficiency of technical tools, thereby optimizing technical support and services.

To observe the distribution of learning behavior data more intuitively, this experiment plots data points in a twodimensional coordinate space, where X and Y indicate the size of the storage space for learning behavior data. The diagram of the distribution of learning behavior data under the two-dimensional coordinate space X and Y is shown in Fig. 2.

As can be seen in Fig. 2, the six types of data points are evenly distributed throughout the two-dimensional coordinate space, with no obvious aggregation or discrete trends. Different types of data points are mixed, and there is no obvious classification boundary. Therefore, to effectively use learning behavior data, it is necessary to accurately classify different types of learning behavior data into clusters.

In order to ensure the reproducibility of the experiment, the K-means algorithm clusters the dataset with following parameters: the number of clusters was set to 6; the initial clustering centers are randomly selected to reduce the possible differences in clustering results; Euclidean distance is the measurement; the maximum number of iterations is set to 100; the stopping condition is that the change in the cluster center is less than 0.0001, random seed is set to 42.

## 3.2    Experimental solution and indicators

This experiment takes learning behavior data clustering effect, consistency ratio, and TOPSIS overall score as the test indicators. We compare and verify our method with the method of reference [8] and the method of reference [9].

Learning behavior data clustering effect: assess whether the clustering algorithm classifies learners with similar learning behaviors into the same class. Within classes, the differences between learners should be as small as possible.

Consistency ratio: the consistency ratio is an indicator derived by comparing the consistency factor to a random consistency indicator. It provides a quantitative way of evaluating the consistency of the comparisons provided by the decision maker in the judgment matrix.

TOPSIS Overall Score: The overall score derived from the TOPSIS (Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution) allows for a comprehensive consideration of the strengths and weaknesses of group decisionmaking algorithms as an overall indicator for evaluating performance.

## 3.3    Experimental results analysis

## 3.3.1    Clustering effect of learning behavior data

Analyzing learning behavior data through clustering helps identify and understand the learning characteristics, tendencies, and habits of different student groups. This helps educators better understand student groups and provides references for personalized teaching and tutoring. Testing learning behavior data through clustering is used to evaluate the quality of higher integrated courses and to understand the learning and feedback of different groups. This helps to identify strengths and room for improvement in course design and enhance the quality of teaching and learning. The clustering effect of learning behavior data by different methods are shown in Fig. 3.

By analyzing Fig. 3, it is found that our method outperforms the methods in reference [8] and reference [9] in terms of the clustering effect of learning behavior data. Specifically, our method clusters learning behavior data more accurately, showing a better clustering effect. This indicates that our method has higher clustering accuracy and effectiveness in dealing with learning behavior data, which is superior to existing methods. The comprehensive graphical results not only confirm this method has excellent performance in learning behavior data clustering but also highlight its potential and advantages in the course quality evaluation of higher integrated education, which provides useful references and insights for further research and practice.



The K-means algorithm allocates data to predetermined K clusters through an iterative optimization process for the similarity between them. Because of the efficiency and simplicity, K-means can quickly and effectively group data when processing large-scale learning behavior data. In this article, precise feature selection and calculation make the data are most similar within each cluster, while most different between clusters. This precise feature selection and computation improve the accuracy of clustering, making the proposed method perform well in learning behavioral data clustering.

## 3.3.2    Consistency ratio

The consistency ratio test is used to evaluate the consistency of group decision-making algorithms in the course quality evaluation of higher integrated education. The consistency ratio test helps to determine whether the decision makers are logically consistent in evaluating to avoid subjective bias and inconsistency. The consistency ratio test measures the degree of consistency between factors or indicators in a group decision-making algorithm. When the consistency ratio is low, there may be inconsistent factors in the decision-making process that need to be further optimized and adjusted to improve the reliability of the decision-making. The results of the consistency ratio test for the above three methods are shown in Fig. 4.

According to the analysis results shown in Fig. 4, it is clearly that our method performs significantly better in terms of consistency ratios than the methods mentioned in reference [8] and reference [9]. Specifically, the consistency ratios of the methods in this paper are generally higher than 0.9, showing a high level of consistency. This indicates that our method performs well in the consistency of evaluation results, and has higher reliability and stability compared with other methods. The comprehensive data shown in the graph not only verifies the excellent performance of this paper's method in terms of consistency ratio but also highlights its significant advantages in the evaluation of the course quality of higher integrated education, which provides substantial support and guidance for further exploring the application and promotion of the method.

The proposed method adopts the evaluation based on group decision-making, which can integrate the opinions of multiple decision makers and gather individual preferences into collective preferences through certain rules and procedures. This improves the consistency and rationality of decision-making, ensures the consistency of evaluation results, and makes the evaluation between different decision-makers more coordinated and unified.

## 3.3.3    TOPSIS comprehensive scoring

The TOPSIS makes a comprehensive evaluation of multiple evaluation indicators and gets a comprehensive score by calculating the weights and scores among the indicators. In the course quality evaluation of higher integrated education, several aspects of indicators may be involved, and TOPSIS helps to take these indicators into account in a comprehensive way and provide comprehensive evaluation results. The results of the TOPSIS overall scores of the three methods are shown in Table 3.

Analyzing the experimental data in Table 3, the advantage of our method over other methods in terms of TOPSIS overall score is visualized. Taking test number 3 as an example, the score of our method is 0.917, compared with 0.658 and 0.543 for the methods of reference [8] and reference [9], respectively. This indicates that our method achieves significantly higher overall scores under this test, which shows that it is more capable of evaluating and consistently performing well across multiple test samples.

This is because the proposed method adopts the principle of multiple evaluations to comprehensively consider course quality from multiple perspectives when constructing the evaluation indicators. This multi-view evaluation reduces the bias from single-view evaluation, improves the comprehensiveness and accuracy of the evaluation results, and thus achieves higher scores in the TOPSIS comprehensive score.

## 4    Conclusion

This paper proposed a course quality evaluation of higher integrated education based on the group decision-making algorithm, which demonstrates significant advantages in the consistency ratio test and the TOPSIS overall score test. The consistency test ensures that the evaluation is fair and objective, the TOPSIS test considers multiple indicators comprehensively. Therefore, this method has the advantages of accuracy, stability, and comprehensiveness in course quality evaluation, which provides a scientific decision-making basis for higher education administrators. It promotes the continuous improvement of higher education quality.

The novelty of the proposed method is as follows:

- (1) Novel application of group decision-making

This article constructs a group decision-making process by using students as decision makers, integrated courses as decision plans, and students' learning as decision attributions. It is more democratic and objective, better reflecting the common opinions and preferences of student groups, thereby improving the consistency and accuracy of evaluation decisions.

- (2) Effectiveness of K-means algorithm

Experimental results show that the K-means algorithm has high clustering accuracy and effectiveness in processing learning behavior data. Through cluster analysis, this method can effectively identify the learning different student groups, providing strong data support for personalized teaching and tutoring.

- (3) Better TOPSIS and Consistency

Experimental results show that the proposed method performs well in TOPSIS overall scoring and consistency ratio. This demonstrates the role of the mult-view evaluation and preferences integration, which more accurately reflect various aspects of course quality and avoid subjective bias and inconsistency.

However, further research is still needed to improve the method in the future. We will explore empirical applications and comparisons with other evaluation methods, to promote the effectiveness and sustainable development of the method in practice.

## Paper Source: Service Selection Using an Ensemble Meta-Learning Classifier for Students with Disabilities

Article

## Service Selection Using an Ensemble Meta-Learning Classifier for Students with Disabilities





Citation: Namoun, A.; Humayun, M.A.; BenRhouma, O.; Hussein, B.R.; Tufail, A.; Alshanqiti, A.; Nawaz, W. Service Selection Using an Ensemble Meta-Learning Classifier for Students with Disabilities. Multimodal Technol. Interact. 2023 , 7 , 42. https://doi.org/ 10.3390/mti7050042

Received: 17 March 2023

Revised: 17 April 2023

Accepted: 19 April 2023

Published: 23 April 2023



Copyright: © 2023 by the authors. Licensee MDPI, Basel, Switzerland. This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (https:// creativecommons.org/licenses/by/ 4.0/).



Abdallah Namoun 1, * , Mohammad Ali Humayun 2 , Oussama BenRhouma 1 , Burhan Rashid Hussein 3 , Ali Tufail 4 , Abdullah Alshanqiti 1 and Waqas Nawaz 1



- 1 Faculty of Computer and Information Systems, Islamic University of Madinah, Madinah 42351, Saudi Arabia
- 2 Department of Computer Science, Information Technology University, Lahore, Pakistan
- 3 Rennes University, Inria, Inserm IRISA UMR 6074, Empenn, ERL U 1228 Rennes, France
- 4 School of Digital Science, Universiti Brunei Darussalam, Tungku Link, Gadong BE1410, Brunei
* Correspondence: a.namoun@iu.edu.sa

Abstract: Students with special needs should be empowered to use assistive technologies and services that suit their individual circumstances and environments to maximize their learning attainment. Fortunately, modern distributed computing paradigms, such as the Internet of Things (IoT), cloud computing, and mobile computing, provide ample opportunities to create and offer a multitude of digital assistive services and devices for people with disabilities. However, choosing the appropriate services from a pool of competing services while satisfying the unique requirements of disabled learners remains a challenging research endeavor. In this article, we propose an ensemble metalearning model that ranks and selects the best IoT services while considering the diverse needs of disabled students within the educational context. We train and test our deep ensemble meta-learning model using two synthetically generated assistive services datasets. The first dataset incorporates 50,000 records representing the possible use of 12 learning activities, fulfilled by 60 distinct assistive services. The second dataset includes a range of 120,000 service ratings of seven quality features, including response, availability, successibility, latency, cost, quality of service, and accessibility. Our deep learning model uses an ensemble of multiple input learners fused using a meta-classification network shared by all the outputs representing individual assistive services. The model achieves significantly better results than traditional machine learning models (i.e., support vector machine and random forest) and a simple feed-forward neural network model without the ensemble technique. Furthermore, we extended our model to utilize the accessibility rating of services to suggest appropriate educational services for disabled learners. The empirical results show the acceptability of our assistive service recommender for learners with disabilities.

Keywords: service selection; disabled students; learners with disabilities; quality of service; ensemble method; deep learning; assistive technology; Internet of Things

## 1. Introduction

With the fast evolution of digital technologies and the development of convenient and affordable gadgets, humans' reliance on digital means to access various services is increasing at an alarming rate. Teaching and learning are no exception, where innovative teaching methodologies are progressively relying on technological tools [1-3]. The ever-increasing online learning content and readily available interactive tools have made learning significantly easier and widely accessible. In addition, heavy reliance on technological gadgets has made the new generation of students more inclined towards digital tools as compared to printed books and handwriting, as they are more comfortable using technology for learning. However, this growing dependence on technology in the education sector might potentially leave behind students with disabilities. Disabled learners might feel challenged using gadgets and tools that do not support accessibility and inclusion; for example, it is more difficult for blind or visually impaired students to benefit effectively from online video lectures in their standard form (i.e., without video-audio descriptions and accessibility features). Hence, a parallel effort is required to develop assistive technologies that can make use of online learning content more conveniently for all forms of disabilities. Disabled students may have various impairments, such as visual, hearing, or physical mobility issues, which hinder their learning activities.



Recently, Internet of Things (IoT)-based automated environments have been developed to assist people with disabilities in different areas [4-6]. Some assistive tools and devices have been proposed to empower learning for students with disabilities [7-10]. However, no standard approach integrates all such services to benefit a wide range of disabilities. Furthermore, little research has focused on the analysis of behaviors of students with disabilities in terms of their preferences for certain services. Such analysis can be utilized by integrating platforms where relevant services can be systematically recommended to disabled users based on service ratings of similar users in comparable conditions.

This research develops an ensemble meta-learning approach that incorporates the learning requirements of students with disabilities and suggests appropriate IoT services that can assist disabled learners based on the nature of their disabilities and physical environments. Our model predicts how likely it is that students will use certain services depending on the type of disability and learning conditions. Moreover, the deep learning model predicts the accessibility ratings other users are likely to assign to the available IoT services. The suggested model can easily be integrated with intelligent recommendation systems to support teaching and learning in online learning environments that target students with disabilities. Learning recommendation systems can use the prediction of service usage and its ratings. In a nutshell, our main research contributions are summarized as follows:

- (1) Two IoT datasets are generated containing diverse user attributes, assistive services for disabled learners, and user ratings. The lack of real datasets for educational scenarios motivated us to create synthetic datasets for students with disabilities. These datasets can be easily refined and extended for other researchers in the same field.
- (2) An ensemble meta-learning prediction model is developed to recommend learning services for disabled students. The predictions are based on the user's disabilities type and various environmental attributes. Our model capitalizes on two key concepts, namely meta-learning and ensemble methods, to improve service recommendations.
- (3) The proposed model predicts the ratings that disabled students are likely to assign to IoT services based on a myriad of features (i.e., response time, availability, throughput, successiblity, reliability, and latency).

The rest of the paper is organized into seven sections. Section 2 presents the recent research efforts regarding the application of the Internet of Things for the learning of students with disabilities. Section 3 describes the synthetic datasets developed for this research. Section 4 explains the experimental setup and the ensemble classification model proposed in this paper. Section 5 analyzes the results achieved using the proposed model. Section 6 discusses the significance of our ensemble model and its predictions. Finally, Section 7 concludes the research with the main findings and defines directions for future research.

## 2. Challenges of Learning for Students with Disabilities

The World Health Organization (WHO) estimates that 1.3 billion people in the world experience a significant disability [11] and a whopping 13% of the civilian population in the U.S.A. has some disability [12]. As many as 65 million school-age children suffer from a form of disability [13]. According to N.C.E.S., approximately 7.2 million students aged between 3 and 21 in the U.S.A. alone received accessibility services in 2020-2021 [13]. These statistics show that there is a dire need to help people with disabilities in performing various tasks. Disability can take various forms, for example, vision, hearing, thinking, learning, movement, mental health, remembering, communicating, and social relationships [14]. Smart technologies and services can be vital means of providing the required assistance Multimodal Technol. Interact.

2023

,

7

to people with special needs. In the context of our paper, support is offered to disabled students through the provision of an immersive teaching and learning environment.

Students with disabilities may face significant challenges in their learning journey due to a lack of assistive services. Such challenges can be categorized as general challenges that might have an impact on disabled students regardless of the disability type and specific challenges that are related to the type of disability a student might have. For example, social, emotional, and behavioral challenges apply to all students with disabilities [15], whereas the teaching of Braille and other related tools are only related to visually impaired students [16].

Assistive technologies and services play an influential role in helping learners with disabilities by creating an inclusive environment that facilitates the attainment of the required knowledge and skills in the desired domain [1]. Several studies were conducted to demonstrate the support that the Internet of Things (IoT) can provide to students with disabilities [7,17-26]. Figure 1 depicts the growing market size of assistive technologies developed to satisfy the wants of the disabled and elderly. The figure represents the future trends and the potential the technology holds to assist disabled users in various ways.

The wide range of IoT services and their variable quality make their selection, depending on user requirements and the context of use, a worthwhile challenge to tackle. Students with special needs have different requirements and constraints compared to fully abled students. However, most of the previous works in the service selection territory concentrated on addressing the needs of able-bodied learners. For instance, recent surveys on the use of computational intelligence in service composition and prediction of student performance [27,28] confirm the lack of intelligent solutions dedicated to serving disabled students. Moreover, the competing nature of services and their quality features (e.g., availability, latency, compatibility, etc.) require a robust machine-learning model that fuses assistive services to fulfill the learning goals of disabled learners in an optimal manner. , x FOR PEER REVIEW 4  of  18

Figure 1. Size of the disabled and elderly assistive technologies market size between 2021 and 2030 (adopted from [29].) Figure 1. Size of the disabled and elderly assistive technologies market size between 2021 and 2030 (adopted from [29]).



The following is a summary of the major learning challenges students with disabilities face. The following is a summary of the major learning challenges students with disabilities face.

1. General challenges: these type of challenges apply to disabled students regardless of the type of disability. These include social, emotional, and behavioral difficulties that hinder the use of educational technologies. These challenges can be an artifact 1. General challenges: these types of challenges apply to disabled students regardless of the kind of disability. These include social, emotional, and behavioral difficulties that hinder the use of educational technologies. These challenges can be an artifact of the

of the contexts of user interaction, not the individuals themselves. For instance, a

context where user privacy is infringed upon is often impeding to the user experi-

ence and poses social restrictions, causing frustration and poor experiences. This

needs to be considered where providing a solution in the wrong context might lead contexts of user interaction, not the individuals themselves. For instance, a context where user privacy is infringed upon is often impeding to the user experience and poses social restrictions, causing frustration and poor experiences. This needs to be considered where providing a solution in the wrong context might lead to the failure of the solution itself.

For example, disabled students who find it hard to process information might suffer from low self-esteem and social withdrawal [15]. Additionally, barring students with disabilities from participating in school-related activities negatively impacts the psychological and social well-being of these students [16].

2. Type-specific challenges: These types of challenges are related to the specific type of user impairment. For instance, the challenges a student with hearing impairments will face differ from those of a student with visual impairments.
3. Service selection challenges: other challenges pertain to the assistive technology itself. This issue is more evident when several competing services are available from a wide range of sources, such as web services, mobile services, IoT services, cloud services, etc. It is not easy to automatically select the most appropriate services for students with disabilities while fulfilling their requirements and interaction context.

These challenges demonstrate the overarching need to develop intelligent frameworks that can search and select the most appropriate IoT services to accomplish the needs of a particular disabled student depending on their disability and learning context.

## 3. Related Work

## 3.1. Use of Internet of Things in Education

Considerable research has been conducted in the Internet of Things (IoT) domain, producing numerous applications to assist humans in performing routine tasks in their life [25]. IoT application areas span from smart cities to health services, waste management, traffic control, water and utility services, medical healthcare applications, and agriculture applications. In [23], the authors discussed the significance of IoT in collecting data for research via remote sensing from challenging environments, such as space. The applications of IoT are analyzed in hospitals for medication and treatment [21]. Equally, IoT-based environments have specifically helped people with disabilities in various ways. However, our study focuses on teaching and learning for students with special needs.

Interestingly, a handful of IoT initiatives are suggested in the literature to enhance the learning of disabled students, such as [7,9,17-20,22,24,26,30]. Heng et al. [20] suggested the use of IoT devices to help students with disabilities in their learning process. In [22], Hengeveld et al. developed a telepresence system for students along with a feature to monitor the rhythm and tone of teachers to analyze the requirements for student engagement. Similarly, Kassab et al. [17] summarized several studies that would help people with different disabilities, such as visual, hearing, and physical disabilities, to use IoT platforms. Kiryakova et al. [26] illustrated the value of interactive technology-based learning environments for toddlers. They proposed an environment where multiple digital interactive tools are utilized to improve language learning by toddlers with disabilities. The tools are designed to teach sign language to children with hearing impairments. They tested their model for four weeks and displayed significant improvements in the learning capabilities of students. Lenz et al. [19] developed an augmented reality software that identifies the name of the objects using computer vision and tags them with audio and text to teach students with impaired learning and comprehension capabilities. In another study [7], McRae et al. proposed an RFID-equipped library environment so that students with visual impairments can locate the books they search for with the help of RFID tags.

Moreover, Noor et al. [24] reviewed the benefits of wireless IoT devices in assisting the education of students with disabilities. They discussed the applications and challenges of using IoT devices in learning environments for disabled students. Similarly, Ullah et al. [18] highlighted the influence of IoT on teaching and learning by directly engaging with students in real-time. They conducted a survey and found that 84% of the sample population indicated that technology helped them in their courses. Wambuaa et al. [9] presented a systematic literature review of the applications of IoT and models developed for educating disabled students. Services such as indoor thermal comforters and wheelchair systems were used for physical disabilities. In another study [30], Bluetooth beacon-based navigation was used to develop assistive walking for students with visual impairments. Real-time visual communication aids were developed for students with hearing impairments. An et al. [31] assembled wireless alerts, smart bracelets, and gloves for sign language recognition.

Lee, H. [32] discussed the trends and challenges of integrating IoT and assistive technologies in the lives of people with disabilities. The lack of clear guidelines on how to incorporate IoT into education remains a key challenge. In another study [33], Mishra et al. reviewed the importance of IoT in higher education to enhance interactive learning and teaching. They suggested that IoT could also assist disabled students in their learning. In a review article [34], Felicia et al. surveyed the Internet of Educational Things (aka IoEdT), emphasizing that IoEdT holds the future where they expect a better learning environment and learning processes that could benefit students with physical disabilities. In another review study [35], Algozani et al. discussed the impact of IoT on higher education, arguing that IoT overcomes the disadvantages of traditional online education and offers ample learning opportunities for students with disabilities. Elsaadany et al. [36] explored the use of IoT in education by experimenting with visually impaired students. They demonstrated that IoT can help students with special needs impart knowledge effectively. Similarly, Ding et al. [37] discussed the possibility of integrating IoT and virtual reality to assist students in physical education.

## 3.2. Machine Learning Models to Empower Disabled Students

Recently, researchers have employed machine learning in support of assistive services [38,39]. Poornappriya et al. [38] presented the application of machine learning to aid dyslexia prediction and support learning and cognitive disorders. Sharif et al. [39] proposed a machine learning-based approach that was applied in dyslexia scenarios to assist students with dyslectic needs with various teaching and learning activities. In another study [40], Namoun et al. investigated and proposed a two-phased machine-learning framework for context-aware service selection to help people with disabilities. By applying a scenario-based design technique, the researchers created a comprehensive disability ontology and a machine-learning framework for service selection for disabled people. However, this research aims to select learning services using an ensemble deep learning model for learners with disabilities.

Education service recommendation using deep learning was presented in [41], where the authors showed that collaborative filtering improves service recommendations' accuracy. Moreover, service selection using meta-learning is explored in these studies [42,43]. The authors suggest that using a meta-learner approach could help improve service ranking and selection. Service classification and recommendation using ensemble learning were also explored in other studies, e.g., refs. [44-46]. These studies demonstrate that the use of ensemble learning in recommendation and service selection systems is a viable and effective strategy. Ensemble learning is also applied in other interesting domains, showing an acceptable prediction performance [47]. However, the above studies did not address the requirements of disabled users, their unique context, and environments. Only a few recent studies have started to analyze the open challenges [27] and propose ML frameworks [40] for empowering the consumption of services and assistive technologies by people with disabilities in accessible modes.

## 3.3. Current Research Gaps

Our analysis of the related studies reveals that several research gaps remain unaddressed to date, as follows:

1. Most of the past works do not propose a predictive model to assist disabled students in choosing among assistive services depending on their needs and dynamic circumstances.
2. The role that an intelligent model can play in integrating various services has not been discussed in the context of supporting conducive learning environments for students with learning disabilities.
3. The context of disabled students has not been considered, including their social needs and accessibility requirements.
4. The application of deep learning for predicting IoT services to assist disabled students is not well-understood.
5. Machine learning solutions presented in previous studies, such as [33-35], do not address the user context problem, and their focus is narrowed to just a particular type of disability.

## 4. Development of IoT Assistive Services Datasets

Our research proposes a novel predictive model that helps to recommend assistive services to students with special needs. The proposed model predicts how likely a user with a specific disability in a particular environment would access a smart IoT service through their devices. Moreover, it anticipates the likelihood of obtaining a particular rating for a specific assistive service from a disabled student. For example, a user with a hearing disability is more likely to use a speech-to-text translation service (i.e., to be able to read the explanation) and will rate the service highly if it has a high availability and low latency. In the initial phase, we developed two synthetic datasets of IoT services to train and test the prediction model (the datasets are posted online on GitHub at: https: //github.com/anamoun/servicesfordisabled, accessed on 10 April 2023). The rest of this section explains the rules employed to create the datasets.

The flowchart in Figure 2 depicts the creation of the synthetic datasets of assistive services. The datasets' creation assumes that disabled learners access various educational services using a myriad of digital devices. Important user characteristics considered in the interaction scenario include the type of disability, time of learning, location or environment, assistive technology used, and communication language. Below we explain the logic and rules that were implemented to derive the assistive services datasets.

For each disabled learner in the dataset, the time of learning is randomly selected from 'Morning', 'Afternoon', 'Evening', language of instruction from 'FirstLan', 'SecondLan', Physical Location from 'Public', 'Private', and disability type from 'Visual', 'Hearing', 'Physical', 'Blind', 'Deaf', 'Speech', 'None'. Next, the assistive device used by the student for accessing the educational materials is selected randomly from 'Desktop Computer', 'Tablet', 'Smart Phone', 'Smart T.V.', 'Smart Watch', and 'Voice Assistant'. However, based on the student's disability type, certain devices are omitted from the choice. For example, Lab P.C. is excluded from the possible devices for visually impaired or blind students or students with hand impairment. Similarly, the voice assistant is excluded for deaf users or students with speech or hearing impairments.

After selecting the assistive device, a subtask vector is generated for each student. The subtask vector contains 12 tasks corresponding to one of the 12 assistive services to achieve the learning outcomes. The set of assistive services includes user-location, class-notification, stream, speech-to-text, text-to-speech, speech-to-sign-language, sign-language-to-speech, subtitles, translate, customize-color, image-reader, and speech-commands. Each of the 12 elements in the subtask vector is initially set to 0 or 1 randomly, indicating if the disabled student has used that assistive service before.

All of the above-mentioned values were averaged and translated into a QoS rating

for each assistive service. The QoS rating is added with a random noise fluctuation to

incorporate human behavioral randomness and rounded off, as an integer, to eventually

obtain a user accessibility rating between 1 = least accessible service and 5 = highest acces-

sible service. If a particular student does not use a service, their rating is set to 0. All the

steps mentioned above are repeated for each of the 3000 disabled students in the dataset.

Figure 2. A flowchart outlining the creation logic of datasets. Figure 2. Aflowchart outlining the logic of datasets' creation.



Finally, some subtasks are manually set to 0 or 1 to account for impossible scenarios of certain services. The streaming service is set to 0 if the user device is a 'Smart Watch' as smart watches cannot be used for video streaming. The speech-to-text service is impossible if the device is 'Voice Assistant', the disability is 'Speech' or 'Deafness', or the user location is 'Public'. Hence, these subtasks are set to 0 in such scenarios. Similarly, the text-to-speech service is set to 0 if the device is 'Voice Assistant' or 'Smart Watch' or Disability is 'Blind'. Likewise, if the device is 'Voice Assistant' or Device is 'Smart Watch' or the disability is not 'Deafness' or 'Hearing', then speech-to-sign-language and sign-language-to-speech services are set to 0. If the device is 'Voice Assistant' or 'Smart Watch' or the disability is 'Blindness', subtitles and text translation services are set to 0.

If the device is 'Voice Assistant' or disability is not 'Visual', customize-color service is set to 0, as only visually impaired users require this service, which is unavailable for voice assistant devices. Similarly, if the device is 'Voice Assistant' or 'Smart Watch' or the disability is not 'Blindness', then the image-reader is set to 0. Since users with a speech disability cannot use the speech-commands service, it is set to 0.

Since all services except user-location and class-notification depend on class streaming, if the streaming service is not used, all these services are set to 0. Moreover, since second language users must use subtitles and translation while streaming the class, if streaming is used and the language is 'SecondLan', the subtitles and translation services must be set to 1.

For each subtask, the following IoT services are selected, respectively: 'Location service', Notifications, 'Google meet', 'Google A.S.R.', 'Google TTS', 'Sign language creation', 'Sign language interpretation', 'Subtitles', 'Machine translation', 'Apple resolution', 'Image caption', 'Alexa'.

After the IoT services are determined, an Internet QoS performance dataset is used to generate user ratings of service accessibility. The IoT service rating is set based on the following features:

- Response time;
- Availability;
- Throughput;
- Successibility;
- Reliability;
- Latency.

Response time indicates the average round trip time for the request to each assistive service. Service availability indicates the average chance of the assistive service being operational each time it is requested. Throughput indicates the maximum data rate a service can offer. Successibility refers to the ratio of successful requests for the assistive service. Reliability indicates the average probability of a service delivering the required output. Finally, service latency is the time delay incurred for the request to reach the service.

All of the above-mentioned values were averaged and translated into a QoS rating for each assistive service. The QoS rating is added with a random noise fluctuation to incorporate human behavioral randomness and rounded off, as an integer, to eventually obtain a user accessibility rating between 1 = least accessible service and 5 = highest accessible service. If a particular student does not use a service, their rating is set to 0. All the steps mentioned above are repeated for each of the 3000 disabled students in the dataset.

## 5. The Proposed Ensemble-Based Deep Learning Model

Our suggested service prediction model takes features such as the time of the study, student location, type of device used to access the learning materials, and the disability of the student as inputs. Based on these inputs, two different classification models have been developed incrementally. The first model predicts the learning services a particular student is expected to utilize. In contrast, the second model predicts the student rating based on their experience of using the assistive services. While deploying for a real learning platform, disabled learners can manually submit their locations, public or private, their languages of instruction, and the specifics of their disabilities. Alternatively, a smart learning tool can automatically detect the time and device of the learner.

The overall experimental setup is outlined in Figure 3. Initially, a synthetic dataset was generated and split into training and test partitions. The training partition and service rating labels are used to train the proposed machine learning model, while the test partition is used to test the model in terms of predicting service rating labels.

The proposed model (see Figure 4) is based on an ensemble of multiple feed-forward learners fused using meta-learners that feed multiple output layers simultaneously. Each input for the meta-learner comprises one hot vector representing time, language, location, disability, and device. The different output layers corresponding to one of the service labels share the hidden layer or the meta learner. The output layers comprise two units for the binary service usage classification and six units for the service rating prediction. The model uses the 'Adam' optimizer for training and ties to minimize the cross-entropy loss. The architecture of the proposed model is illustrated in Figure 4.

Since our task involves a small number of input features for training classification models, a single trained model may not generalize well when deployed in production. Hence, our proposed method involves training multiple learners (e.g., heterogeneous models) to further improve the generalization of unseen data. These learners can be viewed as a set of Ensemble learning models aggregate multiple individual classifiers utilizing their diversity in capturing the input features. The individual models for the model proposed in this paper are based on different input features and try to learn the relationship between the service rating labels and these features separately.

Input: a training\_dataset with student characteristics as input features, base\_models, meta\_learner, Bayesian\_optimization\_algorithm, while tasks used and service ratings as target labels Output: ensemble\_model with trained weights Multiple aggregation methods, such as weighted averaging, simple averaging, or summation, can be used to merge the individual classifiers. However, recently, metalearning has been more successful for ensemble models. Meta learners stack an additional classifier to merge the outputs of the individual classifiers. The meta-learning classifier automatically learns the emphasis for each of the individual classifiers feeding it. The overall network is also called a stacked network and is trained end-to-end using the training dataset. The proposed model utilizes meta-learning to merge the individual feature classifiers with additional hidden layers that learn while training the weights for combining the individual classifiers. The training process emphasizes the input classifiers that significantly impact the service rating prediction output.
