11  of  18

The proposed model needs to simultaneously predict the labels for multiple services. Hence, the model is designed using multiple feed forward neural networks that predict the class labels in parallel using the input features. During training, each classifier fits class labels for a separate service category. Similarly, the classifiers infer the class labels for different services during the testing phase. In contrast to a single end-to-end feed forward neural network, the proposed model ensembles multiple stand-alone neural networks to make predictions. The models are trained separately, and their predictions are combined to compute the final performance measures.

The proposed stacked model needs to predict the labels for multiple services simultaneously. Hence, the meta-learning layers feed multiple output feed-forward layers in parallel. The models are trained separately, and their predictions are combined to compute the final performance measures.

All input features, i.e., time, language, location, disability, and device are separately converted to a one-hot vector. Time is represented as a one-hot vector with three dimensions, each indicating morning, evening, or afternoon with one. Language is converted into twodimensional input, each indicating Arabic or Non-Arabic. Two-dimensional vectors also indicate the location to represent public or private spaces. A seven-dimensional vector, each indicating hearing, blind, physical visual deaf, speech, or no disability, represents the disability type. The device is represented by five-dimensional vectors, each dimension indicating tablet, smart watch, smart phone, smart T.V., or voice assistant.

The input layers represent one-hot vectors for each of the student features, i.e., disability, device location, time, and language. Each of these input layers feeds an individual learner comprising two dense hidden layers, each with linear activations and 12 neurons. The individual learners are then merged using a shared meta-learning model with three dense layers, each comprising 100 units with rectified linear activations. The shared metalearner feeds 12 individual output models with dense Softmax layers. The Softmax output layers comprise two neurons for binary classification of service usage and six neurons for the service rating task, whereby five out of the six neurons represent the rating level for the service, whilst the sixth one represents service not being used.

The model is optimized using Adamax optimizer to minimize categorical cross and entropy loss. Figure 4 illustrates the ensemble neural network architecture used as the machine learning model. A dense layer with linear activations is used as the common hidden layer, while separate dense layers with softmax activations are used as output layers.

Atotal of 70% of the data was used to train the ensemble model, while the remaining 30% was retained to test the model. The prediction of the output layer with the highest probability is selected as the target student rating. The performance was evaluated by comparing it with the true labels within the dataset in terms of accuracy and F-score. Our meta-learning model is implemented in Keras.

Since our task involves a small number of input features for training classification models, a single trained model may not generalize well when deployed in production. Hence, our proposed method involves training multiple learners (e.g., heterogeneous models) to further improve the generalization of unseen data. These learners can be viewed as a set of feature extractors where predicted outputs are then combined (in the form of auxiliary features) and used as input features to train the meta-learners. All models' hyperparameters are optimized using a Bayesian approach, as depicted in Algorithm 1.

## Algorithm 1: Pseudocode for the proposed ensemble meta-learning approach

- Input: a training\_dataset with student characteristics as input features, base\_models, meta\_learner, Bayesian\_optimization\_algorithm, while tasks used and service ratings as target labels

## Output: ensemble\_model with trained weights

1. Initialize: list of base models and their predictions

Taking the student specific features as input and feeding its output as auxiliary features to the meta learners

2. For each base model in base\_models:
- a. Optimize hyper parameters of the base model using Bayesian\_optimization\_algorithm on training\_data
- b. Train the optimized base model on training\_data
3. Combine the predictions of base models into auxiliary features
4. Train meta\_learner using auxiliary features as input and the tasks usage along with the service ratings as separate outputs
5. Store the meta\_learner's weights and predictions on validation\_data
6. Repeat the steps until stopping criterion is met:
- a. Train meta\_learner on validation\_data using auxiliary features as input
- b. Store the meta\_learner's weights and predictions on validation\_data
7. Return ensemble\_model consisting of the trained base learners and the updated meta learner

## 6. Results

The prediction of the ensemble meta-learning model is evaluated in terms of primary performance metrics, including accuracy, and f-score, for both the service usage likelihood and service ratings by disabled learners. The results are compared in terms of classification accuracy and f-score. The classification accuracy represents the number of accurate predictions out of the total predictions. The f-score represents the harmonic mean of precision and recall. The harmonic mean is used to average the ratios. The precision indicates the number of actual protections out of the total predictions for a particular class. At the same time, recall represents the number of accurate predictions out of all the samples of that class. Weighted averages of precision and recall across all categories are computed to obtain the average metrics, which are then used to calculate the harmonic mean to represent the f-score. The accuracy and f-score for binary usage prediction of each service are reported separately in Table 1. The average accuracy across the 12 assistive tasks is 84.91%.

Table 1. Task classification scores.

| Task                                  | Assistive Service            |   Accuracy |   F-Score |
|---------------------------------------|------------------------------|------------|-----------|
| Identify user location                | Location service             |      0.858 |     0.879 |
| Notify user about class               | Notifications                |      0.846 |     0.832 |
| Live stream the classroom             | Google Meet                  |       0.86 |     0.832 |
| Speech to text                        | Google ASR                   |      0.845 |     0.841 |
| Text to speech                        | Google TTS                   |      0.831 |     0.864 |
| Speech to sign language               | Sign language creation       |      0.857 |     0.908 |
| Sign language to speech               | Sign language interpretation |      0.844 |     0.873 |
| Show subtitles or captions on screen  | Subtitles                    |      0.854 |     0.912 |
| Translate text from Arabic to English | Machine translation          |       0.85 |     0.864 |

| Image text reader                     | Image caption                |      0.857 |     0.903 |

The performance scores for the task of service rating prediction are in a lower range because the task is more complex and involves more randomness. All the classification scores lie in the range of 56 to 60%. However, the performance scores are still quite significant as the classification scores are above the chance level, around 20% for a task with five possible outcomes. Moreover, the classification accuracy indicates that the rating for the speech-to-text service is relatively easier to predict. This is logical, as students with hearing or speaking disabilities are likely to rate such services higher.

Figures 5 and 6 compare the performance scores across the assistive services, as bar charts, for both classification tasks. Figure 7 indicates the optimization curves for the training of all the output layers corresponding to each of the indicated services.

The end-to-end optimization process aims to minimize the overall loss of the network. The overall loss is the sum of the optimization losses for each output corresponding to one of the 12 services. The optimization curves illustrate the loss minimization for each service output. The overall optimization curve is the sum of the individual curves. Similarly, the performance scores indicate each service's class and optimization curves. Most of the services achieve similar results with slight variations.

The classification results are significant in terms of binary prediction of tasks as any recommendation system can utilize this classification model and consequently predict if a disabled person will be interested in a particular assistive service. Recommending relevant IoT services to users will build their trust in any platform. Similarly, the prediction model for user rating can also be used to analyze IoT-enabled platforms with highly rated services for students with disabilities.

For comparison with the benchmark, the same classification task was implemented using a simple feed-forward neural network, which achieves 82% accuracy for the binary classification and around 36% for service prediction, which is significantly lower than the results achieved by our proposed ensemble learning architecture. The results were also compared with shallow machine learning models, specifically support vector machine (SVM) and random forest. ## The end-to-end optimization process aims to minimize the overall loss of the net-

7. Discussion of Key Findings and Limitations

The proposed prediction model was tested in two scenarios: (a) for binary classifica-

## 7. Discussion of Key Findings and Limitations

The proposed prediction model was tested in two scenarios: (a) for binary classification to predict if a disabled user will use a service or not; and (b) for multiple category classification to predict the user rating for the assistive service. The results for binary classification for service usage are encouraging, and the model correctly identifies whether a user will use a service. This result motivates the online learning tools to incorporate our prediction model as part of their architecture to recommend assistive services to students based on their input parameters. The correct recommendations put forward by the model will, in turn, build trust for the student in the learning platform. The positive impact on the user created by the proper recommendations develops a friendly and trustworthy human-computer interface. Consequently, the user will more likely rate the service and the learning tool highly, which will also affect the secondary classification task.

However, the second model to predict user ratings for the current dataset needs to report satisfactory accuracy, although it is way above the chance level of random classification for five categories. The lower accuracy for service rating can be attributed to an increased amount of randomness in the dataset for user rating of the service compared to service usage. Service usage is more deterministic than service rating as usage is more tightly bound to user parameters such as disability type, usage device, and user location.

A major limitation of this research is the synthetic nature of the datasets that we used in our research. This decision was made because of the scarcity of this type of data. It is noteworthy that the dataset developed and used in this research has a specific amount of deterministic algorithm and randomness, as explained earlier in the dataset section. The synthetic nature of the data is also visible in the different performance scores for both classification tasks. It may be possible that testing the proposed model using real datasets might yield different performance outcomes. Another limitation concerns our weak understanding of the mental models of people with disabilities and how they utilize assistive services. Further user studies are advocated to decipher the ways in which disabled learners interact with smart educational services, with opportunities to fuse those services in different contexts to achieve complex goals. Examples of user studies that established a strong theoretical understanding of the mental models of users who aggregate software services include [48,49]. Both studies also highlight the challenges of understanding user mental models in the context of service development.

## 8. Concluding Remarks and Future Work

With technological advancements, modern education systems rely more on online and digital learning tools. However, the needs of students with disabilities must be catered for by such digital learning platforms. Our research addresses the problem of using Internet of Things-based environments by students with disabilities to enhance their learning experiences. IoT has been used recently for intelligent environments in many areas; however, to the best of our knowledge, this study's application of deep learning for predicting IoT services to assist disability users is the first effort of its kind.

This paper proposed a deep learning-based prediction model which predicts the services a learning environment must suggest to students with different disabilities. The model predicts whether a user will use a service or not, along with the accessibility rating for the service on a 5-point Likert scale (least accessible to highly accessible). The proposed ensemble neural network model achieved an overall accuracy of 84.91% for the binary classification of using a service and an overall accuracy of just around 58.47% for predicting service ratings by users with disabilities. Our prediction scores are superior to those achieved with a simple neural network and traditional machine learning models (e.g., SVM and Randon Forest), showing the importance of ensemble and meta-learning in ameliorating service predictions for people with special needs.

The predictions of our model can be used as a module in learning recommendation systems for suggesting assistive services to students with disabilities in virtual environ-

## Paper Source: Subject Dependent Speech Verification Approach for Assistive Special Education
============================================================

## Subject dependent speech verification approach for assistive special education

Umut Zeki 1 · Tolgay Karanfiller 2 · Kamil Yurtkan 1,3

Received: 23 August 2023 / Accepted: 15 January 2024 / Published online: 7 February 2024 © The Author(s) 2024

## Abstract

The developmental, characteristics and educational competencies of students who need special education are developing slowly in compared to their agemates. This is  because their expressive language is different. In order to overcome these challenges, assistive technologies can be used under the supervision of the teachers. In this paper, a person dependent speech verification approach is proposed for special education students. The system verifies the speech of special education students in various ways. Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) is employed for the classification task. Audio signals that are collected as datasets are accomplished by collecting  samples  from  the  real  education  centers  involving  special  education  students. For each subject, a different CNN is trained. Obtained audio signals undergo a frequency domain transform, and then their spectrograms are computed. The spectrogram images of every audio sample are then processed as inputs to the CNN. In this way, better representations of the audio signals are achieved where the spectrogram images of the audio files of different subjects are discriminable. This is also the result of special education students' personal and unique speaking styles. The proposed approach is tested on the dataset that is constructed by real subject recordings. The system achieves promising results by performing comparable recognition accuracies of around 96%.

Keywords Speech verification · Special education · Deep learning · Convolutional neural Networks · Speech recognition

* Umut Zeki umut\_zeki@hotmail.com

1 Software Engineering Department, Rauf Denktaş University, Northern Cyprus Via Mersin 10, Girne St. Surlarici, Nicosia, Turkey

2 Faculty of Education, Cyprus International University, Northern Cyprus Via Mersin 10, Haspolat, Nicosia, Turkey

3 Artificial Intelligence Application and Research Center, Cyprus International University, Northern Cyprus Via Mersin 10, Haspolat, Nicosia, Turkey





## 1  Introduction

Speech recognition and verification are frequently confused. On one side, 'speech recognition' is the skill of being able to identify spoken words. On the other side, speech verification uses speech recognition to verify the accuracy of the spoken language. Speech recognition is a simple task for an adult human, whereas it is a  challenge  for  the  modern  computer.  The  challenge  is  that  everyone's  pronunciation  varies.  Speech  verification  is  the  solution  to  this  problem;  that  uses  the speech  and  verifies  if  the  desired  word  is  spoken.  Speech  recognition  technology is used in many applications today. Such technologies can also be found in educational  applications.  These  days,  there  is  a  growing  trend  and  demand  for educational  software  created  for  people  with  special  needs.  However,  successfully  implementing  the  aforementioned  technologies  in  developed  educational applications is a difficult procedure. Because individuals who have special needs have challenges using the previously mentioned applications via speaking (İlker &amp; Melekoğlu, 2017). This makes speech recognition task difficult. However, in order to get satisfied results while studying with people who require special education,  it  is  preferable  to  work  with  data  collected  directly  from  them,  instead of  working  with  existing  datasets.  Because  they  may  have  problems  with  making sounds or problems of colloquial stuttering, expressed in speech flow disturbances, partial word repetitions, or prolonged sounds and syllables. For all these reasons, speech recognition systems designed for individuals with normal development are not successful enough in special education.

Based  on  above  mentioned  problems,  we  propose  a  novel  person-dependent speech verification approach designed for students who has special needs. Our system gets the user's speech samples once using them for the first time as a dataset. Then, the verification step is done. Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) is used as classifier. On the other hand, the pre-processing advantages are inherited (Saha, 2018). The CNN employed is created and trained from the scratch. The dataset used is  also  an  originality of the paper. Audio signals that are collected as datasets are accomplished  by  collecting  from  the  special  education  students  by  recording  the audio samples in which they pronounce digits. For each subject, a different CNN is trained. Another level is to change the audio representation. Here, the audio signal undergoes a frequency domain transform, and then its spectrogram is computed. The spectrogram images of every audio sample are then processed as inputs to the CNN. With this way, a better representation of the audio signal is achieved where the spectrogram images about the audio files of different subjects are discriminable. The proposed system is unique because there is no study encountered in the literature for special education which does person-dependent speech verification. Therefore, these technologies can be employed in other special education applications to accurately check the student's speech. As a result, the students with special needs will benefit from similar assistive technologies. The models are simulated and the performance evaluations are done accordingly.

The  development  of  assistive  technologies  is  utmost  important  in  several aspects.  For  example,  enhancing  the  teacher's  performance  with  an  assistive technology  is  important.  On  the  other  hand,  by  using  assistive  technologies,  a digital archive can be formed where the progress of the students can be tracked. Another importance is that the students themselves can use automated applications under the supervision. Regarding all these aspects, the proposed approach is  solving the automatic audio verification tasks where it can be adapted to any related assistive technology to be used in special education domain.



Considering the marginality of the speech of individuals with special needs, the proposed approach will fill in this area. The proposed approach has been developed for  a  possible  mobile  application's  speech  verification  module.  In  this  way,  when the user uses the speech recognition feature, the evaluations will be done using the subject's own dataset, and the speaking problems can be easily overcome. The main contribution  of  the  paper  is  that  it  is  one  of  the  proposed  approaches  applied  on real data that come from special education students and that will be a positive addition to the related literature. Also, its person-dependency using the spectrogram levels before the CNN computations are applied to these original data. The approach achieves  promising  results  by  performing  comparable  recognition  accuracies  of around 96%.

## 2    Related works

In  applications  using  machine  learning  and  computer  vision,  speech  recognition presents  a  significant  problem.  Numerous  machine  learning  techniques  have  been employed to address the issue of speech recognition in the literature.

The first speech recognition mechanism was developed in 1920. It was a celluloid dog who came out from his base when he heard and recognized his name (David &amp; Selfridge, 1962). During the 1950s, Audrey used speech recognition technology to recognize digits (Biddulph &amp; R &amp; Balashek S, 1952). A tool called the Shoebox was presented by IBM at the 1962 Seattle World's Fair. Besides being able to perform mathematical functions, it could also recognize the digits and 16 spoken words (Shaikh, 2023). Significant improvements are reported later then in the field.

Deep learning, on the other hand, has improved voice recognition to the point that it  can be used outside of highly regulated contexts (Geitgey, 2016). The suggested Intelligent Phishing Detection System (IPDS) by Adebowale et al. had a classification accuracy of 93.28%. The technique can screen harmful websites based on behavioral characteristics obtained from earlier data sets (Adebowale et al., 2020). Bingol and Aydogmus proposed a DNN Pruning Deep Neural Network (p-DNN), which was compared to different classification techniques, in 2020. A robot manipulator was created and used to implement the HRI framework. The robot begins to listen to the dialogue when it hears the term "KUKA" (Vickers, 2017). Moreover, in 2019, Huang et al.'s approach to identifying speech emotion in spontaneous speech takes into account both verbal and nonverbal speech sounds. Experimental results showed  that  the  proposed  approach  procured  a  52.00%  detection  accuracy  that exceeded classical methods (Huang et al., 2019).

In  contrast  to  conventional  classification  techniques,  CNN,  a  sub-branch  of Deep Learning, can take an input image and separate its many aspects and objects with less pre-processing. In this field, there have been a lot of studies done on speech recognition. For example, Jing et al. (2019) suggested two voice recognition algorithms based on CNN to improve speech accuracy. A dynamic adaptive pooling (DA-pooling) algorithm is suggested for the CNN model's pooling layer. In a full-connected layer, a dropout strategy based on sparseness is presented to overcome standard  dropout  hiding  neuron  nodes  randomly.  The  results  suggest that adding a DA-pooling algorithm and improving the dropout sparseness technique to CNN-based speech recognition will boost the sparseness of speech recognition.  To  classify  emotions  in  speech,  Zhao  et  al.  (2019)  presented  1D  and 2D CNN LSTM networks. Raw audio recordings and log-Mel spectrograms were used to investigate how local similarities and global contextual knowledge may be gleaned from them. On the Berlin EmoDB of speaker-dependent and speakerindependent  experiments,  the  2D  CNN  LSTM  network  achieves  95.33%  and 95.89% recognition accuracies, respectively, which is far better than the conventional approaches. Solanki and Pandey (2022) proposed a deep convolution neural  network architecture for identifying polyphonic music instruments. The network is designed to calculate an arbitrary number of instruments for fixed-length music. After 60 epochs, the study finds an exceptional result of 92.80% precision. In 2018, Nagajyothi and Siddaiah (2018) presented an airport inquiry framework based on ASR. The database is created based on the most frequently asked questions in an airport inquiry. Because of its high efficiency, CNN has been used for database training and analysis. There are several applied methods for speech recognition. But there are very few studies that use spectrograms for speech recognition. Musaev et al. (n.d.) has studied speech recognition by using spectrogram images  of  Uzbek  spoken  digits.  Spectrogram  images  from  speech  signals  were generated and they were used for deep CNN training. As a subset of speech recognition, there are no study has been encountered related to speech verification using CNN in the literature. However, numerous research in various disciplines have used CNN to verify the correctness of a picture, a face, a fingerprint, or a traffic sign. For instance, a study proposed by Mohapatra (Hafemann et al., 2017) was conducted in the realm of biometrics in 2019 and used CNN to verify offline handwritten signatures. On the other hand, a unique end-to-end method for textdependent speaker verification  is  described  in  the  work  on  speaker  verification that Shi-Xiong Zhang published in 2016 (Zhang et al., 2016). Additionally, there are several studies in the area of face verification. The approach for unbounded face verification presented by Jun-Cheng Chen that year uses convolutional features and is evaluated on both the conventional Labeled Face in the Wild (LFW) dataset  and  the  freshly  released  IARPA  Janus  Benchmark  A  (IJB-A)  dataset (Chen  et  al.,  2015).  There  are  many  speech  recognition  studies  on  individuals in  need of special education. In 2011 Garret J. T. employed speech recognition software as an alternate treatment to help people with physical limitations write more fluently (Garrett et al., 2011). The findings also showed that when utilizing speech recognition rather than word processing, people with physical limitations wrote  longer  drafts.  Ok  M.  W.  investigated  how  students  with  high  incidence impairments used speech recognition (SR) technology in grades 4-8 and how students and teachers perceived utilizing SR as a writing process in 2020 (Ok et al.,





2022). Overall, research on speech recognition applications still faces challenges. To achieve robust recognition systems, significant research studies are ongoing.

The implemented approach in this study is a person-dependent speech verification system using CNN. Rather than recognition, the proposed approach performs verification  task.  The  following  is  a  brief  explanation  of  the  differences  between these two methods:

Speech  verification  answers:  Is  this  digit  x?  Whereas,  speech  recognition answers: What is this digit?

The proposed approach utilizes the information in the input audio signal by transforming it into the frequency domain. This is achieved by spectrogram transformation of the audio signal. The transformation is summarized in Sect. 3.2.

## 3    Speech verification for special education

The speech recognition system is capable to translate the spoken words into text. Text can be either in terms of words or sequences of words or it could be in syllables form. Speech recognition is one of the challenging tasks in machine learning. We know that human speech signals are very varied due to differences in speaker characteristics and speaking styles, and that they can even incorporate environmental noises (Nidhi &amp; Satish, 2019). Speech verification is a sub-discipline of speech recognition.  The  system attempts to validate a spoken digit, as the name implies. It is a direct comparison. The system's goal here is to validate whether the spoken digit is correct rather than to correctly recognize it. Figure 1 depicts this scenario.

A spectrogram is a visual representation of a signal's frequency spectrum as it changes over time. A graph with two geometric dimensions is a typical format: one axis  indicates  time,  while  the  other  axis  shows  frequency.  In  this  study,  spectrograms are constructed from a time-domain signal by computing the Fourier Transform (FT) from the time signal.  An  FT  is  a  mathematical  transform  that  decomposes functions based on space or time into functions by using spatial or temporal frequency. FT is mainly used to create a spectrogram. It's a well studied transformation in recent studies. Considering the signal in the time domain, it is sampled and decomposed into windows that may overlap. Then, the frequency analysis are done for every window based on FT. Each chunk is then represented as a vertical line in the image, which represents a magnitude versus frequency measurement at a certain point in time. These spectrums or time plots are then "placed side by side" to  take  a  picture  or  a  three-dimensional  surface,  or  slightly  overlapped  in  various ways, which is a process known as windowing. The Short-Time Fourier Transform (STFT) squared magnitude of the signal is determined in this technique (Spectrogram n.d.). STFT (Sejdić et al., 2009) is a Fourier-related transform that is used to determine  the  sinusoidal  frequency  and  phase  content  of  small  sections  of  a  signal  as  they  vary  over  time.  In  practice,  STFTs  are  computed  by  dividing  a  larger temporal signal into equal-length segments and computing the FT independently on each shorter segment. The Fourier spectrum is presented on each shorter section. The changing spectra as a function of time are then displayed using a spectrogram or waterfall plot, which is commonly used in Software Defined Radio (SDR)-based spectrum displays. Full bandwidth displays covering the entire spectrum of an SDR often use Fast Fourier Transforms (FFTs) with 224 points. Simply put, the function to be transformed is multiplied by a window function that is nonzero for just a short period in the continuous-time situation. As the window is moved down the time axis, the FT (a one-dimensional function) of the resulting signal is taken, resulting in a two-dimensional representation of the signal. Equation 1 shows the transformation.



Fig. 1 A generic digit verification system





The window function is w(T), which is usually a Hann window or a Gaussian window that is centered to zero. The signal to be transformed is x(t). W is the frequency, and w is the window function. x(T,W) is the Fourier transform of x(t)w(t-T), which is a complex function that represents the signal's phase and magnitude with time and frequency (Allen, 1977). For example, a one-dimensional waveform image is turned into a spectrogram image as in Fig. 2, allowing it to be easily recognized from other sounds. In the proposed approach, every spoken digit was captured, and then the audio signals were transformed into spectrograms using the STFT method.

CNN is designed basically for digital images and adapted to take matrices as inputs for the input layers. Using spectrogram images of the audio input supports this input layer flexibility of the network as well as enhancing the signal representation. Otherwise, the first layers must be modified in order to support one-dimensional audio signals as inputs. Furthermore, using a spectrogram as the input of CNN reduces  it  to  an  image  classification  problem.  This  image  represents  the spoken digit from left to right promptly. It can also be used to verify a phrase that is written from left to right, by identifying the hidden characters (Katrik, 2020).



Fig. 2 Generated waveform image from an audio file of the student's pronunciation of zero is converted to a spectrogram image



CNN is  a  deep  learning  class  that  requires  substantially  less  pre-processing and produces better outcomes as the number of training sessions grows (Gilu &amp; Bhaskaran, 2020). Figure 3 depicts the network architecture of the voice recognition  CNN technique. The processing in CNN starts with feature extraction layers and ends with fully linked Neural Network classification layers. Using many layers  enhances  recognition  accuracy  while  being  insensitive  to  tiny  geometric alterations of the input images. CNN is successfully utilized on real-world data for classification jobs due to its robustness.



CONNICTEO

Fig. 3 The architecture of our proposed approach: Ten different person-dependent CNNs



## 4    Proposed approach

The proposed method is being developed for a special education application to teach numerals. It seeks to recognize the digit pronounced by a user in a short period of time with high precision. In one area of the application, the user should respond  to  the  query  by  saying.  As  a  result,  before  beginning  to  learn  a  digit, each student's sound samples should be collected as inputs. For example, if digit 2 is to be taught, the application instructs the pupil to speak it numerous times. These data are subsequently employed in the training of CNN, together with the student's sound, while learning the given digit in the application (Fig. 3). What the student says at the beginning of the application is used as a training sample, and what the student says throughout the application is used as a test sample. In this method, person-dependent training is carried out. This means that each subject must have its own CNN. There should be n × 10 CNN structures for every n subjects. This is the outcome of having ten different spectrograms to verify for each digit (0-9). Figure 3 depicts the general technique, where 10 (0-9) CNN are constructed for a single subject.

## 4.1    Dataset

A special education expert visited Algm Special Education Center to create the datasets. The speech samples were collected by ten mildly impaired special education students. To avoid distractions while collecting students' speech samples, a quiet and plain room was set up. To avoid data loss, a dedicated voice recording  device  was  employed  to  gather  and  transfer  information  to  the  computer. Also a special education expert was present during the data collection process. The  students  voice  is  recorded  during  their  spellings  of  the  digits  from  0  to  9 (0-9). They have entered to the room one by one to avoid any interference. The process was carried out ten times for each student. A break was given as necessary because some of the pupils became easily distracted and tired of repeating the  same  figures.  Since  some  students  were  excited  when  they  first  entered  the class, or because they had difficulties repeating the numbers accurately, they were requested to repeat the numbers from some sets. Overall, one thousand sampler acquired from the total of ten students with slight mental retardation. Audio signals were transformed to RGB spectrogram images after gathering sample forms. As a result, image resolutions were reduced during designing by considering the inverse ratio between proper estimation rate and rapid running. Then the images undergo  grayscale  transformation.  Figure  4  shows  some  examples.  The  dataset is  then  divided  into  two  parts  where  the  first  part  includes  3600  samples  (10 users × 10 sets × 9 false digits × 4 samples) for training, and the part two includes 400 samples (10 users × 10  sets × 1  true  digit × 4  samples)  for  testing.  For  convenient use, all the images are stored in an appropriate file format.





0

Fig. 4 Speech samples of digits (one set) of one subject are converted to spectrogram images

## 4.2    System architecture

The  transformed  signals  undergo  preprocessing  steps.  To  use  the  spectrograms properly, the preprocessing steps are compulsory.

Firstly,  because of the audio files of each student containing all the digits in one file, they are cropped to separate them into single digits without any space at the beginning and the end. Then, each audio file is transformed into a spectrogram. Because of having spectrogram files in RGB format with high resolution; they are then converted into grayscale(black and white) images with lower resolution. This process is done to spped up the system.

The data of each student was put in a separate folder. Ten subfolders have been added for each digit and named as 0,1,2…9 respectively. In each subfolder, two more folders were created and named as true and false. Spectrogram samples of the digits that the system needs to verify are placed in the true folder. The rest of the digits are put in the false folder. As it is illustrated in Fig. 5, if thestudent 'Furkan''s digit 0 is to be verified, sound sample spectrograms of 0 are placed in the true folder and sound sample spectrograms other than 0 (1-9) are placed in the false folder.



Fig. 5 The illustration of file and folder structure is used in the proposed algorithm



A CNN is made up of numerous layers, each of which transforms a volume of activations across a differentiable function. While building the network, we used a total of 15 layers. These are Convolution Layer, Pooling Layer, Relu Layer, Softmax Layer, Classification Layer, Image Input Layer, and Batch Normalization Layer. The architecture of the system is illustrated in Fig. 6.



Fig. 6 Illustration of the Convolution Neural Network model used



CNN's first layers function as feature extractors, and the first block of that type of neural network builds the feature. It applies template matching by utilizing convolution filtering processes. The first layer filters the image using convolution kernels and returns "feature maps" that are normalized using an activation function and/ or shrunk. The audio signal undergoes a frequency domain transform and then its spectrogram is computed. The spectrogram images of every audio sample are then processed as inputs to the CNN. So, the spectrogram is used in the input layer like input 2-D images to a network. Its dimension is 100 × 186x1 with 'zero center' normalization. The resolution of the supplied image is 100 × 186, and 1 denotes a grayscale image. The input images are then sent through a series of convolutional filters, each of which activates different features in the images. The number of filters in this case is eight, and the filter size is 3  ×  3. Convolutions are also used with stride [1 1] and padding [0 0 0 0]. A Batch Normalization Layer is used after convolution to equalize each input channel across a mini-batch. This layer is utilized between the Convolutional and Relu Layers to accelerate CNN training and reduce susceptibility to network initialization. The fourth layer, Relu Layer, serves as the activation layer. A Relu Layer applies a threshold operation to each element of the input, setting any value less than zero to zero. Following that, we must do downsampling by dividing the  input  into  rectangular  pooling  regions  and  determining  the  maximum  of  each zone by adding a Max-Pooling Layer with pool size [2 2] and stride [2 2]. In the second block, all layers except the input layer are repeated. The Convolution Layer is the only difference here. Its specifications differ from the first. This time, a 2-D Convolutional Layer is built with 32 filters of size [3 3] and'same' padding. During training, the software finds and puts the size of the zero paddings, so that the output layer has the same size as the input. In the last block of Convolution Layer like the second block, Batch Normalization Layer and Relu Layer are repeated. Later, the fully connected layer after the Relu Layer performs the classification. Then, multiplies the input by a weight matrix and to a bias vector with an output size of 2. The last two layers are output layers. The first one is the Softmax Layer and the last one is  the  Classification  Layer.  Figure  7  shows  the  architecture  of  the  CNN  approach used in details for one subject and one sound signal of one digit (Bishop &amp; Nasrabadi, 2006; Ioffe &amp; Szegedy, 2015; Krizhevsky et al., 2017; LeCun et al., 1989, 1998; Murphy, 2012; Nagi et al., 2011; Nair &amp; Hinton, 2010; Shijie et al., 2017).



Fig. 7 The architecture CNN approach for one subject and one digit



Fig. 8 True(Test) class of a user for 0



Fig. 9 False(Training) class of a user for 0



## 5    Results and discussions

## 5.1    Experimental setup

A deep learning library is used that is a network architecture to implement CNN architecture in the simulator. The pixel sizes of the images that are trained are small enough to maintain acceptable processing time whereas large enough not to reduce the recognition quality. There are 4000 samples which are 3600 from training and 400 from testing by a training dataset separately.

The samples that the system would train, were put in the True class (Fig. 8); and the samples that the system would test, were put in the false class (Fig. 9). For example, if the target is to verify whether the pronounced digit is 5 or not, the true class contains samples of the 5's taken from user speech and converted to a spectrogram; the false class contain other numbers than 5, that are pronounced by the user.

| Subject8        | 100%  | 100% | 100% | 90%  | 100% | 90%  | 100% | 100% | 90%  | 90%  |
| Subject9        | 100%  | 100% | 100% | 90%  | 90%  | 100% | 100% | 100% | 90%  | 100% |
| subject10       | 90%   | 100% | 90%  | 100% | 100% | 100% | 90%  | 90%  | 100% | 100% |
| Overall Average | 96.7% |      |      |      |      |      |      |      |      |      |

## 5.2    Training, validation and results

With a learning rate of 0.001, the system is trained using stochastic gradient descent with momentum (SGDM). Table 1 shows the number of epochs and iterations.

In total of 4000 tests, including ten subjects with ten digits, the best training and validation accuracies are achieved. Each subject's sound sample from 0 to 9 is tested 4 times. At the beginning of every experiment, the overall data is shuffled and then training and validation split is done. This process is repeated 4 times and then the average rates are recorded in order to validate the results.

Table 2 displays the test accuracy for each individual vs. digit. The figures here are the average of four tests for each digit. The average test accuracy for each digit is provided in the same table. The system's validation accuracy is 96.7% on average.

Table 3 displays the validation accuracy for each subject vs. digit. The figures here are the average of four tests for each digit. The system achieves the average validation accuracy of 95.5%.

Although few digits are not pronounced good, the system is even capable to classify them as well. The results can be considered as satisfactory as in a conventional voice recognition system the accuracies are even lower for some poorly pronounced digits (Fig. 10).

The pronunciation of  the  special  education  students  is  more  difficult  to  understand compared to their agemates. Therefore, the system is trained for each student separately  and  it  is  subject  dependent.  Table  4  shows  the  comparison  of  the  proposed study together with the other studies.

The  proposed  approach  achieves  comparable  performances  as  shown  in  Table  4. The other state of the art studies listed in the table are dedicated to speech recognition, rather than verification. In (Nagajyothi &amp; Siddaiah, 2018), the performances of the standard Feed Forward Neural Network and the Convolutional Neural Network are compared for speech recognition. In (Tanaka et al., 2019), a hybrid Neural NetworkHidden Markov Model is employed. All the reported experimental results are seemed to be satisfactory but their problem domains are different. For instance, in Nagajyothi and Siddaiah (2018), the experimental data is collected from an airport. In (Ioffe &amp; Szegedy, 2015), Japanese language is used. In this aspect, the originality of the proposed approach is that its domain is special education students.

A fair comparison of the proposed method is done with M.Musaev et al.'s study. They have also used a similar approach to recognize the Uzbek digits. Their training set is enriched, including the speech data of every individual subject, whereas, in our study, we only use students who need special education. Under this limitation, our system performs. On the other hand, our system is a verification system, whereas the compared system is a recognition system. Also, application to the Turkish language domain is another significant point to discuss. Under all the limitations and similarities, the proposed method achieves comparable and high rates.

## 6    Conclusion and future work

In  this  paper,  a  speech  verification  approach  is  proposed  that  is  a  person-dependent approach, in order to verify the digits from the speech of the special education students. Considering the marginality of the speech of individuals with special needs, the proposed approach fills in this gap and it is a positive addition to the literature in this aspect. The approach uses s a CNN. The CNN used is trained from scratch. The dataset used is another originality of the paper, which is accomplished by collecting the pronounced digits of the students under the supervision of special education experts. The overall approach verifies speech in a satisfactory level. The system is capable to achieve 96.7% average accuracy. We believe that, with a possible mobile application integration, the system will be able to support the teaching of special education students in the future.

## 6.1    Theoritical contribution

The proposed approach is contributing to the field in the way that it is employing deep learning models with spectrogram inputs of audio signals that are obtained from special education students. Furthermore, the approach contributes to artificial intelligence applications related to special education. Another aspect is the person dependency of the approach where each student is treated as separate sample and a dedicated deep learning model is trained for every student by using their own audio inputs. Considering all these aspects, the approach is a positive addition to the literature about employing artificial intelligence algorithms to special education domain.



## 6.2    Practical implications

The proposed approach will be useful in mobile or web based software applications in order to assist the teacher. As the system is able to automatically recognize the voices of the special education students and their digit pronunciations, it will be an assistive technology to be used by the teachers. In practice, the special education students need one to one sessions. In this sense, using an assistive application will enhance the teacher's performance. On the other hand, another practical  implication  is  that  the  proposed  approach  can  be  a  part  of  a  possible mobile or web based application that will be directly used by the students under the supervision. Automatic audio verification tasks of such an application can be performed by using the proposed approach.

## Paper Source: Towards Adaptive Multimedia System for Assisting Children with Arabic Learning Difficulties
============================================================

## Towards Adaptive Multimedia System for Assisting Children with Arabic Learning Difficulties

Moutaz Saleh, Jihad Mohamad Alja'am

Department of Computer Science &amp; Engineering

College of Engineering, Qatar University Doha, Qatar {moutaz.saleh, jaam}@qu.edu.qa

Abstract -Children with learning difficulties (LD) are increasing  dramatically  in  the  Arab  world.  Such  children require quick intervention especially during the early childhood years. This paper presents a dynamic multimedia system  for  helping  children  with  LD  to  overcome  their learning problems. We introduce an approach to automatically  convert  modern  standard  Arabic  children's stories to the finest representative images that can efficiently illustrate the meaning of words. Specifically, first, we apply natural language processing techniques to analyze the text in stories and we extract keywords of all characters and  events  in  each  sentence.  Second,  we  apply  an  image captioning  process  through  a  pre-trained  deep  learning model for all retrieved images from our multimedia database as well as the Google search engine. Third, using sentence  similarities,  most  significant  images  are  retrieved back by selecting top highest similarity values. The proposed  system  aims  to  better  enhance  understanding, communications, and thinking skills for children with LD in elementary schools and special education centers.

Keywords-Learning;  Assistive  Technology;  Multimedia Content;  Knowledge  Extraction,  Semantic  Analysis,  Natural Language Processing.

## I. INTRODUCTION

Generally, children with LD have problems in several language skills [1,2], for instance they cannot distinguish properly among letters  and  words;  different  words;  forming  simple  proper sentence s . In addition, they may have difficulties in understanding  the  correct  meaning  of  simple  words  in  each context, forgetting names of objects, establishing logical relations  between  words.  These  difficulties  can  be  due  to different  physical  and  psychological  problems,  i.e.  intellectual disabilities, concentration deficit, difficulties in learning through conventional teaching methods, disorganization, limited self-reliance, and low self-esteem.

Children  with  LD  are  of  average  intelligence  and  they  need different teaching methodologies to help them understand. They can  continue  as  normal  students  if  the  appropriate  teaching methods are found to suit their effective needs. These children are  currently  integrated  in  normal  schools,  and  this  adds  key strains on these schools and its teachers. In fact, these children need  more  dedication  and  one-to-one  teaching  methodology, but  schools  cannot  recruit  new  teachers  to  follow  children individually. Indeed, Early intervention with appropriate teaching  and  assessment  methods  tailored  to  these  children's effective needs can decrease their delays in learning and break their isolation. The interactivity between teacher and student is important  for  learning  but  is  almost  absent  in  the  learning sessions  due  to  the  children's  lack  of  understanding  of  the concepts  being  taught,  and  to  the  teachers'  lack  of  media resources  in  remedial  teaching.  Multimedia  technology  can improve  language  learning  for  children  with  LD,  it  keeps  the children engaged for a longer time and takes into consideration their different levels of difficulties to learn new concepts.

Recently,  Increasing  attention  has  been  drawn  in  the  HumanComputer Interaction (HCI) community towards the design and development  of  accessible  computer  applications  for  children with  LD.  Due  to  better  healthcare  and  education,  the  learning quality  of  for  such  children  has  been  obviously  improved especially that these children grow up and live in the community  while  surrounded  by  computer  applications  and software. Despite the potential benefit of the usage of assistive computer technology for children with LD and the potential that this  technology  may  provide  for  them,  there  is  a  substantial need of baseline information on a number of topics, including the basic computer skills of people with LD, the objectives for using  computers,  and  the  difficulties  they  experience  while using computers. Knowledge on these issues will help researchers and practitioners to form an accurate picture of this unique population, and make better use of computer technology.

In  this  paper,  we  present  an  adaptive  multimedia  learning system for assisting children with Arabic  LD to enhance their understanding, communications, and thinking skills. Our proposed  system  dynamically  generates  multimedia  contents which can be effectively used by instructors to provide children with LD the opportunity to learn with contents designed to suit their  intellectual  needs,  and  maximize  the  education  process through  reducing  the  administrative  and  teaching  overheads. This paper is organized as follows: section 2 introduces related works, section  3  summarizes  text-to-picture  systems  that  have been  developed,  section  4  describes  our  adaptive  multimedia contents development showing both Arabic text processing and its  knowledge  extraction,  section  5  shows  system  operation, section  6  presents  the  assessment  method,  while  section  7 concludes the paper.

## II. RELATED WORK

Researchers in special education are to some extent informed of the  potential  of  computer  technology  in  helping  children  with LD [3].  The  claim  is  that  computer  technology  can  help  such children    increase  confidence  and  motivation  through  creative activities. Computer technology also has other benefits, including  errorless  learning,  immediate  feedback,  self-paced learning,  and  independence  of  learning.  Though,  it  is  widely advised that the actual benefits of computer technology may be reduced or not  even  apparent  depending  on  the  quality  of  the software, examples are the contents of many software programs are  not  age  appropriate,  many  educational software are unable to  reach  educational  goals  and  are  used  as  a  tool  for  mere entertainment, and many applications do not promote independent  learning.  Thus,  It  is  important  to  examine  how assistive  technologies  could  help  children  with  LD  be  more independent, yet remain connected to others and in touch to get assistance when needed.

There have been many  efforts in developing multimedia learning systems in the literature. Cheng et al. [3] designed an online  learning  system  for  Arabic  language  with  ready-made content.  The  system  can  select  the  learning  material  for  every learner based on the knowledge that he provides at start. Erradi et al. proposed a simple game-like system called 'ArabicTutor' [4] to teach Arabic. The content shows an Arabic word with its synonyms in different contexts with images. Wastam et al. [5] have  developed  a  system  to  teach  children  stories  through flashcards. The instructor selects a story and asks then the child to  arrange  its  flashcards  in  a  logical  sequence.  Rosmani  and Abdul Wahab [6] proposed a simple prototype called 'i-IQRA' to teach children the Holy Qura'n. It helps them to pronounce the verses in the right manner. Cheng et al. [7] proposed Crome for  children  and  adults.  Every  lesson  is  followed  by  a  set  of exercises  to  assess  the  progress  of  the  learners.  Tabot  and Hamada [8] have proposed a web-based educational system to teach physics to students. Ping et al. [9] have built an educational  system  for  children  with  hearing  impairment  to teach them the Malay language. Wuang et al. [10] have built a multimedia courseware system that is based on learning theories.  All  these  systems  are  based  on  static  contents  and address  different  learning  objectives.  However,  none  of  the related work  that we  have seen so far could adaptively converting Arabic language text into an appropriate personalized multimedia contents that would significantly enhance the learning process for children with LD.

## III. TEXT-TO-PICTURE SYSTEMS

A Text-to-picture system is a system that automatically converts  natural  language  text  into  pictures  representing  the meaning of that text. The pictures can be static illustrations such as  images  or  dynamic  illustrations  such  as  animations.  In  this section, we investigated and analyzed a number of well-known text-to-picture systems and approaches. We show their constituent steps such as knowledge extraction, mapping, image layout, as well as their performances and limits.

## A. Story Picturing Engine

This  system  refers  to  the  process  of  illustrating  a  story  with suitable pictures [11]. The  system  is a pipeline of three processes namely: story processing and image selection, estimation  of  similarity,  and  reinforcement-based  rank.  The estimation  of  similarity  between  pairs  of  images  is  based  on their  visual  and  lexical  features.  The  images  are  ranked  based on  a  mutual  reinforcement  method  and  most  highly-ranked images  are  retrieved.  Figure  1  shows  an  output  for  a  story picturing engine.

Figure 1. Output from Story Picturing Engine for the text 'on walk through Paris', H = highest ranked, L = Lowest ranked



Despite the good  accuracy  and  performance  of the story picturing engine, it only retrieves one picture for a given story and  ignores  many  aspects  such  as  the  temporal  or  spatial relations.  More  advanced  language  processing  techniques  can be  incorporated  into  the  Story  Picturing  Engine  for  richer performance.

## B. Text-to-Picture Synthesis System

In  contrast  to  the  story  picturing  engine,  this  system  [12] associates a different picture to each extracted key phrase and presents the story as a sequence of related pictures (see Figure 3). It treats the text-to-picture conversion problem  as an optimization process but still inherits the drawbacks of text-topicture  systems  despite  its  performance  compared  to  Story Picturing Engine.

Figure 2. Output from Text-to-Picture Synthesis for the sentence 'The girl rides the bus to school in the morning'



## C. Word2Image

This  system  proposed  various  techniques  [13],  including  the correlation analysis, semantic and visual clustering in order to produce sets of images from Flickr. To make the representative image  more  easily  understandable,  a  large  version  of the original  sized  image  will  be  shown  when  the  user  places  the mouse  over  it,  and  the  top-4  keywords  will  be  displayed  to depict  its  content.  Figure  3  shows  an  example  output  for  the concept 'pyramid'.



## Discoveredtopics:

- "France-Paris-museum-louvre
- Africa-Egypt-cairo-desert
- history-architecture-gizaspohinx
- Mexico-yucatan-maya-temple

Figure 3. Output from Word2Image for the word 'Pyramid'

Although, Word2Image translate concepts into its visual counterpart  with  sets  of  high  quality,  precise,  diverse  and representative  images,  it  inherits  the  problems  related  to  input language such as restricting the input to single words only.

## D. Chat with Illustration

This is a visual assisted instant messaging scheme named Chat with  illustration  (CWI),  which  presents  users  visual  messages associated  with  textual  message  automatically  [14].  The  CWI scheme  consists of Image  database  construction,  Dialogue analysis,  Image  selection  and  ranking,  Visual  layout,  Visual dialogue  summarization.  Figure  4  shows  visualized  dialogue. Despite the accuracy and the intuitiveness of the visual layout, CWI relies on making excessive preparation of image resource. Furthermore,  only  few  unpicturable  concepts,  such  as  verbs, adjectives,  fixed phrases and interrogatives are considered and labeled to images manually which makes the chat directed more to concrete concepts only. Another disadvantage of this system is its limited language processing capabilities and grammatical and logical relations.

Figure 4. Output for the message "Good idea."



## E. WordsEye

WordsEye is a text-to-scene system that can convert input text into  representative  3D  static  scenes  automatically  [15].  The system consists of two main components, including a linguistic analyzer and a scene depicter. The geometric information of the objects  is  attached  to  3D  models,  and  once  the  layout  is completed, the static scene is rendered using OpenGL similar to the example shown in Figure 5.

Figure 5. Output for the text on the left side



Although WordsEye has achieved a good degree of success for describing  the  scenes,  it  is  still  not  interactive  and  does  not exploit the user's feedbacks. Moreover, WordsEye relies on its huge offline rule-base and data repositories containing different geometric shape which lacks an automatic annotation task.

## F. CONFUCIUS

This is a multi-modal text-to-animation conversion system that can generate animation from a single input sentence containing an  action  verb  and  synchronize  it  with  speech  [16].  It  is composed of several modules with different tasks to accomplish such as Knowledgebase, Language processor, Media allocator, Animation engine, Text-to-speech engine, Story narrator, Synchronizer. As seen in Figure 6, CONFUCIUS supports lip synchronization,  facial  expressions,  and  parallel  animation  of the upper and the lower body of human models.

Figure 6. Output animation of 'John put a cup on the table.'



Generally,  CONFUCIUS  is  not  interactive  in  a  sense  that  it does  not  let  the  user  modify  the  generated  animation.  In addition,  only  a  single  sentences  in  each  input  is  allowed  and only a restricted format of the input sentences (i.e., one action verb  per  sentence  and  only  simple  verbs  are  considered)  is permitted, hence the user is restricted in expressing the intended description.  Table  1  gives  an  overall  comparison  of  surveyed text-picture systems focusing on their common features.

Table 1. Comparison of text-to-picture systems

| System                       | Syntax Analysis (NLP)   | Semantic Analysis (NLU)            | Input Modality       | Output Modality      |
|------------------------------|-------------------------|------------------------------------|----------------------|----------------------|
| Story picturing engine       | Bag-of words            | Semantic association               | Paragrap h           | 2D Picture           |
| Text-to- picture synthesizer | POS tagging             | Association Semantic Role labeling | Typed Text           | 2D Picture Collage   |
| Word2Image                   | Bag-of words            | Semantic association               | Words being pictured | 2D Picture Collage   |
| Chat with Illustration       | Bag-of words            | Semantic association               | Typed Short text     | 2D Picture           |
| WordsEye                     | Statistical parsing     | Dependenc y Parsing                | Story                | 3D Pictures          |
| CONFUCIUS                    | Dependen cy parsing     | Lexical Visual Semantic            | 1 Sentence           | 3D Animati on, Audio |

## IV. SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE

We propose a new system to improve the learning process of the  Arabic  language  for  children  with  LD  and  for  non-native Arabic  learners.  Our  system  aim  is  to  generate  personalized multimedia contents dynamically through providing an automatic  mechanism  to  query  for  a  desired  topic  based  on semantic content analysis. The system will statistically learn the preferred customization per student and will generate automatically customized tutorials. The dynamic tutorials would become more time efficient due to the machine learning process of the automated system. Figure 7 shows a high level architecture  of  the  proposed  system.  It  consists  of  generating multimedia-based tutorials by mining Arabic text, and it uses a core multi-domain ontology that exploits existing Arabic natural language processing technologies including morphological analysis, logical rules and discretization.

Figure 7. Proposed System Architecture



The  first  input  is  the  educational  text  that  will  be  processed using  segmentation,  stemming,  and  analyzing.  We  parse  text with the Stanford Parser that aids to retrieve sentences containing pairs of keyword entities and represents them using a dependency graph. The objective is to identify the actors (i.e., lion,  elephant,  tiger),  the  objects  (i.e.,  water,  plant),  and  the actions  (i.e.,  attack,  live,  eat)  in  the  story  text.  Each  retrieved sentence  is  analyzed  to  extract  the  raw  relationships  between keywords entities. The extracted relationships and entities will be semantically mapped and validated according to the domain description captured in an ontology model. All raw relationship instances  are  sent  as  semantic  queries  in  order  to  retrieve  the available multimedia instances in our ontology (i.e., the knowledgebase).  Complimentary  queries  will  sent  also  to  the Google  image  database  through  Google  APIs  to  fetch  the corresponding  multimedia  elements.  Hence,  the  teachers  can then select the appropriate multimedia elements that are suitable to customize the tutorial based on the needs of each child in the classroom.

## A. Proposed Approach

In this section, we  describe thoroughly the approach  for mapping Arabic simple stories to images. This approach is split into  two  main  parts,  represented  in  Figure  1:  (1)  Story  text processing  and  image  retrieval  using  keyword-based  search, containing  the  steps  1,  2  and  3.  (2)  Image  ranking  using automatic captioning process and sentence similarity, containing the steps 4, 5, and 6.

Figure 8. Proposed Approach for text-image mapping



To achieve this functionality, we performed the following tasks:

1. Collecting Arabic simple stories in the domain of animals.
2. Processing Arabic  text using different NLP  tools  for Arabic.
3. Generating  SQL  queries  and  search  engine  queries  to retrieve images  and  creating a database to store the mapping  of  keywords  to  images  to  serve  as  preliminary image pool.
4. Captioning retrieved images using a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) pre-trained model.
5. Matching  semantic  aspects  by  retrieving  a  set  of  images with initial input text.
6. Validating  and  ranking  of  the  retrieved  images  using sentence similarity.

To perform those tasks, we used several open source tools such as MT tool [17], CNN/LSTM image captioning model [18], etc. In the following, we present in detail the different steps.

## B. Description of the Approach Steps

## 1) Keyword Extraction:

First,  story  text  is  split  into  sentences  and  some  preprocessing steps  are  made  such  as  segmentation,  stop-word  removal,  and part-of-speech  tagging,  etc.  Second,  we  select  relevant  single word  tokens  as  keywords  for  each  sentence  and  we  translate them to English using this MT translation tool [17], [19].  We organize the keywords in the order that they appear in the text to preserve their role within the text.

## 2) Query Formulation:

In  this  step,  we  formulate  keyword-based  queries  to  retrieve candidate images for them. A useful heuristic for finding better representative images for the characters and the events in search engines  is  to  concatenate  the  extracted  keywords  including verbs as a single query for each sentence. A standard method of multiple  queries  is  also  employed.  The  combination  of  single query and multiple queries is also employed.

## 3) Image Selection:

The retrieved images using the concatenation of keywords are downloaded,  saved  locally  and  thus  prepared  for  further  prefiltering and captioning process in the next step.

## 4) Image Captioning

The prepared set of images is ready for going through this step in  order  to  be  captioned  by  a  deep  learning  model.  Image captioning via deep convolutional  neural  network  (CNN), recurrent  neural  network  (RNN)  and  long  short-term  memory (LSTM)  have  witnessed  significant  improvements  in  recent years  [20].  Deep  CNNs  can  fully  represent  an  image  by embedding it into a fixed-length vector. Then, RNN, especially LSTM [21], decodes the fixed-length vector to a desired output sentence by iterating a recurrence relation [22]. We used a pretrained model as a fine-tuned checkpoint which has been trained over 3 million iterations using the MSCOCO dataset [23].

## 5) Sentence Similarity

The  sentence  similarity  is  requested  in  order  to  make  the matching  between  initial  keywords  and  captions.  We  apply sentence similarity after the Machine Translation (MT) process to guarantee basic textual information for the matching process. The  obtained  English  captions  are  then  MT  processed  into Arabic. The  resulting Arabic captions are  compared  with keywords to find out which images are kept for final representation. We use sentence similarity to estimate values for similarity.  At this early stage, is not clear yet  which similarity function best fit within our case. It is a hard task and most of the metrics  fail  in  identifying  the  similarity  between  all  variations of text as argued in [24] and [25]. For sentences similarity task, we adopt a standard approach to compare the similarity between sentence  pairs  by  computing  a  cosine  similarity  [26]  between two sentences. Besides, we employed semantic similarity using WordNet  published  in  these  open  resources  [27],  [28]  for English  sentences.  Only  images  with  similarity  greater  than  a user-defined threshold are considered for further selection.

## 6) Image Ranking

Based  on  obtained  similarity  values,  we  select  sentences  that have  higher  similarity  values  with  the  keywords.  Associated top-10 images are ranked and eventually shown to the user/teacher, whenever he clicks the option for that.

## 7) Image Evaluation

This step is based on manual evaluation for each relevant image where the user can give a rank from 1 to 5. The system uses this value as an initial score for the final score calculation.

## C. System Operation

We  briefly  describe  the  system  work  flow.  As  depicted  in Figure  9,  our  proposed  system  works  as  follows:  User  or teacher  inputs  a  story  text  in  Arabic  using  simple  sentence structure, here '/g997/g1019/g944/g976/g991/g909  /g751/g909/g941/g1005  /g942/g996/g1000/g991/g909  /g958/g987/g942/g1011''.  Then,  key  words  are extracted  using  main  text  processing  steps;  text  segmentation, pos-tagging, and stop-words removal. After that,  the  MT process to English is applied. Then, the user clicks on 'Multimedia search'' either local search or Google search. After that, retrieved images are displayed. The user can validate them to  be  stored  in  a  local  database,  in  case  they  are  not  yet validated.  Finally,  the  user  can  display  top-10  ranked  images and  use  them  to  explain  the  main  ideas  and  objective  of  the given each sentence in a story.

Figure 9. Systematic flowchart of the proposed system



## V. EXPERIMENTS AND EVALUATIONS

This section discusses the evaluation of main components of the proposed  system.  These  components  are  (i)  keyword  based image  search;  (ii)  sentence  similarities;  and  (iii)  the  image retrieval  and  ranking.  The  goal  of  the  evaluation  is  to  test whether the images generated by our proposed system are able to  accurately  convey  the  main  characters  of  the  stories.  We distinguish  here  four  different  cases:  AW,  AWC,  EW,  and EWC as indicated in Table 2.

| Table 2. Description of four cases used in evaluations   | Table 2. Description of four cases used in evaluations   |
|----------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|
| Cases                                                    | Descriptions                                             |
| AW                                                       | Arabic Words without Captions                            |
| AWC                                                      | Arabic Words with Captions                               |
| EW                                                       | English Words without Captions                           |
| EWC                                                      | English Words with Captions                              |

## A. Experimental Setup

In the experimental setup, we start by following settings:

- Input text data set, we input 30 short simple stories in the domain  of  animals  to  our  system.  In  the  future,  it  is planned to set up a corpus for Arabic children's stories.
- Database has been set to store images and keywords and their mappings. For each keyword, we store 10 images and set a manual ranking from 1 to 5. Once we rank an image, we  validate  it  to  be  shown  to  the  learner.  This  step  is necessary to preserve a safe learning environment for the learner. Image captions have been generated using a CNN pre-trained model [18]. Obtained captions are  available in

English only and been MT translated in Arabic. These data are also stored in the database.

- Search engine, we used Google image search [29] to  fetch additional  images.  It  is  worth  mentioning  that  we    use Google  image  search  in  our  prototype  for  illustrative purpose only. In the future, it is planned to set up a dataset of  appropriate  educational  images  for  better  learning  and understanding.
- Deep learning model, we use im2txt, which is a pre-trained model  for  tensorflow  published  on  github  [18].  It  is  a model developed by Google that takes an image as input and creates a caption for it. Basically, a deep CNN is used to encode images to a fixed-length vector representation. It is  followed  by  a  LSTM  network  which  in  turn  takes  the encoding and produces a caption.
- MT translation: Extracted keywords are MT processed to English using the QCRI  MT-tool  [18]. Furthermore, captions are MT processed to Arabic using the same tool.

## B. Evaluation Criteria

We define following evaluation metrics based on general notion of positive and negative human judgments in image retrieval:

- /g131 Relevance a metric of retrieved images that most represent a specific keyword/story.
- /g131 Precision [30] is a proportion of relevant retrieved images to all retrieved images.
- /g131 Top10 are relevant images having 10 highest scores.

The similarity score produced by these measures has a normalized  real-number  standing  within  the  unit  interval.  We have performed evaluation experiments using these metrics on a set of 30 stories to analyze user satisfaction with the output and the  impact  of  the  automatic  captioning  process  on  similarity measures and consequently on the ranking of top-10 images.

## C. Evaluation of Image Relevance

The relevance and the suitability of each image were obtained through expert user. User was provided with 30 stories together with  their  output  images  from  our  proposed  system.  He  was asked to judge if the images were a representation of the main subject  of  the  topic  and  provide  a  rating  on  a  scale  of  0 (completely  unsuitable)  to  1  (very  suitable).  The  obtained results show that the average precision of our system is 44% for the AW-version compared to 49% for the EW-version. The user satisfaction with top10 is 38% for the AW-version whereas it is equal to 52% for the EW-version. We adopt the precision as the evaluation  criterion  by  comparing  the  output  each  time  a  new story is entered.

## D. Evaluation of Image Captioning

We applied an automatic captioning process for top-10 retrieved  images  which  are  treated  as  a  preliminary  set  of images.  The  obtained  results  show  that  the  average  precision value  for  top-10  under  the  AWC-version  is  46%  compared  to 56%  for  the  EWC-version.  The  captioning  process  is  done automatically  and  saved  us  time  and  resources,  however,  the results  are  weak;  the  model  creates  meaningful  captions  for images with common objects. But, it fails when more abstract objects  or  similar  objects  in  shape  are  present.  This  could  be improved by considering different techniques for training.

## E. Evaluation of Translation Quality

For evaluating the quality of MT translation of image captions from  English  to  Arabic,  each  sentence  pair  is  judged  by  two humans  whether  the  sentence  pair  is  semantically  coherent  or not. The users gave a similarity score between 5 and 0, where 5 means a good translation and 0 means that there is no semantic similarity  between  the  pair  of  sentences.  The  obtained  results showed overall 15 % of the total sentence pairs are judged to be positive  examples  means  they  are  semantically  equivalent. Consequently,  the  value  indicates  relatively  poor  performance in  judging  similar  pairs.  Thus,  it  adversely  affects  the  overall accuracy. For the quality of translation, we remark that translations of image captions from English to Arabic have led to  low  sentence  semantic  similarity  values with  the input stories. These results are not encouraging to use MT translation tool for our future work.

## F. Evaluation of Text-Multimedia Mapping

We target at enhancing our current proposed system that is still under development and improvement. We are currently processing  sentences  with  simple  structure  where  stop  words are eliminated. Then, keywords are extracted and searched first in the local database and  their  corresponding  images  are presented.  Using  the  concatenation  of  all  keywords  produced often  more  relevant  for  delivering  the  meaning  of  the  whole story  and  consequently  has  led  to  better  performance  than separate  keywords.  Top10  images  are  also  an  option  to  save time in looking over all relevant images. Whenever  the retrieved images are not relevant, the user can address a query to Google Search. A validation step for new images is required in this case to keep a safe learning environment.

## VI. CONCLUSION

We  proposed  an  adaptive  multimedia  learning  system for assisting children with Arabic LD to enhance their understanding skills of Arabic vocabulary and sentences. In this paper,  we  introduced  an  approach  to  automatically  convert simple Arabic children's stories to the best representative images  from  Google  image  search  using  deep  learning  model for  image  captioning.  The  system  can  be  effectively  used  by instructors to provide children with LD the opportunity to learn with contents designed with multimedia which would maximize the learning process and reduce the administrative and teaching overheads. The system can be used in schools' settings as well as at home where parents can assist their children to review the materials they studied in schools.
